On the Complexity of the Matching Problem of Regular Expressions with Backreferences Thanks: This is the full version of the paper accepted to ICALP 2026.
Soh Kumabe, Yuya Uezato

Abstract
Regular Expression Denial of Service (ReDoS) is a well-known type of algorithmic complexity attack, where an adversary supplies maliciously crafted strings to a regular expression matching engine, aiming to exhaust computational resources of systems. Even quadratic-time behavior in matching engines has been exploited in successful attacks, as exemplified by major outages at Stack Overflow (2016) and Cloudflare (2019). These incidents motivate a fundamental question: Is it possible to construct matching engines that are provably efficient, running in linear or near-linear time in the length of the input string? For classical regular expressions (REGEX), Thompson’s construction yields a linear-time algorithm for fixed expressions. However, practical engines support powerful features such as backreferences, which allow capturing a substring and reusing it later. This feature strictly extends the expressive power of REGEX but unfortunately increases the risk of ReDoS attacks. This paper investigates the fine-grained complexity of the string matching problem for regular expressions with backreferences (REWBs). Specifically, we consider r r -use k k -REWBs, i.e., REWBs with k k variables such that, in any computation, the total number of backreference executions is at most r r . On the hardness side, we show that the string matching problem for k k -REWBs cannot be solved in O ⁡ ( n 2 ​ k − ϵ ) O(n^{2k-\epsilon}) time for any ϵ > 0 \epsilon>0 under the Strong Exponential Time Hypothesis (SETH), where n n is the length of the input string. We also prove that this problem is W[2] -hard when parameterized by the length of the REWB expression, strengthening the previous W[1] -hardness result. Moreover, we prove that this problem for 2 2 -use 2 2 -REWBs cannot be solved in n 1 + o ⁡ ( 1 ) n^{1+o(1)} time unless the triangle detection problem can be solved in that time. On the algorithmic side, we present an O ⁡ ( n ​ log 2 ​ n ) O(n\log^{2}n) -time algorithm for 1 1 -use REWBs. In particular, we focus on the ABCBD problem , which is the REWB matching problem for the form A ​ ( B ) x ​ C \ x ​ D A\,(B)_{x}\,C\,\backslash x\,D where A A , B B , C C , and D D are fixed REGEXes. We also show that every 1 1 -use REWB can be transformed into this canonical form. Our algorithm significantly improves upon the recent O ⁡ ( n 2 ) O(n^{2}) -time algorithm for the ABCBD problem by Nogami and Terauchi (MFCS, 2025). Our algorithm is highly non-trivial and employs several techniques including suffix trees, transition monoids of REGEXes, factorization forest data structures, and periodicity of strings.



1
Introduction

Background.

Regular expressions are widely used in web services to validate and filter user-provided text.
However, depending on the engine implementation and the specific expressions used,
the worst-case regular expression matching time can be prohibitive.
In most web-service settings, expressions are fixed by the application,
while the input string is provided by potentially untrusted users.
In such cases,
an adversary can craft inputs that trigger the worst-case behavior, potentially exhausting computational resources (CPU and memory) and causing service outages.

Attacks that exploit such worst-case algorithmic behavior are known as

algorithmic complexity attacks

[
30
]
.
Those specifically involving regular expressions are called
ReDoS
(Regular Expression Denial of Service)
[
31
]
.

R
eDoS is a major vulnerability and is cataloged in the Common Weakness Enumeration (CWE, the de facto standard for vulnerability classification) as CWE-1333
[
75
]
.

We highlight two well-documented service outages caused by
R
eDoS,
together with the real-world regular-expression that triggered the
worst-case behavior in the deployed engines.
1
1

1

These two examples are written in the syntax of the corresponding real-world
regex engines, not in the formal syntax introduced below.
For example,
\s
is a whitespace character class and

\u200c
is the Unicode escape
for U+200C.
These examples are used only to demonstrate that backtracking engines can already cause quadratic-time
R
eDoS without backreferences.

1.

The 2016 Stack Overflow outage, caused by the expression
^[\s\u200c]+|[\s\u200c]+$

[
101
]
.

2.

The 2019 Cloudflare outage, caused by an expression whose core part is
.*.*=.*

[
57
]
.

According to their reports
[
101
,
57
]
,
these outages occurred because the employed regular expression engines relied on
backtracking
algorithms.
Consequently, the above expressions exhibited quadratic-time computation on crafted inputs.
2
2

2

Readers can reproduce the quadratic-time behavior of these expressions using the online demos in
[
9
,
8
]
.

It is well known that backtracking-based engines can cause catastrophic
exponential
backtracking
[
48
,
25
,
53
]
,
and it is widely believed that avoiding exponential behavior would be sufficient.
However, these incidents were surprising in showing that even quadratic-time behavior can lead to a system outage. This is stated explicitly in the post-mortem
[
101
]
, which we quote verbatim below:

So the Regex engine has to perform a “character belongs to a certain character class” check (plus some additional things) 20,000+19,999+19,998+…+3+2+1 = 199,990,000 [sic] times, and that takes a while. This is not classic catastrophic backtracking (
performance is
O(n^{2})
, not exponential, in length
), but it was enough.

Motivated by this background, we propose the following criterion:

A regular expression engine is ReDoS-
safe
if, for each fixed expression, it runs in linear or near-linear time in the length of the input string.

In this paper, we address the following fundamental questions:
Can we construct a
R
eDoS-safe regular expression engine in the above sense?
If so, which practical extensions can such an engine still support?

Complexity of
R
egex matching problems.

The term “regular expression” is used for several different formalisms.
In this paper, following classical formal language theory,
we use the standard (Kleene) regular expressions, denoted by
R
egex.

Definition 1

(Syntax of
R
egex)
.

The syntax of
R
egex over an alphabet
\Sigma
is given by the grammar:

E::=\emptyset\mid\epsilon\mid\sigma\mid E+E\mid E\cdot E\mid E^{*}\qquad\text{where $\sigma\in\Sigma$}.

We give the formal semantics, including the language
\mathbb{L}\left(\cdot\right)
, in Section
2
.
For
R
egex, we consider the following computational problem.

†
†
itembox-title:
R
egex Matching Problem
Fixed Object
: A
R
egex
E
.

Input
: A string
w
.

Task
: Deciding if
w\in\mathbb{L}\left(E\right)
where
\mathbb{L}\left(E\right)
is the language of
E
.

Is it possible to design a
R
eDoS-safe engine for the
R
egex matching problem?
The answer is
Yes
.
Using Thompson’s construction
[
103
]

and the standard efficient NFA simulation
[
25
]
[
3
, Sec 3.7]
,
we can solve this problem in
O(|E|\,|w|)
time,
which is linear in
|w|
for each fixed
E
.

From
R
egex to
R
EWB.

Does the linear-time algorithm for
R
egex matching fully resolve
R
eDoS
in practice
?
The answer is
No
.
This is because most real-world regular expression engines support extensions beyond
R
egex.
One of the most prominent extensions is backreferences.
We use
R
EWB to denote the class of
regular expressions with backreferences
.
Unlike
R
egex,
R
EWB allows
variables
that capture substrings matched by a subexpression and refer back to that captured string later.
Backreferences are widely supported in the standard regular expression libraries of major programming languages (e.g., C
++
, Java, JavaScript, Python, Ruby, Perl, PHP, and OCaml).
The syntax of
R
EWB is given as follows.

Definition 2

(Syntax of
R
EWB)
.

The syntax of
R
EWB is given by the following grammar:

E::=\emptyset\mid\epsilon\mid\sigma\mid E+E\mid E\cdot E\mid E^{*}\mid(E)_{x}\mid\mathord{\backslash x},

where
\sigma\in\Sigma
and
x
is a variable.
The expression
(E)_{x}
captures the substring matched by
E
and stores it in
x
, while
\mathord{\backslash x}
refers to the content stored in
x
.

As with
R
egex, we give the formal semantics of
R
EWB in Section
2
.

We note that
R
EWB is strictly more expressive than
R
egex.
Consider the expression
E:=((0+1)^{*})_{x}\>\mathord{\backslash x}
.
Intuitively, the subexpression
((0+1)^{*})_{x}
matches an arbitrary string
w\in\{0,1\}^{*}
and binds it to
x
.
Then,
\mathord{\backslash x}
matches the captured substring stored in
x
.
Hence,
E
defines the non-regular language
\{ww:w\in\{0,1\}^{*}\}
.

This paper focuses on the following problem.

†
†
itembox-title:
R
EWB Matching Problem
Fixed Object
: A
R
EWB
E
.

Input
: A string
w
.

Task
: Deciding if
w\in\mathbb{L}\left(E\right)
.

This raises a natural question:
Is it possible to design a
R
eDoS-safe engine for the
R
EWB matching problem?
Unfortunately,
No
.
If we view
E
as part of the input and parameterize the problem by
c
, the number of variable occurrences in
E
,
then the
R
EWB matching problem is
W[1]
-hard
[
102
,
42
]
.
In particular, under the standard assumption
\mathbf{FPT}\neq\textbf{W[1]}
,
there is no uniform algorithm running in
f(c)\cdot|w|^{O(1)}
for all inputs
(w,E)
.
However, this worst-case hardness is too pessimistic for practice.
In practice, many real-world
R
EWB expressions use backreferences in restricted ways.
To identify which
R
EWB subclasses are
R
eDoS-safe and which remain vulnerable,
we need a fine-grained analysis of the
R
EWB matching problem.

Classification of
R
EWB.

We write
k
-
R
EWB to denote the set of
R
EWBs that have at most
k
distinct variables.

We say that an expression
E
is
r
-use if, for every input string
w
and
for all computations of
E
on
w
, the total number of executed backreferences
(i.e., executed occurrences of
\mathord{\backslash x}
) is at most
r
.
Intuitively, a computation is one possible run of
E
on
w
;
different runs may arise from different choices at the union and Kleene-star
subexpressions of
E
, and may therefore execute different numbers of backreferences.

If no such finite
r
exists, we call
E

\omega
-use
.
We illustrate these notions with the following examples:

•

(\Sigma^{*})_{x}\mathord{\backslash x}
is a
1
-use
1
-
R
EWB because there is only one variable
x
, which is referenced once.

•

(\Sigma^{*})_{x}\>\mathord{\backslash x}\>\mathord{\backslash x}
is
a
2
-use
1
-
R
EWB because there is only one variable
x
, which is referenced twice.

•

(\Sigma^{*})_{x}\>\mathord{\backslash x}\>(\Sigma^{*})_{x}\>\mathord{\backslash x}
is
also a
2
-use
1
-
R
EWB that reuses the single variable
x
.

•

(\Sigma^{*})_{x}\>(\mathord{\backslash x})^{*}
is
an
\omega
-use

1
-
R
EWB, as
x
can be referenced an unbounded number of times.

•

(\Sigma^{*})_{x}\ (\Sigma^{*})_{y}\mathord{\backslash y}\mathord{\backslash x}
is
a
2
-use
2
-
R
EWB because it contains two variables
x
and
y
, which are referenced a total of two times.

We allow both capture groups and backreferences to appear inside capture groups.
That is, the body of a capture group
(\cdots)_{x}
may contain subexpressions
of the form
\mathord{\backslash y}
or
(\cdots)_{y}
, where
y
may be equal to
x
.

For example,
(\mathord{\backslash x}\,\mathord{\backslash x})_{x}
(“doubling the content of
x
”) and
(0\,\mathord{\backslash x}\>0)_{x}
(“wrapping
x
with
0
s”) are well-formed.

Known algorithmic results for
R
EWB matching.

The current best-known algorithm for
k
-
R
EWB matching runs in
O(|E|\cdot|w|^{1+2k})
time
[
35
, §IX. C]
[
88
]
based on standard dynamic programming.
Recently, Nogami and Terauchi studied the
ABCBD problem
, a subclass of the
1
-
R
EWB matching problem, and gave an
O(|E|^{2}|w|^{2})
-time algorithm
[
88
, Thm. 5]
.
Although their result improved upon the standard DP-based
O(|E|\,|w|^{3})
-time algorithm,
it is not
R
eDoS-safe due to its quadratic dependence on
|w|
.

1.1
Our Contribution

We summarize our main hardness and algorithmic results.

1.1.1
Hardness Results for the
R
EWB Matching Problem

We strengthen the previously known
W[1]
-hardness results
[
102
,
42
]
by showing
W[2]
-hardness.

Theorem 3
.

When the expression
E
is part of the input,
the
R
EWB matching problem is
\mathbf{W[2]}
-hard parameterized by the expression size
|E|
.

Assuming that Triangle Detection on an
m
-edge graph does not admit an
m^{1+\delta-o(1)}
-time algorithm
[
2
, Conjecture 3]
,
we derive a conditional lower bound even for the highly restricted class,
2
-use
2-
R
EWB.

Theorem 4
.

Assuming the no-almost-linear-time hypothesis for Triangle Detection,
there exists a
fixed

2
-use
2
-
R
EWB expression
\Psi
such that the matching problem for
\Psi
cannot be solved in
|w|^{1+\delta-o(1)}
time for some constant
\delta>0
.

Under the
k
-Orthogonal Vectors hypothesis
(
k
-OV
)
[
107
]
, which is a weaker assumption than the
strong exponential time hypothesis
(
SETH
), we have the following result.

Theorem 5
.

Assuming the
k
-OV
hypothesis,
for each fixed integer
k\geq 1
,
there exists a fixed
\omega
-use
k
-
R
EWB expression
\Psi_{k}
such that
the matching problem for
\Psi_{k}
cannot be solved in
O(|w|^{2k-\epsilon})
time for any
\epsilon>0
.

Very recently and independently of our work,
Nogami and Terauchi
[
89
, Theorem 1]
proved the following lower bound:
assuming the
2
-
OV
hypothesis, no algorithm solves the
1
-
R
EWB matching problem in
O(|w|^{2-\epsilon}\,\text{poly}(|E|))
time.
Our result strengthens theirs in the following two ways:
(1) our hardness holds for general
k
; and
(2) as our hardness holds for a fixed expression
\Psi_{k}
, it rules out any speedup gained by allowing arbitrary dependence on
|E|
.

1.1.2
Algorithmic Results

We have shown that the
R
EWB matching problem remains hard even for
2
-use
2
-
R
EWBs (Theorem
4
)
and for (
\omega
-use)
1
-
R
EWBs (Theorem
5
with
k=1
)
when seeking a near-linear-time algorithm.
This naturally raises the question: What is the complexity of
1
-use
1
-
R
EWB matching?
Our main algorithmic result is a near-linear time algorithm for the following special case:

†
†
itembox-title:
ABCBD Problem
Fixed Objects
:
R
egexes
A
,
B
,
C
, and
D
.

Input
: A string
w
.

Task
: Deciding if we can decompose
w
into
w=w_{A}\,w_{B}\,w_{C}\,w_{B}\,w_{D}
so that
w_{A}\in\mathbb{L}\left(A\right)
,
w_{B}\in\mathbb{L}\left(B\right)
,
w_{C}\in\mathbb{L}\left(C\right)
, and
w_{D}\in\mathbb{L}\left(D\right)
.
Equivalently, decide whether
w\in\mathbb{L}\left(A\,(B)_{x}\,C\,\mathord{\backslash x}\,D\right)
.

At first glance, this syntax may appear highly restricted.
However, the ABCBD problem captures the general case of
1
-use
R
EWB in the following sense:

Theorem 6
.

For any 1-use
R
EWB
E
,
there exists an equivalent
R
EWB of the following form:

(A_{1}\,(B_{1})_{x}\,C_{1}\,\mathord{\backslash x}\,D_{1})+(A_{2}\,(B_{2})_{x}\,C_{2}\,\mathord{\backslash x}\,D_{2})+\cdots+(A_{K}\,(B_{K})_{x}\,C_{K}\mathord{\backslash x}\,D_{K})+E^{\prime}

where
A_{i}
,
B_{i}
,
C_{i}
,
D_{i}
,
E^{\prime}
are
R
egexes.
We can construct the above representation in
O(|E|^{4})
time.
Furthermore, the following holds for their size:

K=O(|E|^{2}),\ |A_{i}|=O(|E|^{2}),\ |B_{i}|=O(|E|),\ |C_{i}|=O(|E|^{2}),\ |D_{i}|=O(|E|),\ |E^{\prime}|=O(|E|^{2}).

The main result of this paper is as follows.

Theorem 7
.

The ABCBD problem admits an
O(|w|\log^{2}|w|)
-time algorithm. The hidden constant is
2^{O((|A|+|B|+|C|+|D|)^{2})}
.

This significantly improves the
O(|w|^{2})
-time algorithm of Nogami and Terauchi
[
88
]
.
Our algorithm is highly nontrivial; for a more detailed overview, see Section
1.3
.
Note that our algorithm can also be viewed as a
near-linear-time fixed-parameter
algorithm for the ABCBD matching problem, parameterized by the (combined) size of
R
egexes
A
,
B
,
C
, and
D
.
Combining Theorems
6
and
7
, we obtain the following.

Theorem 8
.

The
1
-use
R
EWB matching problem admits an
O(|w|\log^{2}|w|)
-time algorithm. The hidden constant is
2^{O(\,|E|^{4}\,)}
.

Note that the hidden constant
2^{O(\,|E|^{4}\,)}
is larger than in Theorem
7
because Theorem
6
may yield expressions of quadratic length.

Remark: Uniformity

The algorithms in Theorems
7
and
8

are uniform.
Namely, they take the relevant expressions
(
A,B,C,D
in Theorem
7
,
and
E
in Theorem
8
)
together with the input string
w
,
perform all preprocessing depending on these expressions, and then run the main procedures.
The fixed-object formulation only emphasizes the dependence on
|w|
;
no non-uniform advice depending on the fixed expressions is assumed.

As a key subroutine for solving the ABCBD problem,
we have also developed an efficient algorithm for the following problem.

†
†
itembox-title:
XY^{\alpha}Z
Problem
Fixed Objects
: An integer
\alpha\geq 2
and
R
egexes
X
,
Y
, and
Z
.

Input
: A string
w
.

Task
: Deciding if we can decompose
w
into
w=w_{X}\,\smash{\overbrace{w_{Y}\,\cdots\,w_{Y}}^{\alpha\text{ times}}}\,w_{Z}
so that
w_{X}\in\mathbb{L}\left(X\right)
,
w_{Y}\in\mathbb{L}\left(Y\right)
, and
w_{Z}\in\mathbb{L}\left(Z\right)
.
Equivalently, decide whether
w\in\mathbb{L}\left(X\,(Y)_{x}\,(\mathord{\backslash x})^{\alpha-1}\,Z\right)
.

Our theorem for this problem is the following.

Theorem 9
.

The
XY^{\alpha}Z
problem admits an
O(|w|\log|w|)
-time algorithm.
The hidden constant is
2^{O(s^{2})}+\alpha\cdot 2^{O(s)}
, where
s:=|X|+|Y|+|Z|
.
Alternatively, at the cost of an additional
\log|w|
factor (i.e., time
O(|w|\log^{2}|w|)
),
we can achieve a polynomial dependence on
s+\alpha
.

1.1.3
Open Problems

Several problems remain to be investigated.
The first direction is to specify classes of
R
EWB that admit near-linear-time algorithms.
We have shown that
1
-use
R
EWBs admit a near-linear-time algorithm, whereas
2
-use
2
-
R
EWBs and
\omega
-use
1
-
R
EWBs are unlikely to admit such algorithms.
However, the matching complexity of
r
-use
1
-
R
EWB for any constant
r\geq 2
remains open.

The second direction is to improve our algorithm for the
ABCBD
problem.
The most direct question is whether we can eliminate the remaining logarithmic factor and obtain a truly linear-time algorithm: reducing
O(|w|\log^{2}|w|)
to
O(|w|)
.
Moreover, when
R
EWB
E
is given as input,
our algorithm has an exponential dependency on the expression length
|E|
.
Specifically, it is open whether it is possible to reduce the dependence on
|E|
to polynomial while preserving near-linear dependence on
|w|
.
Note that, if we allow quadratic dependence on
|w|
, Nogami and Terauchi
[
88
]
already achieves
O(|E|^{2}|w|^{2})
-time.
Furthermore, as we describe in Section
1.3
, our algorithm consists of many building blocks and is therefore quite complex.
Simplifying it is a meaningful task both for advancing theoretical understanding and for improving practical implementability.

The third direction is to broaden the scope.
Real-world regular expression engines support many features beyond backreferences, such as lookaround
[
48
,
55
]
,
greedy and lazy Kleene stars
[
48
,
56
]
,
and atomic grouping
[
48
,
54
]
.
Therefore, investigating the complexity of matching regular expressions augmented with such features is important in practice.
We believe that these problems are also theoretically interesting, in the same way that the
R
EWB matching problem admits deep algorithmic study, as demonstrated in this paper.

1.2
Related Work

1.2.1
R
eDoS

Regular Expression Denial of Service (
R
eDoS) has been recognized as an important instance of
algorithmic complexity attacks

[
30
]
since at least 2003
[
31
]
.
Outages at Stack Overflow (2016)
[
101
]
and Cloudflare (2019)
[
57
]
are representative examples, and notably, both incidents were triggered by quadratic behavior of regular expression engines, highlighting that even
polynomial
complexity can result in severe real-world failures.

R
eDoS is a common type of
D
oS attack that is officially cataloged as CWE-1333: Inefficient Regular Expression Complexity
[
75
]
.
Vulnerabilities continue to be discovered and reported in practice;
high-risk examples include
[
79
,
80
,
81
,
82
,
83
]
from 2021 to 2024.
In 2025, notable entries in the CVE (Common Vulnerabilities and Exposures, which maintains a public record of disclosed security issues) include
[
84
,
85
,
86
]
.

At its core,
R
eDoS arises from backtracking-based regex engines, which may exhibit catastrophic (possibly exponential) backtracking
[
53
]
.
This behavior has long been known to automata theorists, and influential books
[
48
]
helped disseminate the problem more widely to practitioners.
For classical
R
egex matching, Cox
[
25
,
26
]
recommended that practitioners prefer Thompson NFA-based implementations over backtracking-based engines, as the former run in
O(|E|\,|w|)
time.
In contrast, it is unlikely that
R
EWB matching admits such a “silver bullet” solution like the Thompson construction, as this problem is
NP
-hard in general
[
4
,
12
]
.
This has motivated complexity-theoretic research into the
R
EWB matching problem, including the identification of tractable classes
[
47
,
88
,
96
]
.

As an example of
R
eDoS involving expressions with backreferences, we focus on
CVE-2017-16114

[
77
]
and
CVE-2019-25103

[
78
]
.
Both cases are triggered by an expression that can be abstracted as
E:=(a^{*})_{x}\ b^{*}\,((a+b)^{*}\,b)\,b^{*}\ \mathord{\backslash x}
, on which backtracking-based engines take cubic time in the worst case.
In
CVE-2019-25103
, the corresponding patch
[
19
]

rewrote
E
to
E_{1}:=(a^{*})_{x}\ ((a+b)^{*}\,b)\ \mathord{\backslash x}
; it modified only the “
R
egex-part” without touching the “backreference-part”.
Consequently,
although
the
R
eDoS vulnerability is mitigated,
potential vulnerabilities remain, as even the patched version exhibits quadratic time complexity; this issue seems to be an inevitable overhead of backtracking-based engines, as they try all possible prefixes that the variable
x
captures.
In
CVE-2017-16114
, the corresponding patch
[
105
]

rewrote
E
to
E_{2}:=(a^{*})_{x}\,\bigl(b+(b(a+b)b)\bigr)^{*}\,\mathord{\backslash x}
.
Although
E_{2}
admits linear-time matching, it alters the accepting language: for example,
E
and
E_{1}
accept
a\,ab\,a
but
E_{2}
does not.
In contrast, the original expression belongs to our
ABCBD
problem; thus, it can be evaluated in almost linear time by our algorithm without modifying the semantics.

1.2.2
Hardness of Regular Language Matching Problems

On
R
egex.

While Thompson’s construction
[
103
]
solves
R
egex matching in
O(\,|E||w|\,)
time,
Backurs and Indyk
[
10
, Thm.3]
proved that
O(\,(|E||w|)^{1-\epsilon}\,)
time is unachievable under
S
ETH.

Recently, Bringmann et al. introduced the NFA Acceptance Hypothesis
[
18
]
:
intuitively, for a dense NFA
A
,
the membership problem
w\in\mathbb{L}\left(A\right)
cannot be solved in
O(\,(|A||w|)^{1-\epsilon}\,)
time for any
\epsilon>0
.
Bille and Gørtz
[
14
]
introduced a parameter called
density

\Delta
(the total number of active states during the simulation on the input
w
)
and proved that matching for sparse NFAs (or
R
egexes) also cannot be solved in
O(\Delta^{1-\epsilon})
time under
S
ETH.
These results suggest that improving upon the textbook
O(|E||w|)
-time algorithm for
R
egex matching is unlikely at present.

On
R
EWB.

The
R
EWB matching problem is known to be
NP
-complete
[
4
, Thm 6.2]

when the
R
EWB expression is a part of the input, even over a unary alphabet
[
12
]
.
Moreover, when parameterized by the number of variables
k
, the
k
-
R
EWB matching problem is
W[1]
-hard
[
42
, Thm. 1]
.
In fact,
W[1]
-hardness already holds for a more restricted fragment of
R
EWB called
pattern languages

[
42
, Thm. 1]
[
102
, Thm. 1]
.
We briefly recall pattern languages below to relate them to
R
EWB and summarize known algorithmic results.

1.2.3
Algorithms for Regular Expression Matching Problems

On
R
EWB.

The current best-known algorithm for the
k
-
R
EWB matching problem is based on dynamic programming and runs in
O(|E|\cdot|w|^{1+2k})
time
[
35
, §IX. C]
.
Applying this algorithm to the ABCBD problem yields an
O(|E|\cdot|w|^{3})
-time algorithm.
Nogami and Terauchi gave an
O(|E|^{2}|w|^{2})
-time algorithm, and thereby improved the dependence on
|w|
from cubic to quadratic.
As noted earlier, our Theorem
7
further improves the dependence on
|w|
to near-linear.

Freydenberger and Schmid
[
47
]
considered the matching problem for the class of
deterministic

R
EWBs, which is a subclass of
R
EWBs that can deterministically match strings when reading from the left. They gave an
O(k|w|)
-time algorithm for
k
-
R
EWB.
Schmid
[
96
]
extended this result to the wider class of
memory-deterministic

R
EWBs and gave an
O(|E|^{c}|w|)
-time algorithm for some constant
c
.
They also refined the time complexity of the
R
EWB matching problem to
O(|E|\cdot|w|^{O(\alpha)})
for the cases of small
\alpha
, where
\alpha\,(\leq k)
denotes the
active variable degree
, which represents the maximum number of variables that are “active” at the same time.

Pattern Language and Word Equation.

A
pattern language
is a class of languages generated by
patterns
, that is, strings that may also contain variables that can be matched to strings.
For example, the pattern
P:=01X10X
over
\Sigma=\{0,1\}
with a variable
X
generates the language
\{\,01w10w:w\in\Sigma^{*}\}
.
This concept was introduced by Angluin in the late 1970s in the context of learning theory and has since been studied extensively
[
6
,
7
,
74
,
99
]
.

Pattern languages can be seen as a very restricted subclass of
R
EWB. Specifically, a pattern language can be expressed as a
R
EWB that:
(1) uses a capture group
(\ldots)_{x}
for the first occurrence of each variable
x
,
(2) uses a backreference
\mathord{\backslash x}
for all subsequent occurrences, and
(3) only allows concatenation as the outermost operator (no union or Kleene star over captures/backreferences).
For example, the above pattern
P
equals the
R
EWB
01(\Sigma^{*})_{X}10\mathord{\backslash X}
.

The matching problem of pattern languages is NP-complete
[
6
,
7
,
39
,
67
]
even for the case with alphabet size
2
, each variable occurs at most twice, and each variable can be replaced with a string of length in
\{0,1\}
or
\{1,2,3\}

[
43
]
.
Ibarra et al. gave an
O\left(\frac{(|P|+|w|)|w|^{k-1}}{(k-1)!}\right)
-time algorithm for the membership problem of
k
-variable pattern languages, where
P
is the given pattern expression
[
63
]
.
In particular, it runs in linear time when
k=1
.
Fernau et al.
[
40
]
gave algorithms for several classes of pattern languages with good properties. Particularly, they showed that for the case of
non-crossing
(i.e., scopes of different variables do not overlap) pattern languages, the matching problem admits an
O(m|w|\log|w|)
-time algorithm, where
m
is the number of one-variable blocks occurring in a given pattern
[
40
, Thm 8]
[
41
, Thm 4.16]
.
Observe that our
XY^{\alpha}Z
problem generalizes the case
m=1
by allowing arbitrary
R
egexes for
X
,
Y
, and
Z
.

Given two pattern languages (possibly sharing variables), the
word equation
problem asks whether there is a string that matches both of them.
The case that one of the languages contains no variables is equal to the pattern language matching problem.
In general, the word equation problem is known to be in PSPACE
[
94
]
.
For the
1
-variable case,
Dąbrowski and Plandowski
[
33
]
gave an
O(m\>+\>\#_{X}\log m)
-time algorithm where
m
is the total size of given patterns and
\#_{X}
is the total number of variable occurrences.
Later, in the RAM-model setting,
Jeż
[
64
]
gave an
O(m)
-time algorithm using the
recompression
technique introduced by Jeż
[
65
]
.
Dąbrowski and Plandowski
[
34
]
gave an
O(m^{5})
-time algorithm for the
2
-variable case.
In contrast, the problem becomes significantly harder for
k\geq 3
variables; even for the
3
-variable case, it is not even known whether the problem is in NP
[
66
]
.
Schulz
[
97
]
considered a variant of the word equation problem that each variable has a
R
egex constraint.
They proved this variant is decidable;
it was later proved to be PSPACE-complete by Diekert et al.
[
36
]
.

On
R
egex with Lookaround.

Lookaround
is one of the features that real-world regular expression engines support, which checks whether a specified pattern exists around the current position, without consuming characters.
It is folklore that the language class of
R
egex equipped with lookaround is regular
3
3

3

We can convert it to a
two-way alternating finite automaton
(2AFA)
[
20
]
, whose language class is regular
[
71
]
.
; thus, it admits
O(|w|)
time matching by Thompson’s construction when we only consider the dependence on
|w|
.
Therefore, research concerns the dependence on the expression length
|E|
, and particularly, focuses on
O(|E|\cdot|w|)
time algorithms.

Mamouras and Chattopadhyay
[
73
]
provided an
O(|E|\cdot|w|)
time membership algorithm.
Chattopadhyay et al.
[
21
]
mechanized an algorithm of the same complexity based on
[
73
]
and verified its correctness using the proof assistant
Rocq
(formerly known as Coq).
Barrière and Pit-Claudel
[
11
]
provided a matching algorithm of the same complexity that is faithful to JavaScript
R
egex matching semantics.
Fujinami and Hasuo
[
49
]
provided a matching algorithm of the same complexity, which also supports
atomic
grouping, which is a practical extension that does not enlarge the language expressiveness
[
48
,
54
]
.
Uezato
[
104
]
proved that the membership problem of
R
EWB equipped with
lookahead
(lookaround that only examines after the current position) is PSPACE-complete.

Gapped Patterns.

Another well-studied regular-language formalism, motivated by practical pattern matching applications (rather than
R
EWB),
is that of
gapped patterns
.
A typical gapped pattern
G
has the form

G\equiv p_{0}\,\Sigma^{[\alpha_{1},\,\beta_{1}]}\,p_{1}\Sigma^{[\alpha_{2},\,\beta_{2}]}\,\cdots\,\Sigma^{[\alpha_{m},\,\beta_{m}]}\,p_{m},

where each
p_{i}\in\Sigma^{*}
is a fixed string and

\Sigma^{[\alpha,\beta]}:=\{w\in\Sigma^{*}:\alpha\leq|w|\leq\beta\}
is the set of substring whose length lies between
\alpha
and
\beta
.
Thus every gapped pattern denotes a regular language and can be expressed as a
classical
R
egex.
Matching such patterns, including dictionary variants in which a
finite set of gapped patterns is matched against a text, has been studied
extensively; see, e.g.,

[
87
,
46
,
13
,
59
,
62
]

and the survey
[
72
]
.
Particularly relevant from a fine-grained perspective is the work of Amir et al.
[
5
]
on dictionary matching with one gap.
As the uniformly bounded case,
a dictionary
\mathcal{D}=\{G_{1},G_{2},\ldots,G_{n}\}
consist of patterns
G_{i}
forms
p^{i}_{1}\,\Sigma^{[\alpha,\,\beta]}\,p^{i}_{2}
with common bounds
\alpha,\beta
.
The task is to report their occurrences for a input text
w
.
Under the 3SUM conjecture
[
51
,
91
,
68
]
,
for suitable dictionaries,
they proved a lower bound of

\Omega(|w|(\beta-\alpha)^{1-o(1)}+\mathit{op})

where
\mathit{op}
is the output size
[
5
, Thm. 5 and Table 2]
.
Complementing this, they also gave an online reporting algorithm whose running time depends on structural parameters of dictionaries,
in particular the degeneracy of the associated bipartite graph
[
5
, Thm. 9]
.

1.3
Technical Overview of Theorem
7

We provide an overview of our algorithm for Theorem
7
,
which finds a decomposition
w=w_{A}\,w_{B}\,w_{C}\,w_{B}\,w_{D}
with
w_{\xi}\in\mathbb{L}\left(\xi\right)
for
\xi\in\{A,B,C,D\}
.
The following figure summarizes the overall structure.

{forest}

To streamline our algorithm,
we first normalize the input
w
and expressions so that
\epsilon\not\in\mathbb{L}\left(D\right)
and the alphabet is binary
\Sigma=\{0,1\}
in Section
4
.
Then, we can assume
w_{D}\neq\epsilon
.
In Section
5
, we reduce the ABCBD problem to two subproblems: the
XYYZ problem
(Section
6
) and the
branching ABCBD problem
(Sections
7
–
12
).
In the former problem, we consider the case
w_{C}=\epsilon
, i.e.,
w=w_{A}\,w_{B}\ w_{B}\,w_{D}
.
In the latter problem, we consider the case where
two suffixes
w_{C}\,w_{B}\,w_{D}
and
w_{D}
satisfy a
branching condition
; that is, their first letters are distinct.
To show that this decomposition covers all cases,
we rely on the closure properties of
R
egex and the equivalence with
nondeterministic finite automata
(NFAs), as detailed in Section
5.2
.

The key structure of the former (XYYZ) problem (our
X\,Y^{\alpha}\,Z
problem with
\alpha=2
, Section
6
) is that, since
w_{C}=\epsilon
, two occurrences of
w_{B}
appear consecutively in
w
as
w=\ldots w_{B}\,w_{B}\ldots
.
We crucially exploit the periodicity of
w
induced by this immediate repetition
w_{B}w_{B}
.
We use Crochemore’s maximal local power enumeration
[
28
, Sec. 9.2]
[
27
]
(Section
6.1
), in combination with the periodic properties of
R
egex (Section
6.3
).
This part also yields Theorem
9
.

To solve the latter (branching ABCBD) problem (Sections
7
–
12
), we work with the
suffix tree
of
w
,
\mathsf{ST}_{w}
, or simply
\mathsf{ST}
(see Section
2
for the definition).
Each leaf
\ell
has an index
i_{\ell}
of
w
and corresponds to the suffix
w[i_{\ell}\,{\!\>..\!\>}\,|w|]

(
w=w[1]\,w[2]\,\ldots,w[|w|]
.)
Each inner node
v
represents the substring given by the path-label from the root.
For each
v
, we maintain the set
\mathscr{I}_{v}
of all indices
i_{\ell}
of leaves
\ell
in the subtree rooted at
v
.
We note that our suffix tree
\mathsf{ST}
is binary because
\Sigma=\{0,1\}
.

The key property of the problem is the branching condition.
It means that
the two paths from the root to
\ell_{w_{B}w_{C}w_{B}w_{D}}
and to
\ell_{w_{B}w_{D}}

branch
at the node
v
corresponding to
w_{B}
(as illustrated in the below figure).
Using this property, it suffices to consider the following task:

(\clubsuit)
:
For each node
v
, find a pair of suffixes branching at
v
that induces a feasible decomposition.

root
v
w_{B}\,w_{D}
w_{B}\,w_{C}\,w_{B}\,w_{D}
w_{B}
w_{D}
w_{C}\,w_{B}\,w_{D}

Illustration

of
(\clubsuit)

Let
\mathscr{H}_{v}:=\mathscr{I}_{u}
, where
u
is a child of
v
maximizing the size
|\mathscr{I}_{u}|
, and define

\mathscr{L}_{v}:=\mathscr{I}_{v}\setminus\mathscr{H}_{v}
.
We employ a classical
small-to-large

(or heavy-light decomposition) strategy:
for each
i\in\mathscr{L}_{v}

we determine whether there exists

j\in\mathscr{H}_{v}
such that the pair
(i,j)
induces a feasible decomposition.
Since
\sum_{v:\text{node}}|\mathscr{L}_{v}|=O(|w|\log|w|)
,
the remaining task of
(\clubsuit)
is to find such an index
j\in\mathscr{H}_{v}
in
O(\log|w|)
time for each
i\in\mathscr{L}_{v}
.
We then split the task
(\clubsuit)
into the two cases
Right-
B
and
Left-
B
in Section
7
.

In
Right-
B
, for each
i\in\mathscr{L}_{v}
,
we consider the case
i<j
: i.e., the shorter suffix
w_{B}w_{D}
starts at
j
.
Namely, we search for
j\in\mathscr{H}_{v}
such that
w[j{\!\>..\!\>}|w|]=w_{B}w_{D}
, illustrated as follows:

(\clubsuit_{R})\quad w=\underbrace{w[1{\!\>..\!\>}i)}_{w_{A}}\,\underbrace{w[i{\!\>..\!\>}i+|w_{B}|)}_{w_{B}}\,\underbrace{w[i+|w_{B}|{\!\>..\!\>}j)}_{w_{C}}\,\underbrace{w[j{\!\>..\!\>}j+|w_{B}|)}_{w_{B}}\,\underbrace{w[j+|w_{B}|{\!\>..\!\>}|w|]}_{w_{D}}.

In
Left-
B
, we consider the case
j<i
where
j
is the starting position of the longer suffix
w_{B}\,w_{C}\,w_{B}\,w_{D}
.
We search for
j\in\mathscr{H}_{v}
such that
w[j{\!\>..\!\>}|w|]=w_{B}w_{C}w_{B}w_{D}
, illustrated as follows:

(\clubsuit_{L})\quad w=\underbrace{w[1{\!\>..\!\>}j)}_{w_{A}}\,\underbrace{w[j{\!\>..\!\>}j+|w_{B}|)}_{w_{B}}\,\underbrace{w[j+|w_{B}|{\!\>..\!\>}i)}_{w_{C}}\,\underbrace{w[i{\!\>..\!\>}i+|w_{B}|)}_{w_{B}}\,\underbrace{w[i+|w_{B}|{\!\>..\!\>}|w|]}_{w_{D}}.

To handle both cases, we use the same high-level approach: traverse
\mathsf{ST}
bottom-up and maintain the sets
\mathscr{H}_{v}
using suitable data structures.
Since the concrete algorithms and data structures differ between the two cases, we present them separately.

Right-
B
Case (Sections
8
and
9
).

Let
v
be a node of
\mathsf{ST}
and

w_{B}
be the string represented by
v
.
Let
i\in\mathscr{L}_{v}
, where
w[i{\!\>..\!\>}|w|]
corresponds to
w_{B}w_{C}w_{B}w_{D}
.
We assume
w[1{\!\>..\!\>}i)\in\mathbb{L}\left(A\right)
and
w_{B}\in\mathbb{L}\left(B\right)
because otherwise there is no feasible solution.
Our task
(\clubsuit_{R})
is to find an index
j\in\mathscr{H}_{v}
such that
w[j{\!\>..\!\>}|w|]
corresponds to
w_{B}w_{D}
.
To treat the constraint
w_{C}\in\mathbb{L}\left(C\right)
,
we manage
\mathscr{H}_{v}
using a data structure that answers whether there is an index
j\in\mathscr{H}_{v}
with
i<j

such that
w[i+|w_{B}|\,{\!\>..\!\>}\,j)\in\mathbb{L}\left(C\right)
.
We call this structure
RightEnumerator
(Section
9
).

However, this is not yet sufficient, as we must also enforce the constraint
w_{D}\in\mathbb{L}\left(D\right)
.
Crucially, whether a fixed index
j
satisfies this constraint depends on the current node
v
.
This is because the substring corresponding to
w_{D}
begins at
j+|w_{B}|
, and this starting position shifts as the length
|w_{B}|
varies with
v
.
Consequently, we must handle the constraints for both
C
and
D
simultaneously. To address this, we partition
\mathcal{H}_{v}
into subsets such that all indices in the same subset share the same validity regarding
D
(Section
8
).
Leveraging the fact that
R
egex admits a finite transition monoid (see Section
2
for definition),
we can ensure that we need to maintain only a constant number of subsets.
By maintaining a separate instance of
RightEnumerator
for each subset and merging them using a disjoint set union data structure, we can resolve the issue and handle this case.

A simple way of constructing
RightEnumerator
is to use a
segment tree
, where each node is equipped with the set of elements of transition monoids (Section
9
).
This yields an
O(|w|\log^{3}|w|)
-time algorithm.
In our algorithm, employing a
factorization forest
(see Section
2
for definition),
we further eliminate one logarithmic factor and achieve
O(|w|\log^{2}|w|)
time.

Left-
B
Case (Sections
10
–
12
).

Let
v
be a node of
\mathsf{ST}
and
w_{B}
be the substring represented by
v
.
Let
i\in\mathscr{L}_{v}
, where
w[i{\!\>..\!\>}|w|]
corresponds to
w_{B}w_{D}
.
We assume
w_{B}\in\mathbb{L}\left(B\right)
and
w_{D}\in\mathbb{L}\left(D\right)
because otherwise there is no feasible solution.
Our task
(\clubsuit_{L})
is to find an index
j\in\mathscr{H}_{v}
with
j<i
such that
w[j{\!\>..\!\>}|w|]
corresponds to
w_{B}w_{C}w_{B}w_{D}
.
Moreover, we can handle the constraint for
A
simply by managing only indices
j\in\mathscr{H}_{v}
with
w[1{\!\>..\!\>}j)\in\mathbb{L}\left(A\right)

(recall that, unlike the Right-
B
case, it is independent of
v
).
The remaining task is to find
j
satisfying
j+|w_{B}|<i

and
w[j+|w_{B}|\,{\!\>..\!\>}\,i)\in\mathbb{L}\left(C\right)
.
We treat it as a data structure problem (Section
10.2
).
We note that
RightEnumerator
is insufficient for our purpose,
since the constraint
j+|w_{B}|<i
depends on
|w_{B}|
,
which varies with the current node
v
.

To address the problem, we consider the following two subcases separately:

\text{{near-$j$:}}~~\text{$j+|w_{B}|\leq i<j+2|w_{B}|$},\qquad\text{{far-$j$:}}~~\text{$j+2|w_{B}|\leq i$}.

In the near-
j
case (Section
12
), we derive a periodicity for
w_{B}
because, for any two indices
j,j^{\prime}
in the near-
j
case, the substrings
w[j\,{\!\>..\!\>}\,j+|w_{B}|)
and
w[j^{\prime}\,{\!\>..\!\>}\,j^{\prime}+|w_{B}|)
overlap.
With the periodicity inherent to
R
egex, we can efficiently solve the near-
j
case in
O(\log|w|)
time.
On the other hand, in the far-
j
case, the index
j
is “sufficiently far” from
i
.
Technically, the condition
j+2|w_{B}|\leq i
implies that
the distance between
j+|w_{B}|
and
i
is at least
|w_{B}|
; we use this margin for the design of our data structure.

To solve the far-
j
case, we adopt the following strategy.
We first build a data structure
AuxiliaryEnumerator
(Section
11
),
that maintains intervals
[j{\!\>..\!\>}k)
as the item
[j,\,k)
while ensuring
j+|w_{B}|\leq k
.
Given
i
, it answers whether there is an interval
[j,k)
such that
w[j+|w_{B}|\,{\!\>..\!\>}\,i)\in\mathbb{L}\left(C\right)
and
k\leq i
.
For the condition
j+2|w_{B}|\leq i
,
it suffices to ensure
k\leq j+2|w_{B}|
holds at any moment.
The issue is that as we traverse
\mathsf{ST}
, the node
v
changes (thus,
|w_{B}|
decreases), so the condition
k\leq j+2|w_{B}|
will be violated.
To resolve this issue, when the violation occurs
(the margin
k-(j+|w_{B}|)
becomes at least
|w_{B}|
),
we “re-insert” the new interval
[j,\,k^{\prime})
to the data structure, where
k^{\prime}:=j+|w_{B}|
.
The total overhead cost is
O(\log|w|)
time because “re-insertion” happens only after
|w_{B}|
is halved;
this is ensured by the “far-
j
” condition.

The remaining task is to construct
AuxiliaryEnumerator
(Section
11
).
This is similar to
RightEnumerator
, but requires additional mechanisms to quickly answer queries of
w[j+|w_{B}|\,{\!\>..\!\>}\,i)\in\mathbb{L}\left(C\right)
,
which depends on the current
v
(and thus,
w_{B}
).
We adopt a
lazy update
strategy for this purpose.
As in
RightEnumerator
, using the segment tree leads to the time complexity
O(|w|\log^{3}|w|)
, which can be accelerated to
O(|w|\log^{2}|w|)
using the factorization forest.

1.4
Organization

The rest of this paper is organized as follows.
In Section
2
, we introduce the necessary preliminaries and algorithmic tools we use in this paper, while we give some proofs in Appendix
A
for the sake of self-containment.
In Section
3
, we establish our hardness results by proving Theorems
3
,
4
, and
5
.
As discussed in Section
1.3
, Sections
4
–
12
concentrate on proving Theorem
7
.
Theorem
9
is proved in Section
6
as a corollary.
In Appendix
B
, we prove Theorem
6
.
In Appendix
C
, we analyze the hidden constants of Theorems
7
,
8
, and
9
.

2
Tools and Preliminaries

Integers.

We write
\mathbb{Z}
for the set of all integers and
write
\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
for
\{i\in\mathbb{Z}:i\geq 0\}
.
For an integer
n\geq 1
, we write
[n]
to denote the set
\{1,2,\ldots,n\}
.

Strings.

Let
\Sigma
be a finite alphabet.
We write
\Sigma^{*}
for the set of all finite strings over
\Sigma
and
\epsilon
for the empty string.
For
w\in\Sigma^{*}
,
|w|
is its length.
We use 1-based indexing: for an index
i\in\{1,\ldots,|w|\}
,
w[i]
is the
i
-th character of
w
.

For indices
i,j\in\{1,\ldots,|w|\}
with
i\leq j
,
w[i{\!\>..\!\>}j]:=w[i]\,w[i+1]\ldots w[j]
is the substring from
i
to
j
, inclusive.
We define
w[i{\!\>..\!\>}j]:=\epsilon
if
i>j
for convenience.
For example, if
w=abcd
, then
|w|=4
,
w[1]=a
,
w[4]=d
,
w[2{\!\>..\!\>}2]=b
,
w[2{\!\>..\!\>}4]=bcd
, and
w[2{\!\>..\!\>}1]=\epsilon
.
We also write
w[i{\!\>..\!\>}j)
for the shorthand for
w[i\,{\!\>..\!\>}\,j-1]
. It is convenient to extract the
\ell
-length substring starting from
i
.
For example, if
w=abcd
, then
w[1{\!\>..\!\>}1+3)=abc
,
w[2{\!\>..\!\>}2+3)=bcd
, and
w[1{\!\>..\!\>}1+0)=\epsilon
.
We similarly define
w(i{\!\>..\!\>}j]
for
w[i+1{\!\>..\!\>}j]
.

For a string
u
and an integer
k\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
, we write
u^{k}
for the string obtained by
k
-power of
u
.
For example,
(abaa)^{2}=abaa\,abaa
.
We naturally extend this notation to
u^{r/|u|}
with a rational
r/|u|\geq 0
.
For example,
(abaa)^{\frac{3}{4}}=aba
and
(abaa)^{2+\frac{2}{4}}=abaa\,abaa\,ab
.

If
w[i]=w[p+i]
for all
i\in\{1,2,\ldots,|w|-p\}
, then we say that
w
has a
period

p
.
Alternatively, if a prefix
u
of
w
satisfies
w=u^{r}
for some
r\geq 1
,
w
also has a period
|u|
.
For example,
w=abaa\,abaa\,ab
has three periods:
(i)
4
for
w=(abaa)^{2+\frac{2}{4}}
;
(ii)
8
for
w=(abaaabaa)^{1+\frac{2}{8}}
;
(iii)
10=|w|
for
w=w^{1}
.

The following theorem is well-known and quite useful property on periodic strings.
It is sometimes called Fine and Wilf’s theorem
[
44
]
.

Lemma 10

(
[
22
, Cor. 6.1]
)
.

Let
w
be a non-empty word.
If
w
has two periods
p
and
q
and
|w|\geq p+q-\mathit{gcd}(p,q)
,
then
w
also has a period
\mathit{gcd}(p,q)
.

R
egex: Classical Regular Expressions.

Let
\Sigma
be a fixed finite alphabet.

The calculus of (classical) regular expressions,
R
egex, is defined by the following grammar:

E::=\emptyset\mid\epsilon\mid\sigma\mid E_{1}+E_{2}\mid E_{1}\cdot E_{2}\mid E^{*}.

where
\sigma\in\Sigma
.

The language of
R
egex,
\mathbb{L}\left(\cdot\right)
, is defined as follows:

\begin{array}[]{lcl}\mathbb{L}\left(\emptyset\right)&=&\emptyset,\\
\mathbb{L}\left(e\right)&=&\{e\}\quad e\in\{\epsilon,\sigma\},\\
\mathbb{L}\left(E_{1}+E_{2}\right)&=&\mathbb{L}\left(E_{1}\right)\cup\mathbb{L}\left(E_{2}\right),\\
\mathbb{L}\left(E_{1}\cdot E_{2}\right)&=&\mathbb{L}\left(E_{1}\right)\cdot\mathbb{L}\left(E_{2}\right),\qquad(\text{\small where $X\cdot Y=\{xy:x\in X,y\in Y\}$})\\
\mathbb{L}\left(E^{*}\right)&=&\bigcup_{i\geq 0}(\mathbb{L}\left(E\right))^{i}\end{array}

where
L^{0}=\{\epsilon\}
and
L^{k+1}=L^{k}\cdot L
for
k\geq 0
; i.e.,
L^{k}
is the
k
-th power of
L
.

We define the size of a regular expression
E
, denoted
|E|
, as the total number of occurrences of operators (such as union, concatenation) and operands (alphabet symbols,
\epsilon
, and
\emptyset
) that appear in
E
.
The length of the string representation of
E
is then clearly bounded by
O(|E|)
.

R
EWB: Regular Expressions with Backreferences.

The calculus of extended regular expressions
R
EWB is an extension of
R
egex with
backreferences
.
Let
\mathcal{V}
be a finite set of
variables
.
Then, the grammar of
R
EWB over
\mathcal{V}
is the following:

E::=\emptyset\mid\epsilon\mid\sigma\mid E_{1}+E_{2}\mid E_{1}\cdot E_{2}\mid E^{*}\mid(E)_{x}\mid\mathord{\backslash x}

where
x\in\mathcal{V}
.
We write
k
-
R
EWB to denote the set of
R
EWBs that have
k
variables.

We define the semantics of
R
EWB via the semantic function
\llbracket E\rrbracket_{\lambda}
and then define the language.
For a
R
EWB
E
and a valuation
\lambda
from
\mathcal{V}
to
\Sigma^{*}
,

\llbracket E\rrbracket_{\lambda}
returns a set of pairs
\langle w,\lambda^{\prime}\rangle
consisting of a matched string and an updated valuation:

\begin{array}[]{lcl}\llbracket\epsilon\rrbracket_{\lambda}&:=&\{\,\langle\epsilon,\lambda\rangle\,\},\\
\llbracket\sigma\rrbracket_{\lambda}&:=&\{\,\langle\sigma,\lambda\rangle\,\},\\[3.0pt]
\llbracket\emptyset\rrbracket_{\lambda}&:=&\emptyset,\\[3.0pt]
\llbracket E_{1}\cdot E_{2}\rrbracket_{\lambda}&:=&\{\langle w_{1}w_{2},\nu\rangle:\langle w_{1},\mu\rangle\in\llbracket E_{1}\rrbracket_{\lambda},\langle w_{2},\nu\rangle\in\llbracket E_{2}\rrbracket_{\mu}\},\\[3.0pt]
\llbracket E_{1}+E_{2}\rrbracket_{\lambda}&:=&\llbracket E_{1}\rrbracket_{\lambda}\cup\llbracket E_{2}\rrbracket_{\lambda},\\[3.0pt]
\llbracket E^{*}\rrbracket_{\lambda}&:=&\bigcup_{k\geq 0}\llbracket E^{k}\rrbracket_{\lambda},\qquad\text{where}\ E^{0}:=\epsilon,\;E^{k+1}:=E^{k}E,\\[3.0pt]
\llbracket\,(E)_{x}\,\rrbracket_{\lambda}&:=&\{\langle w,\mu[x:=w]\rangle:\langle w,\mu\rangle\in\llbracket E\rrbracket_{\lambda}\},\\[3.0pt]
\llbracket\mathord{\backslash x}\rrbracket_{\lambda}&:=&\{\langle\lambda(x),\lambda\rangle\}.\end{array}

This function is well-founded for the height of Kleene stars.
This definition follows the formalization in
[
104
]
.

Let
\iota
be the initialized valuation that maps every variable to the empty string
\epsilon
: i.e.,
\iota(v)=\emptyset
for all
v\in\mathcal{V}
.
We simply write
\llbracket E\rrbracket
to denote
\llbracket E\rrbracket_{\iota}
.

We define the language of a
R
EWB
E
,
\mathbb{L}\left(E\right)
, as follows:

\mathbb{L}\left(E\right):=\{w:\langle w,\lambda\rangle\in\llbracket E\rrbracket\}.

If every computation generated by an expression
E
reads from variables at most
R
times in total,
we say
E
is
R
-use
.

R
EWB Example.

For example, the following expression
E_{1}
is a 1-
R
EWB because there only exists a variable
x
:

E_{1}=(\Sigma^{*})_{x}\ \#\ \Sigma^{*}\ \mathord{\backslash x}\ \Sigma^{*}.

The language of
E_{1}
is
\mathbb{L}\left(E_{1}\right)=\{w_{\text{pat}}\#w_{1}w_{\text{pat}}w_{2}:w_{\text{pat}},w_{1},w_{2}\in\Sigma^{*}\}
, and it is called a
pattern searching language
because, if
w_{\text{pat}}\#w
is matched with
E_{1}
, then
w_{\text{pat}}
appears in
w
.
The above
E_{1}
is
1
-use because every computation read
x
once by
\mathord{\backslash x}
.

\epsilon
-NFA: Nondeterministic finite automata with
\epsilon
-transitions.

An
\epsilon
-NFA
A
is a tuple
A=(Q,\Sigma,\Delta,\mathcal{E},q_{0},F)
where

Q
is a finite set of states,

\Sigma
is an input alphabet,

\Delta\subseteq Q\times\Sigma\times Q
is a set of transition rules,

\mathcal{E}\subseteq Q\times Q
is a set of
\epsilon
transitions,

q_{0}\in Q
is the initial state,
and
F\subseteq Q
is a set of final (accepting) states.
We write
p\xrightarrow{\sigma}q
for each transition element
(p,\sigma,q)
of
\Delta
and
p\xrightarrow{\epsilon}q
for
(p,q)\in\mathcal{E}
.
A rule
p\xrightarrow{\sigma}q
means that we can move to
q
from
p
by consuming a letter
\sigma
.
We write
p\xRightarrow{\epsilon}q
if we can reach
q
from
p
only using
\epsilon
-transitions
p\xrightarrow{\epsilon}\cdots\xrightarrow{\epsilon}q
.
We also write
p\xRightarrow{\sigma}q
if we can move to
q
from
p
by only consuming a letter
\sigma
: i.e., we have a sequence of transitions of the form

p\xRightarrow{\epsilon}p^{\prime}\xrightarrow{\sigma}p^{\prime\prime}\xRightarrow{\epsilon}q
.

If
\mathcal{E}=\emptyset
, we call
A
an
\epsilon
-free NFA.
We write
\Delta(p,\sigma)
to denote the set of states reachable from
p
by
\sigma
: i.e.,
\Delta(p,\sigma):=\{q:p\xrightarrow{\sigma}q\}
.
If
\mathcal{E}=\emptyset
and

|\Delta(p,\sigma)|=1
for all
(p,\sigma)\in Q\times\Sigma
,
we call
A
a
deterministic
automaton (DFA).

The language of
A
,
\mathbb{L}\left(A\right)
, is the set of words accepted by
A
and formally defined as follows:

\mathbb{L}\left(A\right):=\{\sigma_{1}\sigma_{2}\ldots\sigma_{n}:q_{0}\xRightarrow{\sigma_{1}}q_{1}\xRightarrow{\sigma_{2}}\cdots\xRightarrow{\sigma_{n}}q_{n},\ q_{n}\in F\}.

If
q_{0}\xRightarrow{\epsilon}q_{F}
, the empty string
\epsilon
belongs to
\mathbb{L}\left(A\right)
.

By the well-known Thompson’s construction,
every regular expression
E
can be translated in linear time into a sparse
\epsilon
-NFA
N_{E}
.

Proposition 11

(Thompson’s construction
[
103
]
[
95
, Sec. 5]
)
.

Let
E
be a
R
egex over an alphabet
\Sigma
.
We can translate
E
to a corresponding sparse
\epsilon
-NFA
N_{E}=(Q,\Sigma,\Delta,\mathcal{E},q_{0},F)
in
O(|E|)
-time where
\mathbb{L}\left(E\right)=\mathbb{L}\left(N_{E}\right)
and
|Q|=|\Delta|=|\mathcal{E}|=O(|E|)
.

Proposition 12

(Product Construction of
\epsilon
-NFA)
.

Let

A=(Q_{A},\Sigma,\Delta_{A},\mathcal{E}_{A},q^{A}_{0},F_{A})
and

B=(Q_{B},\Sigma,\Delta_{B},\mathcal{E}_{B},q^{B}_{0},F_{B})
be
\epsilon
-NFAs, where
\Delta_{A},\mathcal{E}_{A},\Delta_{B},\mathcal{E}_{B}
are represented by adjacency lists.
Then, there exists an
\epsilon
-NFA
C
such that

\mathbb{L}\left(C\right)=\mathbb{L}\left(A\right)\cap\mathbb{L}\left(B\right)
.
In particular, we can construct
C
in
O(|Q_{A}|\cdot|Q_{B}|+|\Delta_{A}|\cdot|\Delta_{B}|+|\mathcal{E}_{A}|\cdot|Q_{B}|+|Q_{A}|\cdot|\mathcal{E}_{B}|)
time.

Proof.

This follows from the classical product construction for DFA and (
\epsilon
-free) NFA (c.f.
[
95
, Sec. 5]
).
For completeness, we define the construction for our product
\epsilon
-NFA
C=(Q_{C},\Sigma,\Delta_{C},\mathcal{E}_{C},q^{C}_{0},F_{C})
as follows:

\begin{array}[]{lcl}Q_{C}&:=&Q_{A}\times Q_{B},\\
\Delta_{C}&:=&\{(p,q)\xrightarrow{\sigma}(p^{\prime},q^{\prime}):p\xrightarrow{\sigma}p^{\prime},\ q\xrightarrow{\sigma}q^{\prime}\},\\
\mathcal{E}_{C}&:=&\{(p,q)\xrightarrow{\epsilon}(p^{\prime},q):p\xrightarrow{\epsilon}p^{\prime}\}\cup\{(p,q)\xrightarrow{\epsilon}(p,q^{\prime}):q\xrightarrow{\epsilon}q^{\prime}\},\\
q^{C}_{0}&:=&(q^{A}_{0},\ q^{B}_{0}),\\
F_{C}&:=&F_{A}\times F_{B}.\end{array}

By the construction,
\mathbb{L}\left(C\right)=\mathbb{L}\left(A\right)\cap\mathbb{L}\left(B\right)
trivially holds.
Note that
adding
\{(p,q)\xrightarrow{\epsilon}(p^{\prime},q^{\prime}):p\xrightarrow{\epsilon}p^{\prime},\ q\xrightarrow{\epsilon}q^{\prime}\}
to
\mathcal{E}_{C}

does not change
\mathbb{L}\left(C\right)
, since such a move is simulated by two
\epsilon
-steps.

Regarding the time complexity, we can construct
Q_{C}
in
O(|Q_{A}|\cdot|Q_{B}|)
time.
Since the transitions are represented by adjacency lists,
we can construct
\Delta_{C}
in
O(|\Delta_{A}|\cdot|\Delta_{B}|)
time by iterating over matching transitions.
Similarly, we can build
\mathcal{E}_{C}
in
O(|\mathcal{E}_{A}|\cdot|Q_{B}|+|\mathcal{E}_{B}|\cdot|Q_{A}|)
time.
∎

Proposition 13

(Removal of the
\epsilon
word from
\epsilon
-NFA’s language)
.

Let

A=(Q_{A},\Sigma,\Delta_{A},\mathcal{E}_{A},q^{A}_{0},F_{A})
be an
\epsilon
-NFA.
Then, there exists an
\epsilon
-NFA
B
such that
\mathbb{L}\left(B\right)=\mathbb{L}\left(A\right)\setminus\{\,\epsilon\,\}
.
In particular, we can construct
B
in
O(|Q_{A}|+|\Delta_{A}|+|\mathcal{E}_{A}|)
time.

Proof.

We construct an
\epsilon
-NFA
B=(Q_{B},\Sigma,\Delta_{B},\mathcal{E}_{B},q^{B}_{0},F_{B})
step-by-step.
First, we define the set
Q_{B}
as follows:

\begin{array}[]{lcl}Q_{B}&:=&\{(q,0),\ (q,1):q\in Q_{A}\},\end{array}

We define
\Delta_{B}
and
\mathcal{E}_{B}
to satisfy the following condition:

•

A state
(q,0)
represents that the automaton has reached
q
from the initial state by using only
\epsilon
-transitions.

•

A state
(q,1)
means that we can reach
q
from
p
by using some non-
\epsilon
-transitions.

Therefore,

\begin{array}[]{lcl}\Delta_{B}&:=&\{(p,i)\xrightarrow{\sigma}(q,1):p\xrightarrow{\sigma}q,\ i\in\{0,1\}\},\\
\mathcal{E}_{B}&:=&\{(p,i)\xrightarrow{\epsilon}(q,i):p\xrightarrow{\epsilon}q,\ i\in\{0,1\}\}.\end{array}

We define
q^{B}_{0}:=(q^{A}_{0},0)
and
F^{B}:=F^{A}\times\{1\}
.

If
w\in\mathbb{L}\left(A\right)
and
w=\sigma w^{\prime}
, then we have a decomposition of the following form:

q^{A}_{0}\xRightarrow{\epsilon}q\xrightarrow{\sigma}q^{\prime}\xRightarrow{w^{\prime}}r\in F^{A}.

This computation is simulated in
B
as follows:

q^{B}_{0}=(q^{A}_{0},0)\xRightarrow{\epsilon}(q,0)\xrightarrow{\sigma}(q^{\prime},1)\xRightarrow{w^{\prime}}(r,1)\in F^{B}.

By this observation,
\mathbb{L}\left(A\right)\setminus\{\epsilon\}\subseteq\mathbb{L}\left(B\right)
clearly holds.
Conversely, if
B
accepts
w
, then the accepting run must reach some state in
F_{A}\times\{1\}
,
hence it uses at least one labeled transition; projecting the run to the first component yields an accepting run of
A
on
w
, so
w\neq\epsilon
and
w\in\mathbb{L}\left(A\right)
.

Regarding the time complexity, we can construct
Q_{B}
in
O(|Q_{A}|
time.
Since the transitions are represented by adjacency lists,
we can construct
\Delta_{B}
in
O(|\Delta_{A}|)
time and
\mathcal{E}_{B}
in
O(|\mathcal{E}_{A}|)
time.
∎

Remark:

The standard
\epsilon
-elimination procedure first computes
\epsilon
-closures (the reflexive transitive closure) of
\mathcal{E}
and then uses them to redirect labeled transitions and accepting states so as to remove
\epsilon
-transitions.
When
\mathcal{E}
is given as adjacency lists,
computing all
\epsilon
-closures by running a BFS graph search from every state takes

O(|Q|\,(|Q|+|\mathcal{E}|))
time in the worst case.
Alternatively, representing
\mathcal{E}
as an
|Q|\times|Q|
Boolean adjacency matrix allows one to compute its transitive closure in
O(|Q|^{3})
time via the Floyd-Warshall algorithm,
or in
O(|Q|^{\omega}\log|Q|)
time via fast Boolean matrix multiplication
[
50
,
76
,
45
]
.
In contrast, Proposition
13
addresses the weaker task of removing only the empty word
\epsilon

from the language (without eliminating
\epsilon
-transitions),
and it runs in linear
O(|Q|+|\Delta|+|\mathcal{E}|)
time without computing the
\epsilon
-closure.

Ultimately Periodic Set.

A set
P
of non-negative integers is
ultimately periodic

if there is 4-tuple
(\mu,M,\lambda,T)
where

•

\mu
is a non-negative integer and
M\subseteq\{0,1,\ldots,\mu-1\}
; and

•

\lambda
is a positive integer and
T\subseteq\{0,1,\ldots,\lambda-1\}

and the following holds:

n\in P\iff\begin{cases}n\in M&\text{if }n<\mu,\\
(n\bmod\lambda)\in T&\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}

The class of ultimately periodic sets enjoys several closure properties.
In this paper, we will use the following ones in
[
52
]
, for which we give a proof in Appendix
A
for self-containedness.

Lemma 14
.

Let
P
and
Q
be two ultimately periodic sets. Then, we have the following.

•

The union
P\cup Q
is ultimately periodic.

•

The Minkowski sum
P+Q:=\{p+q:p\in P,q\in Q\}
is ultimately periodic.

•

For any positive integer
e
,
the set
e\cdot P:=\{ep:p\in P\}
is ultimately periodic.

Monoid.

A
monoid

\mathcal{M}
is a tuple
(M,\odot,1_{M})
where
M
is a base set,
\odot:M\times M\to M
is an associative binary function —
(m_{1}\odot m_{2})\odot m_{3}=m_{1}\odot(m_{2}\odot m_{3})
for any
m_{1},m_{2},m_{3}\in M
— and
1_{M}
is the identity element —
m\odot 1_{M}=1_{M}\odot m=m
for any
m\in M
.
An element
e\in M
is
idempotent
if
e\odot e=e
holds.
By definition, the identity element
1_{M}
is idempotent, but the opposite direction does not necessarily hold.

The following objects are typical examples of monoids:
(1) The set of all strings of
\Sigma^{*}
forms an infinite monoid
(\Sigma^{*},\bullet,\epsilon)
where
\_\bullet\_
is concatenation: i.e.,
w_{1}\bullet w_{2}=w_{1}w_{2}
.
(2) The set of all mappings
X^{X}=\{f:X\to X\}
on a set
X
forms a monoid
(X^{X},\circ,\text{id}_{X})
where
\circ
is the function composition and
\text{id}_{X}
is the identity function.
(3) The set of non-negative integers with addition
(\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0},+,0)
forms an infinite monoid.

Let
\mathcal{M}=(M,\odot,1_{M})
be a monoid,
m\in M
be an element of
M
, and
X,Y\subseteq M
be subsets of
M
.
We naturally extend the monoid operator
\odot
to sets as follows:

m\odot X:=\{m\odot x:x\in X\},\quad X\odot m:=\{x\odot m:x\in X\},\quad X\odot Y:=\{x\odot y:x\in X,y\in Y\}.

Let
\mathcal{M}_{1}=(M_{1},\odot_{1},1_{M_{1}})
and
\mathcal{M}_{2}=(M_{2},\odot_{2},1_{M_{2}})
be monoids.
If a function
\varphi:\mathcal{M}_{1}\to\mathcal{M}_{2}
has the following property, it is a
monoid homomorphism
:

\varphi(a\odot_{1}b)=\varphi(a)\odot_{2}\varphi(b).

Example.

Let
\mathcal{M_{1}}
be the monoid formed by the set of all strings
\Sigma^{*}
with concatenation and
\mathcal{M}_{2}
be the infinite monoid of the non-negative integers
\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
with addition
+
.
Then, the length function
\ell:\mathcal{M}_{1}(=\Sigma^{*})\to\mathcal{M}_{2}(=\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0})
is a monoid homomorphism, as
\ell(w_{1}w_{2})=\ell(w_{1})+\ell(w_{2})
for any string
w_{1}
and
w_{2}
.

Transition Monoids.

Let
\mathcal{M}=(M,\odot,1_{M})
be a finite monoid,

S\,(\subseteq M)
be a subset of
M
, and
\alpha:\Sigma^{*}\to\mathcal{M}
be a monoid homomorphism.
A language
L\subseteq\Sigma^{*}
is
recognized
by the triple
(\mathcal{M},S,\alpha)
if
L=\alpha^{-1}(S)=\{w:\alpha(w)\in S\}
.
In this case, we refer to
\mathcal{M}
as a
transition monoid
of
L
.

4
4

4

Traditionally, a transition monoid is a transformation monoid
T\subseteq X^{X}
(under function composition) on some set
X
.

T
is typically generated by the transition maps of an automaton (particularly, of a DFA).
Given a recognizing triple
(\mathcal{M},S,\alpha)
,
we obtain a transformation monoid on
X=M
via the right action
\rho_{w}(m):=m\odot\alpha(w)
.
Indeed,
w\in L\iff\alpha(w)\in S\iff\rho_{w}(1_{M})\in S
.
Thus, we use “transition monoid” for
\mathcal{M}
as a mild abuse of terminology.

The following classical result connecting regular languages and
recognizability by finite monoids is fundamental in automata theory.

Proposition 15

(
[
93
,
92
]
[
95
, Chap. II]
)
.

Let
L\subseteq\Sigma^{*}
be a language.
The following conditions are equivalent:

1.

L
is regular: i.e., there exists a
R
egex
A
or an
\epsilon
-NFA
A
such that
L=\mathbb{L}\left(A\right)
.

2.

L
is recognized by some triple
(\mathcal{M},S,\alpha)
.

For a
R
egex
E
, the standard construction—translating
E
to an NFA, determinizing it to a DFA, and then taking the transition monoid—yields a transition monoid of size at most
N^{N}
with
N=2^{O(|E|)}
, and hence at most
2^{2^{O(|E|)}}
.

The following explicit construction yields a smaller transition monoid of size
2^{O(\,|E|^{2}\,)}
.

Lemma 16

(
[
15
,
61
]
)
.

Let
E
be a
R
egex over an alphabet
\Sigma
.
There exists a triple
(\mathcal{M},S,\alpha)
that recognizes
\mathbb{L}\left(E\right)
.
In particular, the size of
\mathcal{M}
is
2^{O(|E|^{2})}
and we can construct it in
2^{O(|E|^{2})}
time.

Proof.

By Proposition
11
, we can construct an
\epsilon
-NFA
A
for
E

with
O(|E|)
states and transitions in time
O(|E|)
.
Moreover, by computing the
\epsilon
-closure of
A
,
we can construct an
\epsilon
-free NFA
B
such that
\mathbb{L}\left(E\right)=\mathbb{L}\left(B\right)
in time polynomial in the size of
A
.
In particular,
A
and
B
have the same set of states.

From the
\epsilon
-free NFA
B
,
we construct our Boolean matrix monoid
\mathcal{M}
as follows.
We write
B=(Q,\Sigma,\Delta,\mathcal{E}=\emptyset,q_{0},F)
.
For each
\sigma\in\Sigma
, let
M^{\sigma}
be the
|Q|\times|Q|
Boolean matrix defined by
M^{\sigma}_{p,q}=1
iff
(p,\sigma,q)\in\Delta
.
Let
\mathcal{M}
be the submonoid of
\{0,1\}^{Q\times Q}

generated by
\{M^{\sigma}:\sigma\in\Sigma\}
under Boolean matrix multiplication — using the Boolean OR
\vee
as addition and the Boolean AND
\wedge
as multiplication — with the identity element given by the identity matrix
\mathbf{I}
.

Now we define our monoid homomorphism
\alpha:\Sigma^{*}\to\mathcal{M}
as follows:

\alpha(\sigma):=M^{\sigma}.

Observe that
w\in\mathbb{L}\left(B\right)
iff there exists
q_{f}\in F

with
(\alpha(w))_{q_{0},q_{f}}=1
.
Therefore, letting

S=\{X\in\mathcal{M}:\exists q\in F.\,X_{q_{0},q}=1\},

the triple
(\mathcal{M},S,\alpha)
recognizes
\mathbb{L}\left(B\right)
and thus
\mathbb{L}\left(E\right)
.

Finally, since
\mathcal{M}\subseteq\{0,1\}^{Q\times Q}
,
we have
|\mathcal{M}|\leq 2^{|Q|^{2}}
.
Since
|Q|=O(|E|)
, this yields
|\mathcal{M}|=2^{O(|E|^{2})}
,
and
\mathcal{M}
can be constructed by
closing
\{\mathbf{I}\}\cup\{M^{\sigma}:\sigma\in\Sigma\}
under multiplication, in time polynomial in
|\mathcal{M}|
and
|Q|
.
∎

Relational Transition Monoid
\mathcal{T}_{E}
and Associated Homomorphism
\Delta_{E}
of
R
egex
E
.

Let
E
be a
R
egex, and let
(\mathcal{M},S,\alpha)
be the recognizing triple constructed in Lemma
16
for
E
.

Hereafter, we refer to the Boolean matrix monoid
\mathcal{M}
as the
(relational) transition monoid
of
E

and write
\mathcal{T}_{E}
to denote
\mathcal{M}
.
Moreover, we write
\Delta_{E}
to denote the homomorphism
\alpha
and set
S_{E}:=S
.
With this notation, we have the following:

w\in\mathbb{L}(E)\iff\Delta_{E}(w)\in S_{E}.

Factorization Forest.

Let
E
be a
R
egex. Consider the following range evaluation problem over a fixed domain.

1.

We receive a domain (string)
w
and perform some pre-processing for
w
.

2.

For each query
(i,j)
with
1\leq i<j\leq|w|
, we decide if
w[i{\!\>..\!\>}j]\in_{?}\mathbb{L}\left(E\right)
in
O(1)
time for fixed
E
.

To answer every query in
O(1)
time for fixed
E
, we employ
factorization forests

[
17
,
23
,
100
]
.

Let
\Sigma
be a finite alphabet,
\mathcal{M}
be a finite monoid, and
\varphi:\Sigma^{*}\to\mathcal{M}
be a homomorphism from the set of strings
\Sigma^{*}
.
Let
w=\sigma_{1}\sigma_{2}\ldots\sigma_{N}\in\Sigma^{+}
be a non-empty string.
A
\varphi
-factorization forest of
w
(Ramseyan factorization tree), denoted by
\mathsf{FF}_{\varphi}(w)
, is an ordered rooted tree in which a letter is assigned to each leaf, and reading those letters on leaves from left to right yields
w
.
Each node
v
is annotated by a value
\nu(v)\in\mathcal{M}
and corresponds to a contiguous factor of
w
.
The annotation
\nu(\cdot)
satisfies the following condition:

Leaf

If
v
is a leaf with letter
\sigma_{i}
, then
\nu(v)=\varphi(\sigma_{i})
.

Binary

If
v
has two children
c_{1}
and
c_{2}
, then
\nu(v)=c_{1}\odot c_{2}
.

Idempotent

If
v
has at least three children
c_{1},c_{2},\ldots,c_{m}
, then
\nu(c_{1})=\nu(c_{2})=\cdots=\nu(c_{m})=e
is an idempotent element.
It also holds that
\nu(v)=e
due to the idempotency of
e
.

For each node
v
of the factorization forest,
we write
[\ell_{v},r_{v})
to denote the interval exactly covered by
v
,
\{\ell_{v},\ell_{v}+1,\ldots,r_{v}-1\}
,
where
\ell_{v}
refers to the leftmost index managed by the node
v
and

r_{v}
refers to the index immediately to the right of the rightmost index managed by the node
v
.

Example of Factorization Forest.

Consider the following NFA
N
over
\Sigma=\{a,b\}
.

q_{0}
q_{1}
q_{2}
\bot
b
a
b
a
b
a
a,b

This automaton is actually a DFA and has four states
q_{0}
,
q_{1}
,
q_{2}
, and
\bot
.
This DFA recognizes the language
b^{*}ab^{*}ab^{*}
: i.e., the set of words that contain exactly two occurrences of
a
.

There are two base monoid elements (matrices)
\delta_{a}
and
\delta_{b}
of the relational transition monoid of
N
, which correspond to the sets of transitions by the letters
a
and
b
:

\delta_{a}=\scalebox{0.8}{$\begin{pNiceMatrix}$},\qquad\delta_{b}=\scalebox{0.8}{$\begin{pNiceMatrix}$}.

By composing these two matrices by using the Boolean matrix multiplication
(\odot)
,
we obtain the following two elements:

\delta_{2}=\scalebox{0.8}{$\begin{pNiceMatrix}$},\qquad\delta_{3}=\scalebox{0.8}{$\begin{pNiceMatrix}$}.

For example,
\delta_{2}=\delta_{a}\odot\delta_{b}
and
\delta_{3}=\delta_{2}\odot\delta_{a}
.
There are no other matrices in the transition monoid; therefore,

\mathcal{T}_{N}=(\{\delta_{a},\delta_{b}=\mathbf{1},\delta_{2},\delta_{3}\},\odot,\mathbf{1})
where
\mathbf{1}
is the identity matrix.

Let us consider the following factorization forest over a string
aaababaabaaabbbbb
:

w
a
a
a
b
a
b
a
a
b
a
a
a
b
b
b
b
b
\delta_{2}
\delta_{1}
\delta_{1}
\delta_{2}
\delta_{1}
\delta_{2}
\delta_{3}
\delta_{3}
\delta_{3}
\delta_{b}
\delta_{b}
\delta_{3}
\delta_{3}

For simplicity, we do not display the values for the leaf nodes. Assume every leaf (i.e., letter
\sigma
) has
\nu(\sigma)=\delta_{\sigma}
.

Then, we can easily check
(1) that every binary node
n
has the correct value
\nu(c_{1})\odot\nu(c_{2})
using its child
c_{1}
and
c_{2}
;
and also
(2) for the two idempotent nodes,
\delta_{3}=\delta_{3}\odot\delta_{3}\odot\delta_{3}
holds and also
\delta_{b}=\delta_{b}\odot\delta_{b}\odot\delta_{b}\odot\delta_{b}
holds.

The (red) idempotent node
v
labeled with
\delta_{3}
covers the interval
[1,2,\ldots,12]
. Thus, we write
[\ell_{v},r_{v})=[1,13)
.
Similarly, the idempotent node
v^{\prime}
labeled with
\delta_{b}
covers the interval
[14,15,16,17]
; thus,
[\ell_{v^{\prime}},r_{v^{\prime}})=[14,18)
.
Every leaf node
\ell_{i}
of the index
i
exactly covers
[i]
; thus,
[\ell_{i},r_{i})=[i,i+1)
.

There are multiple factorization forests for a single string.
For every monoid homomorphism
\varphi:\Sigma^{*}\to\mathcal{M}
from
\Sigma^{*}
to a
finite
monoid
\mathcal{M}
,
the following result ensures that we can effectively compute a factorization forest
\mathsf{FF}_{\varphi}(w)
for a given string
w
in linear time
O(|w|)
.
Moreover, the height of the factorization forest does not depend on
w
; indeed, it just depends on
\mathcal{M}
.

Lemma 17

(Factorization Forest
[
16
,
23
,
69
,
70
,
100
]
)
.

Let
\mathcal{M}=(M,\odot,1_{M})
be a finite monoid and

\lambda
be the computational cost of performing
\odot
once.
For every word
w\in\Sigma^{*}
, there is a
\mathsf{FF}_{\varphi}(w)
of height
\leq 3|M|
, which depends only on
M
, but not on
w
or
\varphi
.
Moreover, we can compute
\mathsf{FF}_{\varphi}(w)
in
O(|M|^{3}\cdot\lambda+|M||w|)
time
5
5

5

Although Kufleitner does not mention the detailed time complexity in
[
69
,
70
]
,
we can obtain the time complexity
O(|M|^{3}\cdot\lambda+|M|\cdot|w|)
as follows.
We first compute the data structure for
\mathcal{J}
-class (Green’s relation), which is a basic tool to build factorization forests, in
O(|M|^{3}\cdot\lambda)
.
Then we can build
\mathsf{FF}_{\varphi}(w)
in
O(|M|\cdot|w|)
time using the recursive procedure in
[
69
]
.
.

We also refer to the following application of factorization forests.

Lemma 18

(Range Evaluation
[
16
,
23
]
)
.

Let
w\in\Sigma^{*}
be a string,
\varphi:\Sigma^{*}\to\mathcal{M}
be a monoid homomorphism, and
\mathsf{FF}_{\varphi}(w)
be a factorization forest of
w
and
\varphi
.
Then, we can compute
\varphi(w[i{\!\>..\!\>}j])
in
O(h\cdot\lambda)
-time, where
h
is the height of
\mathsf{FF}_{\varphi}(w)
and
\lambda
is the computational complexity of the monoid operation cost of
\mathcal{M}
.

(Proof Sketch).

We search nodes in
\mathsf{FF}_{\varphi}(w)
top-down to exactly cover
w[i{\!\>..\!\>}j]
.
Efficiently using idempotent nodes, we can achieve
O(|M|)
monoid operations; therefore, we totally need
O(|M|\cdot\lambda)
-time.
For more detailed construction, refer to
[
16
, Section 4]
and
[
23
]
.
∎

Suffix Tree.

The
suffix tree

[
28
,
29
,
58
]

\mathsf{ST}_{w}
of a string
w
is an edge-labeled rooted tree such that

•

each edge is equipped with a substring of
w
, and

•

for each index
i\in[|w|]
, there is a node
v
such that the string obtained by concatenating the strings on the edges on the root-
v
path (in this order) coincides with the suffix
w[i{\!\>..\!\>}|w|]
.

For a given string
w
,
\mathsf{ST}_{w}
can be computed in linear time
O(|w|)
, where labels on edges are represented by intervals.

We use the following notation:

•

\Gamma(v)
: The path label of
v
: i.e., the string obtained by concatenating the edge labels on the path from the root to
v
.

•

\mathit{isSuffix}(v)
:
A binary flag indicating whether
\Gamma(v)
is a suffix of
w
.
Specifically,
\mathit{isSuffix}(v)=1
if and only if
\Gamma(v)
is a suffix of
w
.

For a suffix node
v
(i.e.,
\mathit{isSuffix}(v)=1
),
we define
\mathit{Occ}(v)
as the starting position of the suffix
\Gamma(v)
in
w
:
\mathit{Occ}(v):=|w|-|\Gamma(v)|+1
.
For a node
v
, we write
\mathsf{ST}_{w}[v]
for the subtree of
\mathsf{ST}_{w}
rooted at
v
.

Remark.

Throughout this paper, we consider suffix trees without appending the special end-marker symbol $.
In particular, some suffixes may end at internal nodes; we mark such nodes with a Boolean flag
\mathit{isSuffix}(v)
, which is 1 iff the path label
\Gamma(v)
is a suffix of
w
.

Example of Suffix Tree.

Let us consider the suffix tree for the following string of length 10:

w=\begin{array}[]{ll}\text{index}&1\,2\,3\,4\,5\,6\,7\,8\,9\,0\\
\hline\cr\text{letter}&a\,a\,b\,a\,a\,b\,a\,b\,b\,a\end{array}

{forest}

This figure puts an integer
i
to a suffix node
v
(i.e.,
\mathit{isSuffix}(v)=1
) where
i=\mathit{Occ}(v)
.
As the figure shows, every leaf node is labeled with an integer
i
where
1\leq i\leq|w|
.
A leaf labeled with
i
corresponds to the suffix of
w
starting at position
i
.
For example, the leaf
\ell_{5}
labeled 5 represents the suffix starting from
5
, i.e.,
w[5{\!\>..\!\>}|w|]=ababba
,
and its path-label is
\Gamma(\ell_{5})=a\,b\,a\,bba
.

In addition to leaves, some inner nodes are also labeled with integers.
For example, the nodes labeled
9
and
10
in the figure correspond to suffixes of
w
.
Let
n_{9}
and
n_{10}
denote these nodes.
Then
\Gamma(n_{9})=ba
and
\Gamma(n_{10})=a
, both of which are suffixes of
w
.
It follows that
\mathit{Occ}(n_{9})=9
and
\mathit{Occ}(n_{10})=10
.

The subtree
\mathsf{ST}_{w}[n_{10}]
contains all suffixes of
w
that start with the letter
a=\Gamma(n_{10})
.
Similarly, the subtree
\mathsf{ST}_{w}[n_{9}]
contains all suffixes of
w
that start with the string
ba=\Gamma(n_{9})

Remark:
Unlike the above illustration, every actual edge from a node
v
to its child
u
, denoted
v\xrightarrow{(l,r)}u
, is labeled a pair of integers
1\leq l\leq r\leq|w|
.
The pair
(l,r)
represents the substring
w[l{\!\>..\!\>}r]
.
This path compression is crucial to build the suffix tree in linear time.
It is noteworthy that on the paths from a node
v
to two of its children,
u_{1}
and
u_{2}
,
where
v\xrightarrow{(l_{1},r_{1})}u_{1}
and
v\xrightarrow{(l_{2},r_{2})}u_{w}
,
the first characters of the edge labels must be different, i.e.,
w[l_{1}]\neq w[l_{2}]
holds.

Heavy-Light Decomposition (of Suffix Tree).

Let
\mathsf{ST}
be the suffix tree of a string
w
.
We classify every edge as either heavy or light as follows.
For each non-leaf node
u\in\mathsf{ST}
with a left child
\ell
and a right child
r
:

•

If
|\,\mathsf{ST}[\ell]\,|\geq|\,\mathsf{ST}[r]\,|
(i.e., the number of nodes in the left subtree
\geq
the number of nodes in the right subtree),
then the edge
u\to\ell
is
heavy
and the edge
u\to r
is
light
.
In case of equality, the left edge is chosen as heavy by convention.

•

Otherwise, the edge
u\to\ell
is
light
and the edge
u\to r
is
heavy
.

For each non-leaf node
u\in\mathsf{ST}
with a single child
v
, the edge
u\to v
is a
heavy
edge.
A heavy path
\pi
is a maximal path such that every edge is heavy.
The collection of all heavy paths forms a partition of the nodes of
\mathsf{ST}
.
We refer to this partition as the
heavy-light decomposition
of
\mathsf{ST}
.

Example of Heavy Light Decomposition.

The left-hand and right-hand sides of the following figure illustrate the heavy-light decomposition of the suffix trees for
aabaababba
and
babbabbba
, respectively.

As the figures illustrate, a leaf node may form a heavy path consisting of a single node. All leaf nodes are the bottommost node of some heavy-path and vice versa. Furthermore, if an inner node has only one child, the edge to that child is heavy by definition.

The heavy-light decomposition guarantees the following basic and well-known property.

Proposition 19
.

Let
\mathsf{ST}_{w}
be a suffix tree of a string
w
, and consider its heavy-light path decomposition.
For any node
v\in\mathsf{ST}
,
the path from the root to
v
contains
O(\log|w|)
light edges.

From this property, the following useful fact can be derived.
Suppose that, for every inner node
v\in\mathsf{ST}_{w}
, we collect all vertices in the subtree
\mathsf{ST}_{w}[u]
rooted at the light child
u
of
v
where
v\to u
is a light edge.
Then the total cost of this collecting operation, summed over all nodes
v
, is
O(|w|\log|w|)
.

3
Hardness

We show our three hardness results in this section:

1.

The
R
EWB matching problem is
W[2]
-hard (Theorem
3
) in Section
3.1
.

2.

Under a hypothesis for the triangle detection problem, 2-use 2-
R
EWB matching problem cannot be solved in almost linear time (Theorem
4
) in Section
3.2
.

3.

Under
S
ETH (more technically,
k-OV
Hypothesis),

k
-
R
EWB matching problem cannot be solved in
O(n^{2k-\epsilon})
time (Theorem
5
) in Section
3.3
.

In Section
3.1
and
3.2
, we use the following notation.
Let
G=(\mathcal{V},\mathcal{E})
be an undirected graph without self-loops where
\mathcal{V}
is a set of vertices and
\mathcal{E}
is a set of edges.
For our constructions, we assume that each vertex
v
is uniquely encoded as a binary string of length
O(\log n)
; that is,
v\in(0+1)^{+}
, where
E^{+}:=E\,E^{*}
denotes one or more repetitions of a
R
EWB
E
.

For readability, in this Section
3
, we sometimes write
[E]_{x}
instead of
(E)_{x}
.

3.1
W[2]-hardness for
R
EWB: Reduction from
k
-Dominating Set Problem

We prove Theorem
3
by reduction from the following
k
-dominating set problem (
k\text{-}\textbf{DSP}
).

†
†
itembox-title:
k\text{-}\textbf{DSP}
:
k
-Dominating Set Problem
Input
: An undirected graph
G=(\mathcal{V},\mathcal{E})
and an integer
k
.

Task
: Deciding if there is a
k
-dominating set: i.e., an (exactly)
k
-vertex subset of
\mathcal{D}
that satisfies the following condition:

\forall v\in\mathcal{V}.\>((v\in\mathcal{D})\lor(\exists u\in\mathcal{D}.(u,v)\in\mathcal{E})).

We note that, since
G
is undirected,
(u,v)\in E\iff(v,u)\in E
.
It is known that this problem is
\mathbf{W[2]}
-complete
[
37
]
when parameterized by
k
.
Furthermore, the following is known.

Lemma 20

(
[
90
]
[
32
, Thm 14.42]
)
.

Assuming
S
ETH, there is no algorithm running in
O(n^{k-\epsilon})
time solving
k\text{-}\textbf{DSP}
for any
\epsilon>0
.

Our main task is to provide the following reduction from
k\text{-}\textbf{DSP}
to
k
-
R
EWB matching problem.

Lemma 21

(Reduction from
k\text{-}\textbf{DSP}
to
k
-
R
EWB Matching Problem)
.

Let
k\geq 1
be an integer.
In time
O(k)
, we can construct a
k
-
R
EWB
\Psi_{k}
of length
O(k)
satisfying the following.

Let
G=(\mathcal{V},\mathcal{E})
be an undirected graph.
There exists a string
\widetilde{G}
such that the following holds:

\widetilde{G}\in\mathbb{L}\left(\Psi_{k}\right)\iff\text{ there is a $k$-Dominating Set $\mathcal{D}$ of $G$}.

In particular, we can construct the string
\widetilde{G}
in
O((n+m)\log n)
time with
|\widetilde{G}|=O((n+m)\log n)

where
n:=|\mathcal{V}|
is the number of vertices and
m:=|\mathcal{E}|
is the number of edges.

From this lemma, we obtain the following
W[2]
-hardness result.
See
3

Proof.

We give an FPT-reduction from
k\text{-}\textbf{DSP}
.

Given an instance
(G,k)
,
we output the matching instance
(\Psi_{k},\widetilde{G})
,
where
\Psi_{k}
is constructed in
O(k)
time
and
\widetilde{G}
is constructed in
O((n+m)\log n)
time as in Lemma
21
,
such that
\widetilde{G}\in\mathbb{L}\left(\Psi_{k}\right)
iff
G
has a
k
-dominating set of size exactly
k
.

Moreover, the parameter of the output instance satisfies
|\Psi_{k}|=O(k)
.
Hence, this is an FPT-reduction, and the claim follows
since
k\text{-}\textbf{DSP}
is
W[2]
-hard.
∎

We note that this result is stronger than
W[2]
-hardness parameterized by the number of variables
k
,
since the number of variables
k
is at most the expression length
|\Psi_{k}|
.

3.1.1
Proof of Lemma
21

To reduce the
k\text{-}\textbf{DSP}
to
k
-
R
EWB matching problem, we first encode the given graph
G=(\mathcal{V},\mathcal{E})
into a string.
For
\mathcal{V}=\{v_{1},\ldots,v_{n}\}
, we assume that each vertex
v_{i}\in\{0,1\}^{+}
is a distinct
\lceil\,\log n\rceil
-bit encoding.

For a vertex
v
with its adjacency list
\text{Adj}(v)=\{u\in\mathcal{V}:(v,u)\in\mathcal{E}\}=\{u_{1},\ldots,u_{t}\}
,
we define

\alpha(v):=\langle v\rangle\>u_{1}\,\#\,u_{2}\,\#\,\cdots\,\#\,u_{t}\,\#\>\langle/v\rangle.

For example, if
\text{Adj}(10)=\{1111,00,01,100\}
,
\alpha(10)=\langle 10\rangle\,1111\,\#\,00\,\#\,01\,\#\,100\#\,\langle/10\rangle
.
It is clear that, for each vertex
v
,
|\alpha(v)|=O(e_{v}\log n)
where
e_{v}:=|\text{Adj}(v)|
is the number of edges connected to
v
.

Then, we define
\widetilde{G}
as a string over an alphabet
\Sigma=\{0,1,\#,\natural,@,\langle,\rangle,/\}
as follows:

w_{\mathcal{V}}:=v_{1}\,\#\,v_{2}\,\#\,\cdots\,\#\,v_{n}\,\#,\qquad w_{\mathcal{E}}:=\alpha(v_{1})\,\natural\,\alpha(v_{2})\,\natural\cdots\natural\,\alpha(v_{n})\,\natural,\qquad\widetilde{G}:=w_{\mathcal{V}}\>@\>w_{\mathcal{E}},

where
\mathcal{V}=\{v_{1},v_{2},\ldots,v_{n}\}
with
v_{i}\in(0+1)^{+}
.
It is clear that
|\widetilde{G}|=O(n\log n)+\sum_{v\in\mathcal{V}}|\alpha(v)|=O(n\log n+m\log n)
, where
n
is the number of vertices and
m
is the number of edges.

Hereafter, we translate the dominating set constraint into a
R
EWB expression.
To this end, we introduce our building blocks (expressions)
\psi_{\text{pick}},\psi_{\text{itself}},\psi_{\text{neigh}}
.

We first define
\psi_{\text{pick}}
as follows:

\psi_{\text{pick}}:=\psi_{\text{skip}}\>(\psi_{\mathcal{V}})_{x_{1}}\>\#\>\psi_{\text{skip}}\>(\psi_{\mathcal{V}})_{x_{2}}\>\#\>\cdots\>\#\psi_{\text{skip}}\>(\psi_{\mathcal{V}})_{x_{k}}\>\#\>\psi_{\text{skip}}

where
\psi_{\mathcal{V}}:=(0+1)^{+}
and
\psi_{\text{skip}}:=(\psi_{\mathcal{V}}\,\#)^{*}
.
Each vertex code is delimited by
\#
,
hence
\psi_{\text{skip}}
can skip any number of vertices before binding the next one.

The expression
\psi_{\text{pick}}
is applied to
w_{\mathcal{V}}
to nondeterministically select
k
vertices.
For example, we consider the following
w_{\mathcal{V}}
:

w_{\mathcal{V}}=0\#1\#01\#10\#11\#100\#

When
k=2
, applying
\psi_{\text{pick}}
generates the following computation, for example:
