\overbracket{\phantom{ho}}^{\psi_{\text{skip}}}\overbracket{\,0\,}^{(\psi_{\mathcal{V}})_{x_{1}}}\overbracket{\,\#\,}^{\#}\,\overbracket{1\#\,01\#\,10\#}^{\psi_{\text{skip}}}\overbracket{\,11\,}^{(\psi_{\mathcal{V}})_{x_{2}}}\overbracket{\,\#\,}^{\#}\,\overbracket{\,100\#\,}^{\psi_{\text{skip}}}.

In particular, after this computation,
x_{1}
is bound to
0
and
x_{2}
to
11
.

From the construction and this explanation,
it is clear that, by applying
\psi_{\text{pick}}
to
w_{\mathcal{V}}
,
we can nondeterministically select all
k
-vertex combinations and store each combination into
k
variables
x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{k}
.
This property of
\psi_{\text{pick}}
is formalized as follows.

Proposition 22
.

For each
k
-subset
U=\{u_{1},\dots,u_{k}\}\subseteq\mathcal{V}
,
there exists a match of
w_{\mathcal{V}}
with
\psi_{\text{pick}}
such that the variables
x_{1},\ldots,x_{k}
are bound to the elements of
U
in the order they appear in
w_{\mathcal{V}}
.

Conversely, in any match of
w_{\mathcal{V}}
with
\psi_{\text{pick}}
,
the variables
x_{1},\ldots,x_{k}
are bound to
k
distinct vertices, hence represent a
k
-subset of
\mathcal{V}
.

In particular, in any match of
w_{\mathcal{V}}
with
\psi_{\text{pick}}
, the string stored in each variable
x_{i}
is a vertex in
V
.
Hereafter, we write
v_{x_{i}}
to denote the vertex captured by
x_{i}
.

We next define the following two expressions for each
i\in\{1,\dots,k\}
:

\psi_{\text{itself},i}:=\langle\mathord{\backslash x_{i}}\rangle\,\psi_{\text{skip}}\,\langle/\mathord{\backslash x_{i}}\rangle,\qquad\psi_{\text{neigh},i}:=\langle\psi_{\mathcal{V}}\rangle\,\psi_{\text{skip}}\,\mathord{\backslash x_{i}}\,\#\,\psi_{\text{skip}}\,\langle/\psi_{\mathcal{V}}\rangle.

To explain these two expressions,
suppose that the input head is positioned at the first symbol of
\alpha(v_{i})
for some vertex
v_{i}
within
w_{\mathcal{E}}=\cdots\natural\ \alpha(v_{i})\ \natural\cdots
.
Applying
\psi_{\text{itself},j}
in this situation succeeds iff
v_{i}=v_{x_{j}}
; otherwise the match fails.
Similarly, applying
\psi_{\text{neigh},j}
succeeds iff
v_{x_{j}}
is adjacent to
v_{i}
.

We formalize the above argument for
\psi_{\text{itself}}
and
\psi_{\text{neigh}}
as the following proposition.

Proposition 23
.

Let
i\in\{1,\dots,k\}
and
v\in\mathcal{V}
.
Let
u_{i}
be the vertex stored in the variable
x_{i}
.
Then,

•

\alpha(v)
matches
\psi_{\text{itself},i}
if and only if
u_{i}=v
, and

•

\alpha(v)
matches
\psi_{\text{neigh},i}
if and only if
(u_{i},v)\in\mathcal{E}
.

Using
\psi_{\text{itself}}
and
\psi_{\text{neigh}}
, we define the following expression
\psi_{\text{dominated}}
, which checks whether the selected vertices

\{v_{x_{1}},v_{x_{2}},\ldots,v_{x_{k}}\}

form a dominating set, when applied to
w_{\mathcal{E}}
:

\psi_{\text{dominated}}:=\left(\,(\psi_{\text{itself},1}+\cdots+\psi_{\text{itself},k}+\psi_{\text{neigh},1}+\cdots+\psi_{\text{neigh},k})\,\natural\right)^{*}~.

We show the following property of
\psi_{\text{dominated}}
.

Proposition 24
.

w_{\mathcal{E}}
matches
\psi_{\text{dominated}}
if and only if
\{v_{x_{1}},\ldots,v_{x_{k}}\}
is a
k
-dominating set of
G
.

Proof.

(
\Rightarrow
)
Assume that
\{v_{x_{1}},\ldots,v_{x_{k}}\}
is a
k
-dominating set of
G
.
Then, for all
v\in\mathcal{V}
,
either
v=v_{x_{i}}
for some
i
or
(v,v_{x_{i}})\in\mathcal{E}
for some
i
.
Therefore, from Proposition
23
, for each
v\in\mathcal{V}
,

\alpha(v)
matches

\psi_{\text{itself},1}+\cdots+\psi_{\text{itself},k}+\psi_{\text{neigh},1}+\cdots+\psi_{\text{neigh},k}
, which is a subexpression of
\psi_{\text{dominated}}
.
Combining such matching for all
v\in\mathcal{V}
yields a matching of
w_{\mathcal{E}}
to
\psi_{\text{dominated}}
.

(
\Leftarrow
)
Conversely, assume
w_{\mathcal{E}}
matches
\psi_{\text{dominated}}
.
The assumption means that,
for each
v\in\mathcal{V}
, the string
\alpha(v)
in
w_{\mathcal{E}}
is consumed by
\psi_{\text{itself},i}
or
\psi_{\text{neigh},i}
for some
i
.
From Proposition
23
,
v=v_{x_{i}}
holds if
\alpha(v)
matches
\psi_{\text{itself},i}
and

(v,v_{x_{i}})\in\mathcal{E}
holds if
\alpha(v)
matches
\psi_{\text{neigh},i}
.
Since it holds for all
v\in\mathcal{V}
,
\{v_{x_{1}},\ldots,v_{x_{k}}\}
is a
k
-dominating set of
G
.
∎

Finally, we combine
\psi_{\text{pick}}
and
\psi_{\text{dominated}}
and obtain our main expression
\Psi
as follows:

\Psi_{k}:=\psi_{\text{pick}}\ @\ \psi_{\text{dominated}}.

Note that
\Psi_{k}
is a fixed
R
EWB expression depending only on
k
.
The following property of
\Psi
clearly holds.

Proposition 25
.

In any match of
\widetilde{G}
against
\Psi_{k}
,
the prefix
w_{\mathcal{V}}
is matched by
\psi_{\text{pick}}

and the suffix
w_{\mathcal{E}}
is matched by
\psi_{\text{dominated}}
.

Proof.

The string
\widetilde{G}=w_{\mathcal{V}}\,@\,w_{\mathcal{E}}
contains exactly one occurrence of
@
.
Hence, in any match with
\Psi_{k}
,
this symbol must be consumed by the
@
in
\Psi_{k}
,
which separates the part matched by
\psi_{\text{pick}}
from the part matched by
\psi_{\text{dominated}}
.
∎

Combining Propositions
22
,
24
, and
25
directly yields the following property, which concludes Lemma
21
:

G
has a
k
-dominating set iff
\widetilde{G}
matches the
k
-
R
EWB
\Psi_{k}
(equivalently,
\widetilde{G}\in\mathbb{L}\left(\Psi_{k}\right)
).

3.2
Hardness for
2
-use
2
-
R
EWB: Reduction from Triangle Detection Problem

We prove Theorem
4
by reduction from the following triangle detection problem.

†
†
itembox-title:
Triangle Detection Problem
Input
: An undirected graph
G=(\mathcal{V},\mathcal{E})
with
n:=|\mathcal{V}|
vertices and
m:=|\mathcal{E}|
edges.

Task
: Deciding whether
G
contains a triangle, that is, whether there is a set of three distinct vertices
\{a,b,c\}
with
(a,b),(b,c),(c,a)\in\mathcal{E}
.

The following is conjectured about the triangle detection problem.

Hypothesis

(
No-Almost-Linear-Time Hypothesis

[
1
,
2
]
)
.

There is a constant
\delta>0
,
such that in the Word RAM model with words of
O(\log n)
bits,
any algorithm requires
m^{1+\delta-o(1)}
time in expectation to detect whether a graph with
m
edges graph contains a triangle.

In this section, our main task is giving the following reduction from the triangle detection problem to the
2
-use
2
-
R
EWB matching problem.

Lemma 26

(Reduction from Triangle Detection Problem to
2
-use
2
-
R
EWB Matching Problem)
.

There exists a fixed
2
-use
2
-
R
EWB expression
\Psi
that solves the triangle detection problem in the following sense:

Let
G=(\mathcal{V},\mathcal{E})
be an undirected graph with
n
vertices and
m
edges.
There exists a string
\widetilde{G}
such that the following holds:

\widehat{G}\in\mathbb{L}\left(\Psi\right)\iff\text{ there is a triangle in $G$}.

In particular, we can construct the string
\widehat{G}
in
O(m\log n)
time, and the size of
\widehat{G}
is
|\widehat{G}|=O(m\log n)
.

We first show our hardness result from Lemma
26
.
While the proof follows a standard reduction argument, we provide the details below for completeness.

See
4

Proof.

Assume the No-Almost-Linear-Time Hypothesis holds.
Let
\delta_{\Delta}>0
be the constant specified in the hypothesis; that is, any algorithm requires time
m^{1+\delta_{\Delta}-o(1)}
.

Let
\Psi
be the fixed
2
-use
2
-
R
EWB expression from Lemma
21
.
For every graph
G
with
n
vertices and
m
edges, we can construct
\widehat{G}
such that
\widehat{G}\in\mathbb{L}(\Psi)\iff G\text{ has a triangle}
.
We construct a triangle detection algorithm
\mathcal{T}
as follows: Given
G
, remove isolated vertices so that
n\leq 2m
, construct
\widehat{G}
, and run the matching algorithm
\mathcal{A}
on
\widehat{G}
.

Suppose for contradiction that there is an algorithm
\mathcal{A}
for
\Psi
running in time
|w|^{1+\delta-o(1)}
for every
\delta>0
.
Specifically, fix
\gamma:=\delta_{\Delta}/2
and assume
\mathcal{A}
runs in time
O(|w|^{1+\gamma})
.

The construction of
\widehat{G}
takes
O(m\log n)
time and yields
|\widehat{G}|=O(m\log n)
. Since
n\leq 2m
, we have
|\widehat{G}|=O(m\log m)
.
Running
\mathcal{A}
on
\widehat{G}
takes the following time
T_{\mathcal{A}}(\widehat{G})
:

T_{\mathcal{A}}(\widehat{G})\leq O\left((m\log m)^{1+\gamma}\right)=O\left(m^{1+\gamma}(\log m)^{1+\gamma}\right).

Since
(\log m)^{1+\gamma}=m^{o(1)}
, the total running time of
\mathcal{T}
is:

T_{\mathcal{T}}(G)=O(m\log m)+m^{1+\gamma+o(1)}=m^{1+\gamma+o(1)}.

Substituting
\gamma=\delta_{\Delta}/2
, we get:

T_{\mathcal{T}}(G)=m^{1+\frac{\delta_{\Delta}}{2}+o(1)}.

For sufficiently large
m
,
1+\frac{\delta_{\Delta}}{2}+o(1)<1+\delta_{\Delta}-o(1)
.
Thus,
\mathcal{T}
solves the triangle detection problem strictly faster than the hypothesized lower bound
m^{1+\delta_{\Delta}-o(1)}
, which is a contradiction.
∎

3.2.1
Proof of Lemma
26

We here encode the given graph
G
into the following string
\widehat{G}
over an alphabet
\Sigma=\{0,1,\#,\natural,@,\langle,\rangle,/\}
:

\widehat{G}:=w_{\mathcal{E}}\ @\ w_{\mathcal{E}}~,

where the string representation of the adjacency lists of
G
,
w_{\mathcal{E}}
, is defined in the previous section.
Therefore, it is clear that the size of
|\widehat{G}|
satisfies
|\widehat{G}|=O(m\log n)
where
n
and
m
are the number of vertices and edges of
G
, respectively.
Moreover, we can construct
\widehat{G}
in
O(m\log n)
time.

If
G
has a triangle
\{a,b,c\}
,
then the following pattern must appear in
\widehat{G}
:

\cdots\langle a\rangle\cdots\#b\#\cdots\#c\#\cdots\langle/a\rangle\cdots\ @\cdots\langle b\rangle\cdots\#c\#\cdots\langle/b\rangle\cdots~~.

Conversely, if this pattern appears in
\widehat{G}
, then
G
has a triangle
\{a,b,c\}
.

We now prepare two expressions
\varphi_{\text{pick}}
and
\varphi_{\text{check}}
where

•

\varphi_{\text{pick}}
nondeterministically chooses one vertex
v
and selects two neighbors and save them to the variables
x
and
y
.

•

\varphi_{\text{find}}
searches the adjacency list of the vertex stored in the variable
x
and then searches the vertex stored in the variable
y
.

We first define
\varphi_{\text{pick}}
as follows:

\varphi_{\text{pick}}:=\Upsilon^{*}\>\langle\psi_{\mathcal{V}}\rangle\ \psi_{\text{skip}}\ (\psi_{\mathcal{V}})_{x}\ \#\ \psi_{\text{skip}}\ (\psi_{\mathcal{V}})_{y}\ \#\ \psi_{\text{skip}}\ \langle/\psi_{\mathcal{V}}\rangle\ \Upsilon^{*}\>@

where

\psi_{\mathcal{V}}:=(0+1)^{+}
,
\psi_{\text{skip}}:=(\psi_{\mathcal{V}}\,\#)^{*}
(as with Section
3.1
),
and
\Upsilon:=\Sigma\setminus\{@\}
.

We explain how this expression
\varphi_{\text{pick}}
nondeterministically selects and saves two vertices in an adjacency list step-by-step.
The part
\Upsilon^{*}\,\langle\psi_{\mathcal{V}}\rangle
nondeterministically consumes a prefix and proceeds to the following part

\displaystyle\qquad\overbracket{\cdots}^{\Upsilon^{*}}\overbracket{\langle a\rangle}^{\langle\psi_{\mathcal{V}}\rangle}\cdots\#b\#\cdots\#c\#\cdots\langle/a\rangle\cdots\ @\cdots\langle b\rangle\cdots\#c\#\cdots\langle/b\rangle\cdots~~.

Then the following subexpression
\psi_{\text{skip}}
can consume the substring before
b
as follows:

\displaystyle\qquad\overbracket{\cdots}^{\Upsilon^{*}}\,\overbracket{\langle a\rangle}^{\langle\psi_{\mathcal{V}}\rangle}\ \overbracket{\cdots\#}^{\psi_{\text{skip}}}\ b\#\cdots\#c\#\cdots\langle/a\rangle\cdots\ @\cdots\langle b\rangle\cdots\#c\#\cdots\langle/b\rangle\cdots~~.

Now the subexpression
(\psi_{\mathcal{V}})_{x}\#
captures
b
to
x
successfully:

\displaystyle\qquad\overbracket{\cdots}^{\Upsilon^{*}}\overbracket{\langle a\rangle}^{\langle\psi_{\mathcal{V}}\rangle}\ \overbracket{\cdots\#}^{\psi_{\text{skip}}}\ \overbracket{\,b\,}^{(\psi_{\mathcal{V}})_{x}}\overbracket{\#}^{\#}\cdots\#c\#\cdots\langle/a\rangle\cdots\ @\cdots\langle b\rangle\cdots\#c\#\cdots\langle/b\rangle\cdots~~.

Similarly, the subexpression
\psi_{\text{skip}}\,(\psi_{\mathcal{V}})_{y}\,\#\psi_{\text{skip}}\,\langle/\psi_{\mathcal{V}}\rangle
can capture the vertex
c
to
y
and successfully reach to the end of the adjacency list
\langle/a\rangle
as follows:

\displaystyle\qquad\overbracket{\cdots}^{\Upsilon^{*}}\overbracket{\langle a\rangle}^{\langle\psi_{\mathcal{V}}\rangle}\ \overbracket{\cdots\#}^{\psi_{\text{skip}}}\ \overbracket{\,b\,}^{(\psi_{\mathcal{V}})_{x}}\ \overbracket{\#}^{\#}\ \overbracket{\cdots\#}^{\psi_{\text{skip}}}\ \overbracket{\,c\,}^{(\psi_{\mathcal{V}})_{y}}\overbracket{\#}^{\#}\ \overbracket{\cdots}^{\psi_{\text{skip}}}\ \overbracket{\langle/a\rangle}^{\langle/\psi_{\mathcal{V}}\rangle}\cdots\ @\cdots\langle b\rangle\cdots\#c\#\cdots\langle/b\rangle\cdots~~.

Finally, the part
\Upsilon^{*}@
successfully consumes the first
w_{\mathcal{E}}
as follows:

\displaystyle\qquad\overbracket{\cdots}^{\Upsilon^{*}}\overbracket{\langle a\rangle}^{\langle\psi_{\mathcal{V}}\rangle}\ \overbracket{\cdots\#}^{\psi_{\text{skip}}}\ \overbracket{\,b\,}^{(\psi_{\mathcal{V}})_{x}}\ \overbracket{\#}^{\#}\ \overbracket{\cdots\#}^{\psi_{\text{skip}}}\ \overbracket{\,c\,}^{(\psi_{\mathcal{V}})_{y}}\overbracket{\#}^{\#}\ \overbracket{\cdots}^{\psi_{\text{skip}}}\ \overbracket{\langle/a\rangle}^{\langle/\psi_{\mathcal{V}}\rangle}\overbracket{\cdots}^{\Upsilon^{*}}\ \overbracket{@}^{@}\cdots\langle b\rangle\cdots\#c\#\cdots\langle/b\rangle\cdots~~.

\Upsilon^{*}
also never goes beyond
@
because
@\notin\Upsilon
.

This argument immediately leads to the following proposition about
\varphi_{\text{pick}}
.

Proposition 27
.

Applying
\varphi_{\text{pick}}
to
\widehat{G}=w_{\mathcal{E}}\,@\,w_{\mathcal{E}}
,
it consumes the prefix including
@
(i.e.,
w_{\mathcal{E}}@
)
and save two vertices
b
and
c
to
x
and
y
, respectively, where they are neighbors of some vertex
a
: that is,
a{-}b
and
a{-}c
.
Moreover,
\varphi_{\text{pick}}
can select all such triples
(a,b,c)
.

Our remaining task is designing a subexpression that can find the following pattern when
b
and
c
are stored in the variables:

\cdots\langle b\rangle\cdots\#c\#\cdots\langle/b\rangle\cdots~~~.

To this end, we define the following expression
\varphi_{\text{find}}
:

\varphi_{\text{find}}:=\Upsilon^{*}\ \langle\mathord{\backslash x}\rangle\ \psi_{\text{skip}}\ \mathord{\backslash y}\ \#\ \Sigma^{*}~.

We apply this expression to the rest of the input consumed by
\varphi_{\text{pick}}
.
The first part
\Upsilon^{*}\langle\mathord{\backslash x}\rangle
successfully finds the adjacency list of the vertex stored in the variable
x
as follows:

\displaystyle\qquad\overbracket{\cdots}^{\Upsilon^{*}}\overbracket{\langle a\rangle}^{\langle\psi_{\mathcal{V}}\rangle}\ \overbracket{\cdots\#}^{\psi_{\text{skip}}}\ \overbracket{\,b\,}^{(\psi_{\mathcal{V}})_{x}}\ \overbracket{\#}^{\#}\ \overbracket{\cdots\#}^{\psi_{\text{skip}}}\ \overbracket{\,c\,}^{(\psi_{\mathcal{V}})_{y}}\ \overbracket{\#}^{\#}\ \overbracket{\cdots}^{\psi_{\text{skip}}}\ \overbracket{\langle/a\rangle}^{\langle/\psi_{\mathcal{V}}\rangle}\ \overbracket{\cdots}^{\Upsilon^{*}}\ \overbracket{@}^{@}~~~\underbracket{\cdots}_{\Upsilon^{*}}\underbracket{\langle b\rangle}_{\langle\mathord{\backslash x}\rangle}\cdots\#c\#\cdots\langle/b\rangle\cdots~~

where the variable
x
now has the vertex
b
.
After entering the adjacency list of
b
,
the part
\psi_{\text{skip}}\ \mathord{\backslash y}\ \#
successfully finds the neighbor stored in the variable
y
as follows:

\displaystyle\qquad\overbracket{\cdots}^{\Upsilon^{*}}\overbracket{\langle a\rangle}^{\langle\psi_{\mathcal{V}}\rangle}\ \overbracket{\cdots\#}^{\psi_{\text{skip}}}\ \overbracket{\,b\,}^{(\psi_{\mathcal{V}})_{x}}\ \overbracket{\#}^{\#}\ \overbracket{\cdots\#}^{\psi_{\text{skip}}}\ \overbracket{\,c\,}^{(\psi_{\mathcal{V}})_{y}}\ \overbracket{\#}^{\#}\ \overbracket{\cdots}^{\psi_{\text{skip}}}\ \overbracket{\langle/a\rangle}^{\langle/\psi_{\mathcal{V}}\rangle}\ \overbracket{\cdots}^{\Upsilon^{*}}\ \overbracket{@}^{@}~~~\underbracket{\cdots}_{\Upsilon^{*}}\underbracket{\langle b\rangle}_{\langle\mathord{\backslash x}\rangle}\underbracket{\cdots\#}_{\psi_{\text{skip}}}\ \underbracket{~c~}_{\mathord{\backslash y}}\underbracket{\#}_{\#}\cdots\langle/b\rangle\cdots~~

The success of applying the last part means that we find the triangle
\{a,b,c\}
; thus, we finally consume the rest of the input by
\Sigma^{*}
as follows:

\displaystyle\qquad\overbracket{\cdots}^{\Upsilon^{*}}\overbracket{\langle a\rangle}^{\langle\psi_{\mathcal{V}}\rangle}\ \overbracket{\cdots\#}^{\psi_{\text{skip}}}\ \overbracket{\,b\,}^{(\psi_{\mathcal{V}})_{x}}\ \overbracket{\#}^{\#}\ \overbracket{\cdots\#}^{\psi_{\text{skip}}}\ \overbracket{\,c\,}^{(\psi_{\mathcal{V}})_{y}}\ \overbracket{\#}^{\#}\ \overbracket{\cdots}^{\psi_{\text{skip}}}\ \overbracket{\langle/a\rangle}^{\langle/\psi_{\mathcal{V}}\rangle}\ \overbracket{\cdots}^{\Upsilon^{*}}\ \overbracket{@}^{@}~~~\underbracket{\cdots}_{\Upsilon^{*}}\underbracket{\langle b\rangle}_{\langle\mathord{\backslash x}\rangle}\underbracket{\cdots\#}_{\psi_{\text{skip}}}\ \underbracket{~c~}_{\mathord{\backslash y}}\underbracket{\#}_{\#}\underbracket{\cdots\langle/b\rangle\cdots}_{\Sigma^{*}}~.

This argument immediately leads to the following proposition about
\varphi_{\text{find}}
.

Proposition 28
.

Assume that the variable
x
and
y
stores a vertex
b
and
c
, respectively.
On the application of
\varphi_{\text{find}}
to
w_{\mathcal{E}}
,
the following conditions are equivalent:

•

the application results in success with consuming all the input

•

the vertices
b
and
c
are neighbors

Combining
\varphi_{\text{pick}}
and
\varphi_{\text{find}}
, we define our expression
\Psi
as follows:

\Psi:=\varphi_{\text{pick}}\ \varphi_{\text{find}}.

Propositions
27
and
28
lead to our main property, which concludes Lemma
26
:

w_{\mathcal{E}}\ @\ w_{\mathcal{E}}\in\mathbb{L}\left(\Psi\right)\iff\text{there is a triangle in $G$}.

3.3
Hardness from
k
-Orthogonal Vectors Problem Hypothesis

We here show our
O(n^{2q-\epsilon})
-time hardness for the
q
-
R
EWB matching problem where
n
is the length of input strings under
S
ETH, or more technically, under the
k
-orthogonal vectors (
k
-OV
) Hypothesis
[
38
,
107
]
, which is weaker than
S
ETH and indeed is implied by
S
ETH.
To use
k
for the
k
-OV
and its hypothesis, we use “
q
” to denote the number of variables of
R
EWB in this section.

Let
v_{1},v_{2},\ldots,v_{k}\in\{0,1\}^{d}
be
d
-dimensional 0-1 vectors over the ring
\mathbb{Z}
.
The
k
inner-product is defined as follows:

\mathbf{IP}_{k}(v_{1},v_{2},\ldots,v_{k}):=\sum^{d}_{i=1}\left(\ \prod^{k}_{j=1}v_{j}[i]\,\right).

These vectors are
k
-orthogonal
if
\mathbf{IP}_{k}(v_{1},v_{2},\ldots,v_{k})=0
.
In our construction, we use the following equivalent condition:

\displaystyle\bigwedge^{d}_{i=1}\bigvee^{k}_{j=1}(v_{j}[i]=0)
.

We now consider the
k
-OV
and the
k
-OV
Hypothesis.

†
†
itembox-title:
\textbf{$k$-OV}_{N,d}
Problem
Input:

k
sets
A_{1},\ldots,A_{k}\subseteq\{0,1\}^{d}

where each set
A_{i}
has size
|A_{i}|=N
.

Task:

Deciding if the following holds:

\exists v_{1}\in A_{1}.\,\exists\>v_{2}\in A_{2}.\>\ldots\>\exists v_{k}\in A_{k}.\ \mathbf{IP}_{k}(v_{1},v_{2},\ldots,v_{k})=0.

Hypothesis

(
k
-OV
Hypothesis
[
38
, Hypothesis 2]
,
[
107
, Hypothesis 4]
)
.

Let
k\geq 2
be an integer.
For any
\epsilon>0
and
every dimension function
d(N)=\omega(\log N)

(e.g.,
d(N)=\lceil\log^{2}N\rceil
),

\textbf{$k$-OV}_{\!N,\,d(N)}
cannot be solved in
O(N^{k-\epsilon})
time.

Remark:
It is known that
k
-OV
Hypothesis is weaker than
S
ETH. Actually,
S
ETH implies
k
-OV
Hypothesis
[
107
, Theorem 3.1]

[
38
,
106
]

We provide an efficient reduction from
2k\text{-}\textbf{OV}
problem to
k
-
R
EWB matching problem.

Lemma 29
.

Let
k\geq 1
be an integer.
There exists a (fixed)
k
-
R
EWB
\Psi_{k}
that solves
2k\text{-}\textbf{OV}
in the following sense:
Let
N\geq 1
be an integer and
A_{1},\ldots,A_{2k}\subseteq\{0,1\}^{d}
be an instance of
2k
-
OV
with
|A_{1}|=\cdots=|A_{2k}|=N
.
There is a string
\mathcal{S}(A_{1},\ldots,A_{2k})
such that

\mathcal{S}(A_{1},\ldots,A_{2k})\in\mathbb{L}\left(\Psi_{k}\right)\iff\exists v_{1}\in A_{1}.\exists v_{2}\in A_{2}.\ \cdots.\exists v_{2k}\in A_{2k}.\ \mathbf{IP}_{2k}(v_{1},v_{2},\ldots,v_{2k})=0.

In particular, we can construct
\mathcal{S}(A_{1},\ldots,A_{2k})
in
O(k\cdot N\cdot d^{2})
time.

Before providing the proof of this lemma (shown in Section
3.3.1
), we here prove Theorem
5
using this lemma.
While the proof follows a standard reduction argument, we provide the details below for completeness.

Theorem

(Reformulation of Theorem
5
)
.

The

q
-
R
EWB matching
problem cannot be solved in
O(|w|^{2q-\epsilon})
time for any
\epsilon>0

under
k
-OV
Hypothesis.

Proof.

We proceed by contradiction.
Suppose there exists an algorithm
\mathcal{A}
solving the problem in time
O(|w|^{2q-\epsilon})
for some constant
\epsilon>0
.
We construct an efficient algorithm to solve
2q\text{-}\textbf{OV}_{\!N,\,d}
as follows:

1.

Given an instance of
2q\text{-}\textbf{OV}_{\!N,\,d}
consisting of
2q
sets
\{A_{1},A_{2},\ldots,A_{2q}\}
of
N
vectors in
\{0,1\}^{d}
, we first transform it into a string
\mathcal{S}:=\mathcal{S}(A_{1},\ldots,A_{2q})
by our reduction.
By the assumption, this reduction takes
T(N)=O(Nd^{2})
time, and
n_{\mathcal{S}}:=|\mathcal{S}|\leq c\cdot Nd^{2}
for some constant
c
depending on
q
.

2.

Next, we run the algorithm
\mathcal{A}
on
\mathcal{S}
. The running time is

T_{\mathcal{A}}(n_{\mathcal{S}})=O(n_{\mathcal{S}}^{2q-\epsilon})=O\bigl((Nd^{2})^{2q-\epsilon}\bigr).

Fix the dimension function
d(N):=\lceil\,(\log N)^{2}\,\rceil
.
Since
d(N)=\omega(\log N)
holds, the
k
-OV
Hypothesis (with
k=2q
) applies also to
2q\text{-}\textbf{OV}_{\!N,\,d(N)}
.

By Step 2 we obtain a
2q\text{-}\textbf{OV}
algorithm with running time :

T_{\text{total}}(N)=O(Nd^{2})+O\big((Nd^{2})^{2q-\epsilon}\big)=O(Nd^{2})+O\big(N^{2q-\epsilon}\cdot d^{4q-2\epsilon}\big)\quad\text{where}\ d=d(N).

Since
d=\lceil\,(\log N)^{2}\,\rceil
,
we have

d^{4q-2\epsilon}\leq\bigl((\log{N})^{2}+1\bigr)^{4q-2\epsilon}=(\log{N})^{O(1)}=N^{o(1)}
.
Thus for any
\delta>0
and sufficiently large
N
,

d^{4q-2\varepsilon}\leq N^{\delta}.

Choose
\delta=\epsilon/2
.
Then

T_{\text{total}}(N)=O(N^{1+o(1)})+O(N^{(2q-\epsilon)+(\epsilon/2)})=O(N^{2q-\epsilon/2}),

contradicting the
k
-OV
Hypothesis with
k=2q
.

∎

3.3.1
Proof of Lemma
29

To explain our idea, we first give a reduction from
2\text{-}\textbf{OV}
to
1
-
R
EWB matching problem.
The reduction can be easily extended to a reduction from
2k\text{-}\textbf{OV}
to
k
-
R
EWB.

Lemma 30

(Reduction for
k=1
)
.

There exists a (fixed)
1
-
R
EWB
\Psi_{1}
that solves
2\text{-}\textbf{OV}
in the following sense:
Let
N\geq 1
be an integer and
A_{1},A_{2}\subseteq\{0,1\}^{d}
be an instance of
2
-
OV
with
|A_{1}|=|A_{2}|=N
.
There is a string
\mathcal{S}(A_{1},A_{2})
such that

\mathcal{S}(A_{1},A_{2})\in\mathbb{L}\left(\Psi_{1}\right)\iff\exists v_{1}\in A_{1}.\exists v_{2}\in A_{2}.\ \mathbf{IP}(v_{1},v_{2})=0.

In particular, we can construct
\mathcal{S}(A_{1},A_{2})
in
O(N\cdot d^{2})
time.

Proof.

We note that the proof presented here can be viewed as the
R
EWB-version of a similar result and construction for 1-turn bounded-height (
\leq d
) pushdown automata
[
60
, Theorem 4]
.

Let
A_{1}=\{v_{1},v_{2},\ldots,v_{N}\}
and

A_{2}=\{u_{1},u_{2},\ldots,u_{N}\}
.
We set
\Sigma:=\{0,1,\#,@,\natural\}
and
define the following string
\mathcal{I}
, which serves as the arena for our construction:

\mathcal{I}:=\#\,\overleftarrow{v_{1}}\,\#\,\overleftarrow{v_{2}}\,\#\ \cdots\#\,\overleftarrow{v_{N}}\,\#@\#\,u_{1}\,\#\,u_{2}\,\#\cdots\#\,u_{N}\,\#\,\natural

where
\overleftarrow{v}
means the reversed version of
v
: i.e.,
\overleftarrow{11001}=10011
.
It should be noted that
|\mathcal{I}|=O(d\cdot N)
.

A key of our reduction is representing a pair of vectors
(v_{i},u_{j})
with
1\leq i,j\leq N
by a
single
substring of
\mathcal{I}
.
A substring
\pi
of
\mathcal{I}

represents

(v_{i},u_{j})
if the following holds:

\mathcal{I}=\cdots\#\,\overleftarrow{v_{i}}\ \overbracket{\#\cdots\#\,@\,\#\cdots\#}^{\text{\large$\pi$}}\ u_{j}\,\#\cdots\natural.

More formally,
let
\pi=\pi(i,j)
be the factor (substring) of
\mathcal{I}
that starts right after the occurrence of
\overleftarrow{v_{i}}
and ends right before the occurrence of
u_{j}
.
Explicitly given by:

\pi(i,j):=(\#\overleftarrow{v_{i+1}}\#\cdots\#\overleftarrow{v_{N}})\,\#@\#\,(u_{1}\#\cdots\#u_{j-1}\#)

If
i=N
, the left part is empty; if
j=1
, the right part is empty.

For example,
if
A_{1}=\{v_{1}=011,v_{2}=001\}
and
A_{2}=\{u_{1}=101,u_{2}=100\}
, then our string
\mathcal{I}
takes the following form:

\mathcal{I}=\#\,110\,\#\,100\,\#\,@\,\#\,101\,\#\,100\,\#\natural.

In this setting, the substring
\pi=\#\,100\,\#\,@\,\#\,101\,\#
represents
(v_{1},u_{2})
because
\mathcal{I}=v_{1}\,\pi\,w_{2}
.
Below we write
\tilde{\mathcal{I}}
to denote
\mathcal{I}
for this example.

We define the following building block expression
\psi
and
\phi_{1},\phi_{2}
where

•

we use
\psi
by applying it to a copy of
\mathcal{I}
to nondeterministically select and represent vectors
v_{*}
from
A_{1}
and
u_{*}
from
A_{2}
; and

•

we use
\phi_{1}
(resp.
\phi_{2}
) by applying it to a copy of
\mathcal{I}
to check
v_{*}[\ell]=0
(resp.
u_{*}[\ell]=0
) for each index
\ell\in[1{\!\>..\!\>}d]
.

We first define our expression
\psi
as follows:

\psi:=\Gamma^{*}\,(\#\,\Gamma^{*}\,@\,\Gamma^{*}\#)_{x}\,\Gamma^{*}\,\natural

where
\Gamma:=(0+1+\#)
is the
R
egex.
We note that
\Gamma
cannot consume the symbols
@
and
\natural
.

We apply
\psi
to
\mathcal{I}
to nondeterministically select vectors
v_{*}
from
A_{1}
and
u_{*}
from
A_{2}
.
For our example
A_{1}=\{v_{1}=011,v_{2}=001\}
and
A_{2}=\{u_{1}=101,u_{2}=100\}
,
applying
\psi
to
\tilde{\mathcal{I}}
can generate the following match, in which
v_{*}=v_{1}
and
u_{*}=u_{2}
are selected:

\overbracket{\#110}^{\Gamma^{*}}\ \overbracket{\#100\,\#@\#\,101\#}^{(\#\Gamma^{*}@\Gamma^{*}\#)_{x}}\ \overbracket{100\#\natural}^{\Gamma^{*}\natural}\quad\text{where $x$ has $\#\,100\,\#@\#\,101\,\#$.}

Another run can select
v_{*}=v_{2}
and
u_{*}=u_{1}
as follows:

\overbracket{\#\,110\,\#\,100}^{\Gamma^{*}}\ \overbracket{\#@\#}^{(\#\Gamma^{*}@\Gamma^{*}\#)_{x}}\ \overbracket{101\,\#\,100\#\natural}^{\Gamma^{*}\natural}.\quad\text{where $x$ has $\#\,@\,\#$}.

By the above observation, the following is clear.

Proposition 31
.

Applying
\psi
to
\mathcal{I}
,
it completely consumes
\mathcal{I}
and saves a substring into the variable
x
, which represents
(v_{i},u_{j})
for some
1\leq i,j\leq N
.
Moreover, any pair
(v_{i},u_{j})
can be captured and represented via
x
by applying
\psi
to
\mathcal{I}
.

Next we define our expressions
\varphi_{v}
and
\varphi_{u}
as follows:

\varphi_{v}:=\Gamma^{*}\#\mathcal{B}^{*}\,(0\,\mathord{\backslash x}\,\mathcal{B})_{x}\,\mathcal{B}^{*}\#\Gamma^{*}\,\natural,\qquad\varphi_{u}:=\Gamma^{*}\#\mathcal{B}^{*}\,(\mathcal{B}\,\mathord{\backslash x}\,0)_{x}\,\mathcal{B}^{*}\#\Gamma^{*}\,\natural.

where
\mathcal{B}:=(0+1)
.

The former
\varphi_{v}
requires
v_{*}[\ell]=0
and the latter
\varphi_{u}
requires
u_{*}[\ell]=0
for some index
\ell\in[1{\!\>..\!\>}d]
.
For example,
applying
\varphi_{v}
to another copy of
\tilde{\mathcal{I}}
under
x=\#100@\#101\#
,
it works as follows:

\overbracket{\phantom{01}}^{\Gamma^{*}}\ \overbracket{\#}^{\#}\ \overbracket{11}^{\mathcal{B}^{*}}\ \overbracket{0\,\#100\,\#@\#\,101\#\,1}^{(0\,\mathord{\backslash x}\,\mathcal{B})_{x}}\ \overbracket{00}^{\mathcal{B}^{*}}\ \overbracket{\#}^{\#}\ \overbracket{\,\natural\,}^{\Gamma^{*}\natural}.

This application also updates
x
as follows:

x=\#100\,\#@\#\,101\#\leadsto 0\,\#100\,\#@\#\,101\#\,1~.

Since one application of
\varphi_{v}
and
\varphi_{u}
extends
x
with a single-letter,
we can check
\displaystyle\bigwedge^{d}_{\ell=1}(v_{*}[\ell]=0\lor u_{*}[\ell]=0)
by repeatedly applying the expression
(\varphi_{v}+\varphi_{u})
.
For example, applying
\varphi_{u}
to another copy of
\tilde{\mathcal{I}}
under the updated
x
,
it checks if
w_{*}[2]=0
and updates
x
as follows:

\overbracket{\phantom{01}}^{\Gamma^{*}}\ \overbracket{\#}^{\#}\ \overbracket{1}^{\mathcal{B}^{*}}\ \overbracket{1\,0\#100\,\#@\#\,101\#1\,0}^{(\mathcal{B}\,\mathord{\backslash x}\,0)_{x}}\ \overbracket{0}^{\mathcal{B}^{*}}\ \overbracket{\#}^{\#}\ \overbracket{\,\natural\,}^{\Gamma^{*}\natural},\quad x=0\#100\,\#@\#\,101\#1\leadsto 1\,0\#100\,\#@\#\,101\#1\,0~.

By the above observation, the following is clear.

Proposition 32
.

Assume the content of
x
represents
v_{i}[\ell]
and
u_{j}[\ell]
for some
1\leq\ell\leq d
.
Then, applying
(\varphi_{v}+\varphi_{u})
to
\mathcal{I}
, the following holds:
if the application succeeds, then
v_{i}[\ell]\cdot u_{j}[\ell]=0
and
x
is updated to represent
v_{i}[\ell+1]
and
u_{j}[\ell+1]
(when
\ell\leq d
).

In summary, we employ the following gadgets:

\mathcal{S}(A_{1},A_{2}):=\overbrace{\mathcal{I}\,\mathcal{I}\,\cdots\,\mathcal{I}}^{\text{$(1+d)${-}copies}},\qquad\Psi_{1}:=\psi\ (\varphi_{v}+\varphi_{u})^{*}

It should be noted that the expression
\Psi_{1}
does not depend on the instance
A_{1}
and
A_{2}
; this
\Psi_{1}
can be used for any
2\text{-}\textbf{OV}
instances.

By the above argument,
it is clear that
\mathcal{S}(A_{1},A_{2})\in\mathbb{L}\left(\Psi_{1}\right)
iff there are orthogonal vectors in
A_{1}
and
A_{2}
.
We note that
|\mathcal{S}(A_{1},A_{2})|=O(N\cdot d^{2})
because
|\mathcal{I}|=O(N\cdot d)
, and
\mathcal{S}(A_{1},A_{2})
can be constructed in
O(N\cdot d^{2})
time.
∎

We can generalize the above reduction to one from
2k\text{-}\textbf{OV}
to
k
-
R
EWB.

See
29

Proof.

We extend the construction for Lemma
30
to the case
k\geq 2
.
We represent
2\cdot k
vectors using
k
variables
x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{k}
.
We assume the following representation:

\begin{array}[]{l}A_{1}=\{v^{1}_{1},\ldots,v^{1}_{N}\},\ A_{2}=\{u^{1}_{1},\ldots,u^{1}_{N}\},\\[5.0pt]
A_{3}=\{v^{2}_{1},\ldots,v^{2}_{N}\},\ A_{4}=\{u^{2}_{1},\ldots,u^{2}_{N}\},\\
~\vdots\\
A_{2k-1}=\{v^{k}_{1},\ldots,v^{k}_{N}\},\ A_{2k}=\{u^{k}_{1},\ldots,u^{k}_{N}\}.\end{array}

We change the definition of
\mathcal{I}
as follows:

\mathcal{I}:=\mathcal{I}_{1}\ \mathcal{I}_{2}\ \cdots\mathcal{I}_{k}\ ,

where

\mathcal{I}_{r}:=\#\,\overleftarrow{v^{r}_{1}}\,\#\cdots\,\#\overleftarrow{v^{r}_{N}}\,\#@\#\,u^{r}_{1}\,\#\cdots\#u^{r}_{N}\#\,\natural\ .

For example, if
k=2
,

\mathcal{I}=\#\overleftarrow{v^{1}_{1}}\#\cdots\#\overleftarrow{v^{1}_{N}}\,\#@\#\,u^{1}_{1}\#\cdots\#u^{1}_{n}\#\natural\ \ \#\overleftarrow{v^{2}_{1}}\#\cdots\#\overleftarrow{v^{2}_{N}}@\#u^{2}_{1}\#\cdots\#u^{2}_{N}\#\natural\ .

We use the variable
x_{r}
(
1\leq r\leq k
) to represent
(v^{r}_{i},u^{r}_{j})
with
1\leq i,j\leq N
.
For example, if
k=2
,
we use
x_{1}
and
x_{2}
as follows:

\#\overleftarrow{v^{1}_{1}}\#\overleftarrow{v^{1}_{2}}\overbracket{\#\cdots\#\overleftarrow{v^{1}_{N}}\,\#@\#}^{x_{1}}u^{1}_{1}\#u^{1}_{2}\#\cdots\#u^{1}_{N}\#\natural\ \ \#\overleftarrow{v^{2}_{1}}\#\overleftarrow{v^{2}_{2}}\overbracket{\#\cdots\#\overleftarrow{v^{2}_{N}}\,\#@\#u^{2}_{1}\#}^{x_{2}}u^{2}_{2}\#\cdots\#u^{2}_{N}\#\natural

To accommodate multiple variables,
we below modify
\psi
,
\varphi_{v}
, and
\varphi_{u}
in Lemma
30
(the case
k=1
).

We first define
\psi
to nondeterministically select vectors from
A_{1},A_{2},\ldots,A_{2k}
and bind them into
x_{1},\ldots x_{k}
as follows:

\psi:=\psi_{1}\psi_{2}\cdots\psi_{k},\qquad\text{ where }\ \psi_{r}:=\Gamma^{*}\,(\#\Gamma^{*}@\Gamma^{*}\#)_{x_{r}}\,\Gamma^{*}\natural,\quad\Gamma:=(0+1+\#).

As with
\psi
in Lemma
30
, the following holds for
\psi
.

Proposition 33
.

Applying
\psi
to
\mathcal{I}
,
it completely consumes
\mathcal{I}
and saves substrings to the variables
x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{k}

where each
x_{r}
represents
(v^{r}_{i},u^{r}_{j})
for some
1\leq i,j\leq N
.
Moreover, for each
1\leq r\leq k
,
any pairs
(v^{r}_{i},u^{r}_{j})
can be captured and represented via
x_{r}
by applying
\psi
to
\mathcal{I}
.

We next define
\varphi_{r}
to check
v_{i}^{r}[\ell]=0
or
u_{i}^{r}[\ell]=0
(
1\leq\ell\leq d
) as follows:

\varphi_{r}:=\varphi_{r}^{1}\ \varphi_{r}^{2}\ \cdots\ \varphi_{r}^{k}\qquad(1\leq r\leq k),

where

\varphi_{r}^{s}:=\begin{cases}\varphi_{s,\top}&(r\neq s),\\
\varphi_{s,v}+\varphi_{s,u}&(r=s),\end{cases}\qquad\begin{array}[]{lcl}\varphi_{s,v}&:=&\Gamma^{*}\#\mathcal{B}^{*}\ (0\,\mathord{\backslash x_{s}}\,\mathcal{B})_{x_{s}}\ \mathcal{B}^{*}\#\Gamma^{*}\,\natural\ ,\\
\varphi_{s,u}&:=&\Gamma^{*}\#\mathcal{B}^{*}\ (\mathcal{B}\,\mathord{\backslash x_{s}}\,0)_{x_{s}}\ \mathcal{B}^{*}\#\Gamma^{*}\,\natural\ ,\\
\varphi_{s,\top}&:=&\Gamma^{*}\#\mathcal{B}^{*}\ (\mathcal{B}\mathord{\backslash x_{s}}\,\mathcal{B})_{x_{s}}\ \mathcal{B}^{*}\#\Gamma^{*}\,\natural,\end{array}\quad\mathcal{B}=(0+1).

Proposition 34
.

Assume that, for an index
\ell\in[1..d]
, every
x_{r}
represents
v^{r}_{i}[\ell]
and
u^{r}_{j}[\ell]
for
1\leq r\leq k
.
If the application of
\varphi_{r}
to
\mathcal{I}
succeeds and consumes
\mathcal{I}
,
then either
v^{r}_{i}[\ell]=0
or
u^{r}_{j}[\ell]=0
holds.
Moreover, every
x_{r}
is updated to represent
v^{r}_{i}[\ell+1]
and
u^{r}_{j}[\ell+1]
(when
\ell\leq d
).

Conversely, if
v^{r}_{i}[\ell]=0
or
u^{r}_{j}[\ell]=0
holds,
then applying
\varphi_{i}
to
\mathcal{I}
must succeed.

Using
\varphi_{i}
, we further define
\varphi
to check the
2k
-orthogonality condition:

\varphi:=\varphi_{1}+\varphi_{2}+\cdots+\varphi_{k}.

We observe that applying
\varphi
to
\mathcal{I}
succeeds iff
\bigl(\prod^{k}_{r=1}(v^{r}_{i}[\ell]\cdot u^{r}_{j}[\ell])\bigr)=0
when each
x_{r}
represents
v_{i}^{r}[\ell]
and
u_{j}^{r}[\ell]
.
Then, we finally define
\mathcal{S}(A_{1},A_{2},\ldots,A_{2k-1},A_{2k})
and
\Psi_{k}
as follows:

\mathcal{S}(A_{1},A_{2},\ldots,A_{2k-1},A_{2k}):=\overbrace{\,\mathcal{I}\ \mathcal{I}\ \ldots\ \mathcal{I}\,}^{(1+d)\text{-copies}},\qquad\Psi_{k}:=\psi\ \varphi^{*}~.

From the above properties, we observe the following, which completes our proof:

\mathcal{S}(A_{1},A_{2},\ldots,A_{2k-1},A_{2k})\in\mathbb{L}\left(\Psi_{k}\right)\iff\text{the $2k$-{OV}{} instance contains $2k$-orthogonal vectors}.

∎

4
Input Normalization

Here, to simplify subsequent arguments, we convert the input to an equivalent, more structured form.
Let
w
be the input string over an alphabet
\Sigma
and
A
,
B
,
C
, and
D
be
R
egexes.

Let
\ell=\lceil\log|\Sigma|\rceil
.
For each
\sigma\in\Sigma
, we assign a unique binary string
\mathrm{bin}(\sigma)
of length
\ell
.
Then, we define the string
\mathrm{san}(w)
by replacing each letter
\sigma
of
w
with the string
\mathrm{bin}(\sigma)\ 0
and appending a letter
1
at the end
(\ell+1)
times; that is,

\mathrm{san}(w)=\underbrace{\mathrm{bin}(w[1])\ 0}_{w[1]}\,\underbrace{\mathrm{bin}(w[2])\ 0}_{w[2]}\,\cdots\,\underbrace{\mathrm{bin}(w[|w|])\ 0}_{w[|w|]}\,1^{\ell+1}.

For example, if
\Sigma=\{a,b,c\}
,
w=abacaba
,
\mathrm{bin}(a)=00
,

\mathrm{bin}(b)=01
, and
\mathrm{bin}(c)=10
,

\mathrm{san}(w)=\underbrace{00\,0}_{a}\ \underbrace{01\,0}_{b}\ \underbrace{00\,0}_{a}\ \underbrace{10\,0}_{c}\ \underbrace{00\,0}_{a}\ \underbrace{01\,0}_{b}\ \underbrace{00\,0}_{a}\ 111.

Clearly,
w
consists only of binary letters
0
and
1
.
Moreover, after the transformation, we have the following, which is the purpose of considering this transformation.

Lemma 35
.

Let
1\leq p<q\leq|\mathrm{san}(w)|-\ell
.
Then,
\mathrm{san}(w)[q{\!\>..\!\>}|\mathrm{san}(w)|]
is not a prefix of
\mathrm{san}(w)[p{\!\>..\!\>}|\mathrm{san}(w)|]
.

Proof.

Since
q\leq|\mathrm{san}(w)|-\ell
,
\mathrm{san}(w)[q{\!\>..\!\>}|\mathrm{san}(w)|]
ends with
\mathrm{san}(w)[|\mathrm{san}(w)|-\ell{\!\>..\!\>}|\mathrm{san}(w)|]=1^{\ell+1}
.
However, by definition,
\mathrm{san}(w)
contains no substring of the form
1^{\ell+1}
other than
\mathrm{san}(w)[|\mathrm{san}(w)|-\ell{\!\>..\!\>}|\mathrm{san}(w)|]
.
Therefore,
\mathrm{san}(w)[p{\!\>..\!\>}|\mathrm{san}(w)|]
cannot contain
1^{\ell+1}
other than at its end, and thus, cannot have
\mathrm{san}(w)[q{\!\>..\!\>}|\mathrm{san}(w)|]
as a prefix.
∎

Moreover, for a
R
egex
E
, we define the
R
egex
\mathrm{bin}(E)
as the
R
egex obtained by replacing every letter symbol
\sigma
occurring in
E
by the string
\mathrm{bin}(\sigma)\ 0
.
For example, if
\Sigma=\{a,b,c\}
,
E=(a+b)^{*}(b+c)^{*}
,
\mathrm{bin}(a)=00
,

\mathrm{bin}(b)=01
, and
\mathrm{bin}(c)=10
,

\mathrm{bin}(E)=(\underbrace{00\,0}_{a}+\underbrace{01\,0}_{b})^{*}\ (\underbrace{01\,0}_{b}+\underbrace{10\,0}_{c})^{*}.

Furthermore, we define

\mathrm{san}(A):=\mathrm{bin}(A),\ \mathrm{san}(B):=\mathrm{bin}(B),\ \mathrm{san}(C):=\mathrm{bin}(C),\ \text{and}\ \mathrm{san}(D):=\mathrm{bin}(D)\ 1^{\ell+1}.

The following lemma is clear from the definition.

Lemma 36
.

For a
R
egex
E
and a string
w
,

\mathbb{L}\left(\mathrm{bin}(E)\right)=\{\mathrm{san}(w):w\in\mathbb{L}\left(E\right)\}
.

The following lemma ensures that this transformation indeed yields an equivalent problem, which is also clear from the definition.

Lemma 37
.

The following two statements are equivalent.

•

w
can be decomposed as
w=w_{A}\,w_{B}\,w_{C}\,w_{B}\,w_{D}
with
w_{A}\in\mathbb{L}\left(A\right)
,
w_{B}\in\mathbb{L}\left(B\right)
,
w_{C}\in\mathbb{L}\left(C\right)
, and
w_{D}\in\mathbb{L}\left(D\right)
.

•

\mathrm{san}(w)
can be decomposed as
\mathrm{san}(w)=w^{\prime}_{A}\,w^{\prime}_{B}\,w^{\prime}_{C}\,w^{\prime}_{B}\,w^{\prime}_{D}
with
w^{\prime}_{A}\in\mathbb{L}\left(\mathrm{san}(A)\right)
,
w^{\prime}_{B}\in\mathbb{L}\left(\mathrm{san}(B)\right)
,
w^{\prime}_{C}\in\mathbb{L}\left(\mathrm{san}(C)\right)
, and
w^{\prime}_{D}\in\mathbb{L}\left(\mathrm{san}(D)\right)
.

The following lemma is clear from the definition of
\mathrm{san}(D)
, but critical for our further use.

Lemma 38
.

\mathbb{L}\left(\mathrm{san}(D)\right)
consists of only strings with length at least
\ell+1
.

Hereafter, throughout this paper, we refer to the string
\mathrm{san}(w)
and the
R
egexes
\mathrm{san}(A)
,
\mathrm{san}(B)
,
\mathrm{san}(C)
, and
\mathrm{san}(D)
simply as
w
,
A
,
B
,
C
, and
D
, respectively.
In particular, we assume that
\Sigma
is binary and there exists an integer
\ell
such that

•

for each
1\leq p<q\leq|w|-\ell
,
w[q,|w|]
is not a prefix of
w[p{\!\>..\!\>}|w|]
(Lemma
35
), and

•

\mathbb{L}\left(D\right)
consists only of strings of length at least
\ell+1
(Lemma
38
).

By Lemma
37
, this rewriting is an equivalent transformation.

5
Decomposing ABCBD to Two Subproblems

The goal of this section is to decompose the ABCBD problem into the following two subproblems, namely, the
XYYZ
problem
and the
branching
ABCBD
problem
.

†
†
itembox-title:
XYYZ
Problem
Fixed Objects
:
R
egexes
X
,
Y
, and
Z
.

Input
: A binary string
w
.

Task
: Deciding if we can decompose
w
into
w=w_{X}\,w_{Y}\,w_{Y}\,w_{Z}
so that
w_{X}\in\mathbb{L}\left(X\right)
,
w_{Y}\in\mathbb{L}\left(Y\right)
, and
w_{Z}\in\mathbb{L}\left(Z\right)
.

†
†
itembox-title:
Branching
ABCBD
Problem
Fixed Objects
:
R
egexes
A
,
B
,
C
, and
D
.

Condition on
D
: Following the argument of Section
4
, we assume
\epsilon\notin\mathbb{L}\left(D\right)
.

Condition on
B
: Following the argument of Section
5.1
, we assume
\epsilon\notin\mathbb{L}\left(B\right)
.

Input
: A binary string
w
.

Task
: Deciding if we can decompose
w
into
w=w_{A}\,w_{B}\,w_{C}\,w_{B}\,w_{D}
so that
w_{A}\in\mathbb{L}\left(A\right)
,
w_{B}\in\mathbb{L}\left(B\right)
,
w_{C}\in\mathbb{L}\left(C\right)
,
w_{D}\in\mathbb{L}\left(D\right)
, and

•

\sigma\neq\sigma^{\prime}
, where
\sigma
and
\sigma^{\prime}
are the first letters that appear immediately after the first and second occurrence of
w_{B}
in this decomposition, respectively.

Let us illustrate the difference between the Branching
ABCBD
problem and the (vanilla)
ABCBD
problem with examples.
Consider
w=0110110
,
A=C=\Sigma^{*}
, and
B=D=\Sigma^{+}:=\Sigma\Sigma^{*}
.

Example 1
.
The decomposition
w=\underbrace{0}_{w_{A}}\underbrace{11}_{w_{B}}\underbrace{\underline{0}}_{w_{C}}\underbrace{11}_{w_{B}}\underbrace{\underline{0}}_{w_{D}}
is a feasible decomposition for the vanilla
ABCBD
problem, but not for the branching version, because the characters appearing immediately after both occurrences of
w_{B}
are the same letter
\underline{0}
.

Example 2
.
The decomposition
w=\underbrace{01}_{w_{A}}\underbrace{1}_{w_{B}}\underbrace{\underline{0}}_{w_{C}}\underbrace{1}_{w_{B}}\underbrace{\underline{1}0}_{w_{D}}
is feasible for both problems, since the character appearing immediately after the first
w_{B}
is
\underline{0}
and the character after the second
w_{B}
is
\underline{1}
.

Example 3
.
The decomposition
w=\underbrace{0}_{w_{A}}\underbrace{1}_{w_{B}}\underbrace{\epsilon}_{w_{C}}\underbrace{\underline{1}}_{w_{B}}\underbrace{\underline{0}110}_{w_{D}}
is also feasible for both problems, since the character appearing immediately after the first
w_{B}
is
\underline{1}
and the character after the second
w_{B}
is
\underline{0}
.

5.1
Removing the empty string
\epsilon
from
B

Let
A
,
B
,
C
, and
D
be classical
R
egexes (without backreferences) over a finite alphabet
\Sigma
.
Recall that

\mathbb{L}\left(A\;(B)_{x}\;C\;\mathord{\backslash x}\;D\right)=\{w_{A}w_{B}w_{C}w_{B}w_{D}:w_{A}\in A,w_{B}\in B,w_{C}\in C,w_{D}\in D\}.

For the
R
egex
B
,
by Proposition
13
, there exists
B_{-\epsilon}
whose language is
\mathbb{L}\left(B\right)\setminus\{\epsilon\}
.The following trivial case analysis is the first step in our decomposition.

Proposition 39
.

If
\epsilon\in\mathbb{L}\left(B\right)
, we have

\mathbb{L}\left(A\,(B)_{x}\,C\,\mathord{\backslash x}\,D\right)=\mathbb{L}\left(A\,C\,D\right)\cup\mathbb{L}\left(A\,(B_{-\epsilon})_{x}\,C\,\mathord{\backslash x}\,D\right).

Otherwise, we have

\mathbb{L}\left(A\,(B)_{x}\,C\,\mathord{\backslash x}\,D\right)=\mathbb{L}\left(A\,(B_{-\epsilon})_{x}\,C\,\mathord{\backslash x}\,D\right).

Since
ACD
is just a
R
egex rather than
R
EWB,
we can solve the string-matching problem for the expression in linear time.
We hereafter focus on the expression
A\,(B_{-\epsilon})_{x}\,C\,\mathord{\backslash x}\,D
.
From now on, for simplicity, we write
B
for
B_{-\epsilon}
: i.e.,
\epsilon\not\in\mathbb{L}\left(B\right)
.

5.2
The Decomposition

For an
\epsilon
-NFA
N=(Q,\Sigma,\Delta,\mathcal{E},q^{0},F)
,
we define
N^{q^{\prime}\to F^{\prime}}
as the NFA
(Q,\Sigma,\Delta,\mathcal{E},q^{\prime},F^{\prime})
obtained
by replacing the initial state of
N
with
q^{\prime}
and the set of accepting states with
F^{\prime}
.

Let
N_{C}=(Q_{C},\Sigma,\Delta_{C},\mathcal{E}_{C},q^{0}_{C},F_{C})
be a NFA that accepts
\mathbb{L}\left(C\right)
.
We similarly define
N_{D}=(Q_{D},\Sigma,\Delta_{D},\mathcal{E}_{D},q^{0}_{D},F_{D})
.
For the main lemma of this section, we define our building block.
For states
q_{C}\in Q_{C}
and
q_{D}\in Q_{D}
of
N_{C}
and
N_{D}
,
by Proposition
12
, there exists a
R
egex
B_{q_{C},\>q_{D}}
that satisfies the following:

\mathbb{L}\left(B_{q_{C},\>q_{D}}\right)=\mathbb{L}\left(B\right)\cdot\left(\mathbb{L}\left(C^{q^{0}_{C}\to\{q_{C}\}}\right)\cap\mathbb{L}\left(D^{q^{0}_{D}\to\{q_{D}\}}\right)\right).

We now present the main decomposition lemma of this section.

Lemma 40
.

Let
w
be an input string. The following two conditions (1) and (2) are equivalent:

(1)

We have
w\>\in A\>(B)_{x}\>C\>\mathord{\backslash x}\>D
.

(2)

We have either of the following.

XYYZ
Case:

For some
f_{C}\in F_{C}
and
q_{D}\in Q_{D}
,
w
is matched with the following
R
EWB:

A\>(B_{f_{C},\,q_{D}})_{x}\ \mathord{\backslash x}\ D^{q_{D}\to F_{D}}.

Branching Case:

For some
q_{C}\in Q_{C}
and
q_{D}\in Q_{D}
,
w
is matched with the following
R
EWB:

A\>(B_{q_{C},\,q_{D}})_{x}\ C^{q_{C}\to F_{C}}\ \mathord{\backslash x}\ D^{q_{D}\to F_{D}},

along with the next letter of the substring matched with
(B_{q_{C},\,q_{D}})_{x}
,
\sigma
, and the next letter of the substring matched with
\mathord{\backslash x}
,
\sigma^{\prime}
, are different (
\sigma\neq\sigma^{\prime}
).

Proof.

(1)
\Rightarrow
(2):

We decompose
w
as
w=w_{A}w_{B}w_{C}w_{B}w_{D}
where
w_{A}\in\mathbb{L}\left(A\right)
,
w_{B}\in\mathbb{L}\left(B\right)
,
w_{C}\in\mathbb{L}\left(C\right)
, and
w_{D}\in\mathbb{L}\left(D\right)
.
Using the longest common prefix
v
of
w_{C}
and
w_{D}
, we can decompose
w_{C}
and
w_{D}
as follows:

w_{C}=v\,w^{\prime}_{C},\quad w_{D}=v\,w^{\prime}_{D}.

We note that
w^{\prime}_{D}\neq\epsilon
holds because of the following reason:
From Lemma
38
, we have
|w_{D}|\geq\ell+1
.
Therefore, from Lemma
35
and the fact
w_{B}\neq\epsilon
,
w_{D}
is not a prefix of the suffix
w_{C}\,w_{B}\,w_{D}
.
Thus, we have
v\neq w_{D}
, and thus,
w^{\prime}_{D}\neq\epsilon
.

Case1:
w^{\prime}_{C}=\epsilon
.

It means that
w_{C}=v
and
w=w_{A}\,w_{B}\,w_{C}\,w_{B}\,\underbrace{w_{C}\,w^{\prime}_{D}}_{w_{D}}
.

•

On the NFA
N_{C}
, let
N_{C}(w_{C})
be the set of possible states after reading
w_{C}
.
N_{C}(w_{C})
contains an accepting state
f_{C}\in F_{C}
because
w_{C}\in\mathbb{L}\left(C\right)
.

•

On the NFA
N_{D}
, let
N_{D}(v)
be the set of possible states after reading
v=w_{C}
.
Since
w_{D}=v\,w^{\prime}_{D}\in\mathbb{L}\left(D\right)
, there is a state
q_{D}\in N_{D}(v)
such that
w^{\prime}_{D}\in\mathbb{L}\left(D^{q_{D}\to F_{D}}\right)
.

Since
w_{B}w_{C}\in\mathbb{L}\left(B_{f_{C},\,q_{D}}\right)
, we have

w=w_{A}\ \,w_{B}w_{C}\ \,w_{B}w_{C}\ \,w^{\prime}_{D}\in\mathbb{L}\left(A\ (B_{f_{C},\,q_{D}})_{x}\ \mathord{\backslash x}\ D^{q_{D}\to F_{D}}\right),

where
w_{A}\in\mathbb{L}\left(A\right)
,
w_{B}w_{C}\in\mathbb{L}\left(B_{f_{C},\,q_{D}}\right)
, and
w^{\prime}_{D}\in\mathbb{L}\left(D^{q_{D}\to F_{D}}\right)
.
Thus, this case matches the
XYYZ
case.

Case2:
w^{\prime}_{C}\neq\epsilon
.

It means that
w=w_{A}\ w_{B}\ \underbrace{v\ w^{\prime}_{C}}_{w_{C}}\ w_{B}\ \underbrace{v\ w^{\prime}_{D}}_{w_{D}}
. Moreover, from the fact that
v
is the longest common prefix of
w_{C}
and
w_{D}
, the first letters of
w^{\prime}_{C}
and
w^{\prime}_{D}
are different.

•

On the NFA
N_{C}
, let
N_{C}(v)
be the set of possible states after reading
v
.
Since
w_{C}=v\,w^{\prime}_{C}\in\mathbb{L}\left(C\right)
, there is a state
q_{C}\in N_{C}(v)
such that
w^{\prime}_{C}\in\mathbb{L}\left(C^{q_{C}\to F_{C}}\right)
.

•

On the NFA
N_{D}
, let
N_{D}(v)
be the set of possible states after reading
v
.
Since
w_{D}=v\,w^{\prime}_{D}\in\mathbb{L}\left(D\right)
, there is a state
q_{D}\in N_{D}(v)
such that
w^{\prime}_{D}\in\mathbb{L}\left(D^{q_{D}\to F_{D}}\right)
.

Then, the string
w=w_{A}(w_{B}v)w^{\prime}_{C}(w_{B}v)w^{\prime}_{D}
matches the following expression:

A\ (B_{q_{C},\ q_{D}})_{x}\ (C^{q_{C}\to F_{C}})\mathord{\backslash x}\ (D^{q_{D}\to F_{D}})

with
w_{B}v\in\mathbb{L}\left(B_{q_{C},q_{D}}\right)
,
w^{\prime}_{C}\in\mathbb{L}\left(C^{q_{C}\to F_{C}}\right)
, and
w^{\prime}_{D}\in\mathbb{L}\left(D^{q_{D}\to F_{D}}\right)
.
Moreover, in such a matching, the letters that appear immediately after the two substrings
w_{B}v
are different.
Thus, this case matches the branching case.

(2)
\Rightarrow
(1):

XYYZ
case:

Assume

w\in\mathbb{L}\left(A\ (B_{f_{C},q_{D}})_{x}\ \mathord{\backslash x}\ D^{q_{D}\to F_{D}}\right),

where
f_{C}\in F_{C}
and
q_{D}\in Q_{D}
.
We decompose
w
as

w=w_{A}w_{B}w_{B}w_{D},

where
w_{A}\in\mathbb{L}\left(A\right)
,
w_{B}\in\mathbb{L}\left(B_{f_{C},q_{D}}\right)
, and
w_{D}\in\mathbb{L}\left(D^{q_{D}\to F_{D}}\right)
.

By the definition of
B_{f_{C},q_{D}}
,
we can further decompose
w_{B}
as
w_{B}=w_{1}w_{2}
, where
w_{1}\in\mathbb{L}\left(B\right)
and

w_{2}\in\mathbb{L}\left(C^{q^{0}_{C}\to\{f_{C}\}}\right)\cap\mathbb{L}\left(D^{q^{0}_{D}\to\{q_{D}\}}\right)
.
Particularly,
w_{2}\in\mathbb{L}\left(C^{q^{0}_{C}\to\{f_{C}\}}\right)\subseteq\mathbb{L}\left(C\right)
and
w_{2}\,w_{D}\in\mathbb{L}\left(D^{q^{0}_{D}\to\{q_{D}\}}\right)\cdot\mathbb{L}\left(D^{\{q_{D}\}\to F_{D}}\right)\subseteq\mathbb{L}\left(D\right)
.
Therefore, we have

w=w_{A}\ \underbrace{w_{1}\ w_{2}}_{w_{B}}\ \underbrace{w_{1}\ w_{2}}_{w_{B}}w_{D}\in\mathbb{L}\left(A(B)_{x}\ C\ \mathord{\backslash x}\ D\right)

with
w_{A}\in\mathbb{L}\left(A\right)
,
w_{1}\in\mathbb{L}\left(B\right)
,
w_{2}\in\mathbb{L}\left(C\right)
, and
w_{2}w_{D}\in\mathbb{L}\left(D\right)
.

Branching Case:

Assume

w\in\mathbb{L}\left(A\>(B_{q_{C},\,q_{D}})_{x}\ C^{q_{C}\to F_{C}}\ \mathord{\backslash x}\ D^{q_{D}\to F_{D}}\right),

where
q_{C}\in Q_{C}
and
q_{D}\in Q_{D}
is a state of
C
and
D
, respectively.
We decompose
w
as

w=w_{A}\,w_{B}\,w_{C}\,w_{B}\,w_{D},

where
w_{A}\in\mathbb{L}\left(A\right)
,
w_{B}\in\mathbb{L}\left(B_{q_{C},q_{D}}\right)
,
w_{C}\in\mathbb{L}\left(C^{q_{C}\to F_{C}}\right)
, and
w_{D}\in\mathbb{L}\left(D^{q_{D}\to F_{D}}\right)
.

By the definition of
B_{q_{C},q_{D}}
, we can further decompose
w_{B}
as
w_{B}=w_{1}w_{2}
, where
w_{1}\in\mathbb{L}\left(B\right)
and

w_{2}\in\mathbb{L}\left(C^{q^{0}_{C}\to\{q_{C}\}}\right)\cap\mathbb{L}\left(D^{q^{0}_{D}\to\{q_{D}\}}\right)
.
Particularly,
w_{2}\,w_{C}\in\mathbb{L}\left(C^{q^{0}_{C}\to\{q_{C}\}}\right)\cdot\mathbb{L}\left(C^{\{q_{C}\}\to F_{C}}\right)\subseteq\mathbb{L}\left(C\right)
and
w_{2}\,w_{D}\in\mathbb{L}\left(D^{q^{0}_{D}\to\{q_{D}\}}\right)\cdot\mathbb{L}\left(D^{\{q_{D}\}\to F_{D}}\right)\subseteq\mathbb{L}\left(D\right)
.
Therefore, we have

w=w_{A}\ \underbrace{w_{1}\ w_{2}}_{w_{B}}w_{C}\ \underbrace{w_{1}\ w_{2}}_{w_{B}}w_{D}\in\mathbb{L}\left(A(B)_{x}\ C\ \mathord{\backslash x}\ D\right)

with
w_{A}\in\mathbb{L}\left(A\right)
,
w_{1}\in\mathbb{L}\left(B\right)
,
w_{2}w_{C}\in\mathbb{L}\left(C\right)
, and
w_{2}w_{D}\in\mathbb{L}\left(D\right)
.
∎

Now, we reduce the
ABCBD
problem into the
XYYZ
problem and the branching
ABCBD
problem.
Let
w
be the given string.
If
\epsilon\in B
, we check whether
w
matches the
R
egex
A\,C\,D
and replace
B
by
B_{-\epsilon}
.
If it matches, we return
\mathsf{yes}
.
Otherwise, Lemma
40
ensures that
w\>\in A\>(B)_{x}\>C\>\mathord{\backslash x}\>D
if and only if

•

w
is a
\mathsf{yes}
-instance of the
XYYZ
problem for
X:=A
,
Y:=B_{f_{C},q_{D}}
, and
Z:=D^{q_{D}\to F_{D}}
for some
f_{C}\in F_{C}
and
q_{D}\in Q_{D}
, or

•

w
is a
\mathsf{yes}
-instance of the branching
ABCBD
problem for
A:=A
,
B:=B_{q_{C},q_{D}}
,
C:=C^{q_{C}\to F_{C}}
, and
D:=D^{q_{D}\to F_{D}}
for some
q_{C}\in Q_{C}
and
q_{D}\in Q_{D}
.

This algorithm solves the
ABCBD
problem by calling oracles for the
XYYZ
problem and the branching
ABCBD
problem.
Moreover, it internally solves the
XYYZ
problem
|F_{C}||Q_{D}|
times and the branching
ABCBD
problem
|Q_{C}||Q_{D}|
times, both of which are constant.
Therefore, we have the following.

Lemma 41
.

Let
f
be a function and assume both of the
XYYZ
problem and the branching
ABCBD
problem admit
O(f(|w|))
-time algorithms. Then, the
ABCBD
problem admits an
O(f(|w|))
-time algorithm.

6
(X\,YY\,Z)
-Problem

We first restate our subproblem, the
XYYZ
problem.

†
†
itembox-title:
XYYZ
Problem
Fixed Objects
:
R
egexes
X
,
Y
, and
Z
.

Input
: A binary string
w
.

Task
: Deciding if we can decompose
w
into
w=w_{X}\,w_{Y}\,w_{Y}\,w_{Z}
so that
w_{X}\in\mathbb{L}\left(X\right)
,
w_{Y}\in\mathbb{L}\left(Y\right)
, and
w_{Z}\in\mathbb{L}\left(Z\right)
.

6.1
Crochemore’s Maximal Local Power Algorithm

A string
x
is
repetitive
or
power
if it is represented as
x=y^{e}
for some string
y
and an integer
e\geq 2
.
If
x
is not repetitive,
x
is
primitive
.

For example,
(1) a string
abababab
is repetitive because
abababab=(ab)^{4}
;
(2) a string
abababa=(ab)^{3}a
is
primitive
.

As in
(ab)^{4}=(abab)^{2}
, there may be multiple representations for one repetitive string.
For the unique representation, we consider the decomposition by primitive words.
More precisely, for a repetitive string
x
, we denote it as
(p,k)
where
x=p^{k}
and
p
is
primitive
.

Proposition 42

(
[
28
, Chapter 9]
)
.

If a string
x
is repetitive, there exists just one representation
(p,k)
.

Proof.

Since
x
is repetitive, for some string
v
,
x=v^{i}
with
i\geq 2
. If
v
is not primitive,
we (repeatedly) decompose
v
and obtain a primitive factor
p
such as
x=p^{k}
.
If
x=p^{k}=q^{r}
held for different primitives
p
and
q
, then
x=y^{e}
for
y=x[1..\mathit{gcd}(|p|,|q|)]
by Fine and Wilf’s theorem (Lemma
10
).
Since
|p|\bmod|y|=|q|\bmod|y|=0
, it contradicts the assumption that
p
and
q
are primitives.
∎

Let
w
be a string.
A substring
x
of
w
is
local power
at an index
p

if
x
is repetitive and
x
starts from the index
p
,
x=w[p{\,..\,}p+|x|)
.
For a primitive
\theta
,
1\leq p\leq|w|
, and
e\geq 2
, we write
(p,\theta,e)
to denote the local power
\theta^{e}
at the index
p
.
A local power
(p,\theta,e)
is (two-sided)
maximal
if
(1) it is not extensible to the left: i.e.,
w[p-|\theta|\,..\,p)\neq\theta
or
p-|\theta|<1
; and
(2) it is not extensible to the right: i.e.,
\theta\neq w[p+e|\theta|\,..\,p+(e+1)|\theta|)
or
p+(e+1)|\theta|>n+1
.

Example.

For a string
x=aabaabaabba
,
there are two kinds of maximal powers:

•

|\theta|=1
:
(1,a,2)
,
(4,a,2)
,
(7,a,2)
, and
(9,b,2)
.

•

|\theta|=3
:
(1,aab,3)
,
(2,aba,2)
, and
(3,baa,2)
.

It is worth noting that
(4,aab,2)
is a local power but not maximal because it is covered by
(1,aab,3)
.
The following result is shown by Crochemore
[
27
]
and plays a critical role in our algorithm.

Theorem 43

(
[
27
]
,
[
28
, Chapter 9]
)
.

Let
w
be a string and
n:=|w|
.
We can compute the set of all maximal local powers of
w
in
O(n\log n)
-time.
The size of this set is also
O(n\log n)
.

We show that we can limit the search space for solutions of the
XYYZ
problem as follows.

Lemma 44
.

The following conditions are equivalent:

(1)

We have a desired decomposition
w=x\>y\,y\>z
where
x\in\mathbb{L}\left(X\right)
,
y\in\mathbb{L}\left(Y\right)
, and
z\in\mathbb{L}\left(Z\right)
.

(2)

There is a maximal local power
(p,\theta,e)
such that

w=w_{1}\ \underbrace{\theta^{i}\ \theta^{j}\,\theta^{j}\,\theta^{k}}_{(p,\theta,e)}\ w_{2},\qquad\text{where}\quad(w_{1}\theta^{i})\in\mathbb{L}\left(X\right),\theta^{j}\in\mathbb{L}\left(Y\right),(\theta^{k}w_{2})\in\mathbb{L}\left(Z\right).

Proof.

Trivial Case
(2)\Rightarrow(1)
:

If
(2)
holds, we just set
x:=w_{1}\theta^{i}
,
y:=\theta^{j}
, and
z:=\theta^{k}w_{2}
.

Case
(1)\Rightarrow(2)
:
Consider a decomposition
w=x\,\underline{y\,y}\,z
.
Since the string
y\,y
is a power, there is a maximal local power
(p,\theta,e)
that covers the substring
\underline{y\,y}
; that is,
y=\theta^{j}
for some
j
and the substring
\underline{y\,y}
begins at the index
p+i|\theta|
for some
0\leq i\leq e-2j
.
By taking this maximal local power
(p,\theta,e)
and integers
i,j
, together with setting
k=e-i-2j
, we have
x=w_{1}\,\theta^{i}
,
y=\theta^{j}
, and
z=\theta^{k}w_{2}
, where
w_{1}=w[1\,..\,p)
and
w_{2}=w[p+e|\theta|\,..\,|w|]
, respectively.
∎

6.2
Our Tool on Ultimately Periodic Set

The following lemma is fundamental.

Lemma 45
.

For a finite monoid
\mathcal{M}
and its elements
\delta
and
\delta^{\prime}
,
\{i\geq 0\colon\delta^{i}=\delta^{\prime}\}
is an ultimately periodic set.

Proof.

Consider the sequence
(\delta^{0},\delta^{1},\dots)
. Since
\mathcal{M}
is finite, there is an element
\delta^{\prime\prime}
that appears at least twice in this sequence.
Let
\delta^{\prime\prime}=\delta^{k_{1}}=\delta^{k_{2}}
for
k_{1}<k_{2}
. Then, for
k\geq k_{2}
, we have
\delta^{k}=\delta^{k-k_{2}}\delta^{k_{2}}=\delta^{k-k_{2}}\delta^{k_{1}}=\delta^{k-(k_{2}-k_{1})}
. Thus, the set
\{i\geq 0\colon\delta^{i}=\delta^{\prime}\}
is periodic for
i\geq k_{1}
.
∎

To give an algorithm for the
XYYZ
problem, we repeatedly use the following lemma.

Lemma 46
.

Let
E
be a
R
egex,
\delta\in\mathcal{T}_{E}
, and
w_{1},\theta,w_{2}
be strings.
Then,
\{i\geq 0\colon\mathcal{T}_{E}(w_{1}\,\theta^{i}\,w_{2})=\delta\}
is ultimately periodic.
Moreover, the parameters
\mu
and
\lambda
of that set are bounded by a constant that depends only on
E
.

Proof.

Let
\delta_{1}=\mathcal{T}_{E}(w_{1})
,
\delta_{\theta}=\mathcal{T}_{E}(\theta)
, and
\delta_{2}=\mathcal{T}_{E}(w_{2})
.
Let
\mathcal{D}=\{\delta^{\prime}\in\mathcal{T}_{E}\colon\delta_{1}\odot\delta^{\prime}\odot\delta_{2}=\delta\}
.
Then, the desired set is
\bigcup_{\delta^{\prime}\in\mathcal{D}}\{i\geq 0\colon\delta_{\theta}^{i}=\delta^{\prime}\}
, which is ultimately periodic because it is the union of a finite number of ultimately periodic sets.
Moreover, since that set depends only on transitions
\delta_{1}
,
\delta_{\theta}
, and
\delta_{2}
, the parameters
\mu
and
\lambda
of that set are bounded by a constant that depends only on
E
.
∎

6.3
Checking each maximal local power in
O(1)
-time

Using Lemma
44
,
for a given maximal local power
(p,\theta,e)
,
we hereafter focus on the problem of whether we can decompose
w
as

w=\underbracket{\,w_{1}\>\theta^{i}}_{\in X}\ \underbracket{\,\theta^{j}\,}_{\in Y}\underbracket{\,\theta^{j}\,}_{\in Y}\underbracket{\,\theta^{k}\,w_{2}\>}_{\in Z}\qquad i+2j+k=e,

where the prefix
w_{1}=[1\,..\,p)
and the suffix
w_{2}=[p+e|\theta|\,..\,|w|]
are uniquely determined from
(p,\theta,e)
.

To solve the above problem (
\star
) in
O(1)
-time, we introduce some sets of exponents that are candidates of integers
i
,
j
, and
k
.
Let

\mathcal{S}_{X}:=\{i\geq 0:X(w_{1}\,\theta^{i})\text{ is accepting}\}=\bigcup_{\begin{subarray}{c}\delta\in\mathcal{M}(X)\\
\delta\text{ is accepting}\end{subarray}}\{i\geq 0:\mathcal{T}_{X}(w_{1}\,\theta^{i})=\delta\},

\mathcal{S}_{Y}:=\{j\geq 0:Y(\theta^{j})\text{ is accepting}\}=\bigcup_{\begin{subarray}{c}\delta\in\mathcal{M}(Y)\\
\delta\text{ is accepting}\end{subarray}}\{j\geq 0:\mathcal{T}_{Y}(\theta^{j})=\delta\},

\mathcal{S}_{Z}:=\{k\geq 0:Z(\theta^{k}\,w_{2})\text{ is accepting}\}=\bigcup_{\begin{subarray}{c}\delta\in\mathcal{M}(Z)\\
\delta\text{ is accepting}\end{subarray}}\{k\geq 0:\mathcal{T}_{Z}(\theta^{k}\,w_{2})=\delta\},

From Lemmas
14
and
46
, all of
\mathcal{S}_{X}
,
\mathcal{S}_{Y}
, and
\mathcal{S}_{Z}
are ultimately periodic whose parameters
\mu
and
\lambda
are bounded by a constant that depends only on
X
,
Y
, and
Z
, respectively.

Now, we define

\mathcal{S}:=\mathcal{S}_{X}+(2\cdot\mathcal{S}_{Y})+\mathcal{S}_{Z}.

From Lemma
14
,
\mathcal{S}
is also ultimately periodic whose parameters
\mu
and
\lambda
are bounded by a constant that depends only on
X
,
Y
, and
Z
.
We have the following.

Lemma 47
.

There is a way to decompose
w
as in (
\star
) if and only if
e\in\mathcal{S}
.

Proof.

Assume there is a way to decompose
w
as in (
\star
).
Let
i
,
j
, and
k
be the exponents in that decomposition.
Since
w_{1}\,\theta^{i}
,
\theta^{j}
, and
\theta^{k}\,w_{2}
are accepted by
X
,
Y
, and
Z
, respectively, we have
i\in\mathcal{S}_{X}
,
j\in\mathcal{S}_{Y}
, and
k\in\mathcal{S}_{Z}
.
Thus, from the definition of the Minkowski sum and the
2
-multiplication, we have
e=i+2j+k\in\mathcal{S}
.

Conversely, assume
e\in\mathcal{S}
.
Then, there is a tuple of integers
i,j,k
with
i\in\mathcal{S}_{X}
,
j\in\mathcal{S}_{Y}
,
k\in\mathcal{S}_{Z}
such that
i+2j+k=e
.
From the definition of
\mathcal{S}_{X}
,
\mathcal{S}_{Y}
, and
\mathcal{S}_{Z}
, we have
w_{1}\,\theta^{i}\in X
,
\theta^{j}\in Y
, and
\theta^{k}\,w_{2}\in Z
. Thus, the lemma is proved.
∎

Clearly, for
\mathcal{S}=(\mu,M,\lambda,T)
and
e\leq|w|
, whether
e\in\mathcal{S}
or not can be determined in
O(\mu+\lambda)=O(1)
-time.
Moreover,
\mathcal{S}
itself can be computed in constant time from the transitions
\mathcal{T}_{X}(w_{1})
,
\mathcal{T}_{X}(\theta)
,
\mathcal{T}_{Y}(\theta)
,
\mathcal{T}_{Z}(\theta)
, and
\mathcal{T}_{Z}(w_{2})
.
Furthermore, those transitions can also be computed in constant time using the factorization forest data structure
\mathsf{FF}_{X}
,
\mathsf{FF}_{Y}
, and
\mathsf{FF}_{Z}
, which can be constructed in linear time.
Thus, with linear time pre-computation, we can answer the problem defined in (
\star
) in constant time, which yields the following.

Lemma 48
.

The XYYZ problem can be solved in
O(|w|\log|w|)
time.

The above discussion still works when we replace the specific constant
2
with any constant integer
\alpha\geq 2
.
Thus, we have Theorem
9
.
See
9

7
Algorithm Overview for the Branching
ABCBD
Problem

7.1
Notation

Recall that
\mathsf{ST}_{w}
is the suffix tree of
w
.
We simply write
\mathsf{ST}
for
\mathsf{ST}_{w}
.

Let
v
be a node of
\mathsf{ST}
.
We write
\mathscr{I}_{v}
for the set of indices of suffix nodes of the subtree rooted at
v
and it is formally defined as follows:

\mathscr{I}_{v}:=\{\mathit{Occ}(u):u\in\mathsf{ST}[v],\mathit{isSuffix}(u)=1\}.

We also write
\mathscr{H}_{v}
and
\mathscr{L}_{v}
for the set of indices of suffix nodes in heavy and light subtrees of
v
, respectively.
More formally, we define
\mathscr{H}_{v}
and
\mathscr{L}_{v}
as follows.
Let
u_{H}
(resp.
u_{L}
) be the child of
v
where
v\to u_{H}
is a heavy edge (resp.
v\to u_{L}
is a light edge). If there is no such child, we set
u_{H}=\bot
(resp.
u_{L}=\bot
).
Then, we define

\begin{array}[]{lcl}\mathscr{H}_{v}&:=&\{\mathit{Occ}(u):u\in\mathsf{ST}[u_{H}],\ \mathit{isSuffix}(u)=1\},\\
\mathscr{L}_{v}&:=&\{\mathit{Occ}(u):u\in\mathsf{ST}[u_{L}],\ \mathit{isSuffix}(u)=1\}\cup\begin{cases}\{v\}&\text{if }\mathit{isSuffix}(v)=1\\
\emptyset&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}\end{array}

where we set
\mathsf{ST}[\bot]=\emptyset
.

Note that
v
itself is in
\mathscr{L}_{v}
when
v
is a suffix node.
Moreover, for each non-leaf node
v
,

\mathscr{I}_{v}=\mathscr{H}_{v}\cup\mathscr{L}_{v}
and

\mathscr{H}_{v}=\mathscr{H}_{u_{H}}\cup\mathscr{L}_{u_{H}}
clearly hold.

7.2
From Branching ABCBD to Two Enumeration Problems

In this section, we introduce two subproblems
Left-
B
Transition Enumeration
and
Right-
B
Transition Enumeration
for decomposing the branching ABCBD problem.
We define both of those problems on a heavy-path
\pi
of the suffix tree
\mathsf{ST}_{w}
,
and utilize them to determine whether there is a branching decomposition of
w=w_{A}\ w_{B}\ w_{C}\ w_{B}\ w_{D}
such that
w_{B}=\Gamma(v)
for some
v\in\pi
.

We consider the following branching decomposition of
w
:

w=w_{A}\ w_{B}\ w_{C}\ w_{B}\ w_{D},

where
w_{B}=\Gamma(v)
for some
v\in\pi
.
We denote
m_{v}:=|w_{B}|
.

Let
p
and
q
be the indices of the left ends of the first and the second occurrences of
w_{B}
:
i.e.,
w[1{\,..\,}p)=w_{A}
,
w[p{\,..\,}p+m_{v})=w_{B}
,
w[p+m_{v}{\,..\,}q)=w_{C}
,
w[q{\,..\,}q+m_{v})=w_{B}
, and
w[q+m_{v}{\,..\,}|w|]=w_{D}
.
It is clear that
p,q\in\mathscr{I}_{v}=\mathscr{H}_{v}\cup\mathscr{L}_{v}
.
We illustrate this situation as follows:

w=\underbrace{w[1{\,..\,}p)}_{w_{A}}\ \underbrace{w[p{\,..\,}p+m_{v})}_{w_{B}}\ \underbrace{w[p+m_{v}{\,..\,}q)}_{w_{C}}\ \underbrace{w[q{\,..\,}q+m_{v})}_{w_{B}}\ \underbrace{w[q+m_{v}{\,..\,}|w|]}_{w_{D}}.

Deciding if there is a branching decomposition for a node
v
of
\mathsf{ST}
, we first check
\Gamma(v)=w_{B}\in\mathbb{L}\left(B\right)
.
This check is done in
O(1)
-time by using the factorization forest
\mathsf{FF}
and Lemma
18
.
Then, we consider the following set of candidate transitions of
C
:

\mathcal{K}=\{\delta\in\mathcal{T}_{C}:\exists p,q\in\mathscr{I}_{v}.\ \mathcal{K}(p,q,\delta,m_{v})\land q+m_{v}\leq|w|\land w[p+m_{v}]\neq w[q+m_{v}]\},

where
\mathcal{K}(p,q,\delta,m)
is a predicate defined as follows:

\mathcal{K}(p,q,\delta,m):=w[1{\,..\,}p)\in\mathbb{L}\left(A\right)\land p+m\leq q\land\delta=\Delta_{C}(\,w[p+m{\,..\,}q)\,)\land w[q+m{\,..\,}|w|]\in\mathbb{L}\left(D\right).

Now, we rephrase our branching ABCBD problem using the above set
\mathcal{K}
.

First, for every node
v
,
the following two conditions are equivalent:

•

There is a branching decomposition
w=w_{A}\ w_{B}\ w_{C}\ w_{B}\ w_{D}
with
w_{B}=\Gamma(v)
.

•

There is a transition
\delta
in the set
\mathcal{K}
where
\delta
is accepting on
C
.

This correspondence is clear from our condition on the branching ABCBD problem; that is, we are seeking the decomposition that satisfies the following condition:

•

the letters immediately after two occurrences of
w_{B}
’s are different.

It should be noted that, from the argument of Section
4
, we assume
\epsilon\notin\mathbb{L}\left(D\right)
.

Next, on a node
v
, assume
\delta\in\mathcal{K}
for some accepting transition
\delta
.
It means that there exist
p
and
q
such that
\mathcal{K}(p,q,\delta,m_{v})
and
q+m_{v}\leq|w|\land w[p+m_{v}]\neq w[q+m_{v}]
.
Then, it holds that one of
\{p,q\}
must belong to the heavy side
\mathscr{H}_{v}
and the other must belong to the light side
\mathscr{L}_{v}
. It is confirmed as follows:

•

Recall
p,q\in\mathscr{I}_{v}=\mathscr{H}_{v}\cup\mathscr{L}_{v}
.

•

The condition
q+m_{v}\leq|w|\land w[p+m_{v}]\neq w[q+m_{v}]
equals the “branching by next letter” condition of the suffix tree.

•

Therefore, one of
\{p,q\}
must belong to the heavy-side subtree
\mathsf{ST}[u_{H}]
of
v
and the other must belong to the light-side subtree
\mathsf{ST}[u_{L}]
of
v
.

Finally, we can compute
\mathcal{K}
by solving the following two enumeration problems:

•

Enumerating transitions
\delta
where
p
is in the heavy side
\mathscr{H}_{v}
and
q
is in the light side
\mathscr{L}_{v}
.

•

Enumerating transitions
\delta
where
p
is in the light side
\mathscr{L}_{v}
and
q
is in the heavy side
\mathscr{H}_{v}
.

More formally, the above two tasks correspond to computing the following two sets:

\begin{array}[]{rll}\mathcal{K}_{L}&:=&\{\delta\in\mathcal{T}_{C}:\exists p\in\mathscr{H}_{v}.\,\exists q\in\mathscr{L}_{v}.\ \mathcal{K}(p,q,\delta,m_{v})\},\\
\mathcal{K}_{R}&:=&\{\delta\in\mathcal{T}_{C}:\exists p\in\mathscr{L}_{v}.\,\exists q\in\mathscr{H}_{v}.\ \mathcal{K}(p,q,\delta,m_{v})\}.\end{array}

For every node
v\in\mathsf{ST}
, it is clear that
\mathcal{K}=\mathcal{K}_{L}\cup\mathcal{K}_{R}
.

7.3
Left-B and Right-B

According to the discussion in the previous section, we define the following problems:

†
†
itembox-title:
Left-
B
Transition Enumeration
Fixed Objects
:
R
egexes
A
,
B
,
C
, and
D
and a string
w
.

Input
: A heavy-path
\pi
of
\mathsf{ST}_{w}
.

Task:
Compute the union of
\mathcal{K}_{L}(v)
for all
v\in\pi
. More formally, enumerate all
\delta\in\mathcal{T}_{C}
such that there is a node
v\in\pi
,
i\in\mathscr{L}_{v}
, and
j\in\mathscr{H}_{v}
with

•

j+m_{v}\leq i
,

•

w[1{\,..\,}j)\in\mathbb{L}\left(A\right)
,
\Gamma(v)\in\mathbb{L}\left(B\right)
, and
w[i+m_{v}{\,..\,}|w|]\in\mathbb{L}\left(D\right)
, and

•

\delta=\mathcal{T}_{C}(\,w[j+m_{v}{\,..\,}i)\,)
.

Recall that
\Gamma(v)=w[j{\,..\,}j+m_{v})=w[i{\,..\,}i+m_{v})
holds by definition of
m_{v}
.

†
†
itembox-title:
Right-
B
Transition Enumeration
Fixed Objects
:
R
egexes
A
,
B
,
C
, and
D
and a string
w
.

Input
: A heavy-path
\pi
of
\mathsf{ST}_{w}
.

Task:
Compute the union of
\mathcal{K}_{R}(v)
for all
v\in\pi
. More formally, enumerate all
\delta\in\mathcal{T}_{C}
such that
there is a node
v\in\pi
,
i\in\mathscr{L}_{v}
, and
j\in\mathscr{H}_{v}
with

•

i+m_{v}\leq j

•

w[1{\,..\,}i)\in\mathbb{L}\left(A\right)
,
\Gamma(v)\in\mathbb{L}\left(B\right)
, and
w[j+m_{v}{\,..\,}|w|]\in\mathbb{L}\left(D\right)
, and

•

\delta=\mathcal{T}_{C}(\,w[i+m_{v}{\,..\,}j)\,)
.

Recall that
\Gamma(v)=w[i{\,..\,}i+m_{v})=w[j{\,..\,}j+m_{v})
holds by definition of
m_{v}
.

These problems are identical except for the positional relation; that is, which interval
[i,i+m_{v})
or
[j,j+m_{v})
corresponding to the string
w_{B}
is located to the left of the other.

Procedure

branching-ABCBD
(
w
,
A
,
B
,
C
,
D
)

1

Compute the suffix tree
\mathsf{ST}_{w}
and its heavy-light decomposition;

2

Compute the factorization forests
\mathsf{FF}_{A}
,
\mathsf{FF}_{B}
,
\mathsf{FF}_{C}
, and
\mathsf{FF}_{D}
for
A
,
B
,
C
, and
D
, respectively;

3

//
in
O(|w|)
-time

for

Heavy-path
\pi
of
\mathsf{ST}_{w}

do

4

\mathcal{K}_{L}\leftarrow
Left-
B
Transition Enumeration
(
\pi,w,A,B,C,D
);

5

\mathcal{K}_{R}\leftarrow
Right-
B
Transition Enumeration
(
\pi,w,A,B,C,D
);

6

if

There is an
accepting
transition in
\mathcal{K}_{L}\cup\mathcal{K}_{R}

then

7

return

\mathsf{yes}
;

8

return

\mathsf{no}
;

9

Algorithm 1

Algorithm Overview.

The remaining part of this paper concentrates on solving those problems in
O(|\mathsf{ST}[\pi]|\log|w|)
time, where we define
\mathsf{ST}[\pi]:=\mathsf{ST}[u]
using the topmost vertex
u
of
\pi
.
Using oracles for those problems as sub-routines, we solve the branching
ABCBD
problem as in Algorithm
1
.
Our algorithm first computes the suffix tree
\mathsf{ST}_{w}
and its heavy-light decomposition, and factorization forests
\mathsf{FF}_{A}
,
\mathsf{FF}_{B}
,
\mathsf{FF}_{C}
, and
\mathsf{FF}_{D}
for further use.
Then, for each heavy-path
\pi
, we compute the union of all
\mathcal{K}_{L}
and
\mathcal{K}_{R}
using our oracles.
Then, the answer is
\mathsf{yes}
if there is an accepting transition in at least one of such
\mathcal{K}_{L}
’s or
\mathcal{K}_{R}
’s.
The following lemma proves that, using our oracles, Algorithm
1
correctly solves the branching
ABCBD
problem efficiently.

Lemma 49
.

Algorithm
1
solves the branching ABCBD problem for
w
in
O(|w|\log^{2}|w|)
-time.

Proof.

From above discussion, it is clear that Algorithm
1
correctly solves the branching ABCBD problem.
The remaining task is to bound the time complexity.

Computation of the suffix tree, heavy-light decomposition, and factorization forests takes
O(|w|)
-time.
As we will show,
Left-
B
Transition Enumeration
and
Right-
B
Transition Enumeration
can be solved in
O(|\mathsf{ST}[\pi]|\log|w|)
-time.
Moreover, the sum of
|\mathsf{ST}[\pi]|
over all heavy-paths
\pi
is
O(|w|\log|w|)
, since the number of vertices in
\mathsf{ST}
is
O(|w|)
and each vertex of
\mathsf{ST}
belongs to at most
O(\log|w|)
different
\pi
’s.
Thus, the time complexity of Algorithm
1
is
O(|w|\log^{2}|w|)
.
∎

For both two enumeration problems, we employ a common strategy to solve them: we process
v\in\pi
in bottom-up order, and for each
i\in\mathscr{L}_{v}
, we efficiently enumerate transitions corresponding to
j\in\mathscr{H}_{v}
that satisfy the conditions using queries to certain data structures.
However, the data structures and algorithms for those two problems are different.
From the next section, we provide efficient algorithms for those two enumeration problems.

8
Right-
B
Transition Enumeration

In this section, we solve
Right-
B
Transition Enumeration
, while the description of the data structure used internally is postponed to Section
9
.

8.1
Overall Strategy

For
v\in\pi
and
i\in\mathscr{L}_{v}
, we define the set
\mathcal{R}(v,i)
by

\mathcal{R}(v,i):=\{\Delta_{C}(\,w[i+m_{v}{\,..\,}j)\,):j\in\mathscr{H}_{v},\ i+m_{v}\leq j,\ w[j+m_{v}{\,..\,}|w|]\in\mathbb{L}\left(D\right)\}.

Then, by definition, the desired output for
Right-
B
Transition Enumeration
is

\bigcup_{\begin{subarray}{c}v\in\pi\\
\Gamma(v)\in\mathbb{L}\left(B\right)\end{subarray}}\ \bigcup_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\in\mathscr{L}_{v}\\
w[1{\,..\,}i)\in\mathbb{L}\left(A\right)\end{subarray}}\mathcal{R}(v,i).

Our algorithm iterates over all
v\in\pi
in bottom-up order,
and then for each
v
with
\Gamma(v)\in\mathbb{L}\left(B\right)
, iterates over all
i\in\mathscr{L}_{v}
with
w[1{\,..\,}i)\in\mathbb{L}\left(A\right)
to compute
\mathcal{R}(v,i)
, and outputs their union.

Now, we focus on computing
\mathcal{R}(v,i)
.
The main obstacle here is the constraint
w[j+m_{v}{\,..\,}|w|]\in\mathbb{L}\left(D\right)
.
To tackle this, we partition
\mathscr{H}_{v}
into the following disjoint sets, which we call
bags
, indexed by a transition
\delta
of
D
:

\lbag\delta\rbag_{v}:=\{j\in\mathscr{H}_{v}:\Delta_{D}(\,w[j+m_{v}{\,..\,}|w|]\,)=\delta\}.

Then,
\mathscr{H}_{v}
can be represented as

\mathscr{H}_{v}=\bigcup_{\delta\in\mathcal{T}_{D}}\lbag\delta\rbag_{v}.

Then, we can compute
\mathcal{R}(v,i)
by computing

\{\Delta_{C}(\,w[i+m_{v}{\,..\,}j)\,):j\in\lbag\delta\rbag_{v},\ i+m_{v}\leq j\},

for each accepting transition
\delta
of
D
and taking their union.

8.2
Disjoint-Set Union

Since the partition
\mathscr{H}_{v}=\bigcup_{\delta\in\mathcal{T}_{D}}\lbag\delta\rbag_{v}
depends on
v
, we should update it when we traverse upward along
\pi
.
Let
u
be the parent of
v
.
The next task is to compute the partition
\mathscr{H}_{u}=\bigcup_{\delta\in\mathcal{T}_{D}}\lbag\delta\rbag_{u}
from the partition
\mathscr{H}_{v}=\bigcup_{\delta\in\mathcal{T}_{D}}\lbag\delta\rbag_{v}
.

Let

\tau:=\Delta_{D}(\,\Gamma(v)(m_{u}{\,..\,}m_{v}]\,)~~(=\Delta_{D}(\,\Gamma(v)[m_{u}+1\,{\,..\,}\,m_{v}]\,).

Then,
\tau=\Delta_{D}(\,w[j+m_{u}{\,..\,}j+m_{v})\,)
holds for all
j\in\mathscr{H}_{v}\cup\mathscr{L}_{v}=\mathscr{H}_{u}

because
\Gamma(v)[1\,{\,..\,}\,m_{v}]=w[j{\,..\,}j+m_{v})
.
(Recall that we employ the
1
-origin indexing,
w=w[1]w[2]\ldots w[|w|]
.)

Thus, for each
\delta\in\mathcal{T}_{D}
, we have

\displaystyle\lbag\delta\rbag_{u}

\displaystyle=\{j\in\mathscr{H}_{u}:\Delta_{D}(\,w[j+m_{u}{\,..\,}|w|]\,)=\delta\}

\displaystyle=\{j\in\mathscr{H}_{v}\cup\mathscr{L}_{v}:\Delta_{D}(\,w[j+m_{u}{\,..\,}j+m_{v})\,)\odot\Delta_{D}(\,w[j+m_{v}{\,..\,}|w|]\,)=\delta\}

\displaystyle=\{j\in\mathscr{H}_{v}\cup\mathscr{L}_{v}:\tau\odot\Delta_{D}(\,w[j+m_{v}{\,..\,}|w|]\,)=\delta\}

\displaystyle=\bigcup_{\delta^{\prime}\colon\tau\odot\delta^{\prime}=\delta}\{j\in\mathscr{H}_{v}\cup\mathscr{L}_{v}:\Delta_{D}(\,w[j+m_{v}{\,..\,}|w|]\,)=\delta^{\prime}\}.\qquad(\natural)

Now, the partition
\mathscr{H}_{u}=\bigcup_{\delta\in\mathcal{T}_{D}}\lbag\delta\rbag_{u}
can be computed from
\mathscr{H}_{v}=\bigcup_{\delta\in\mathcal{T}_{D}}\lbag\delta\rbag_{v}
as follows:
First, for each
i\in\mathscr{L}_{v}
, we add
i
to the bag
\lbag\Delta_{D}(\,w[i+m_{v}{\,..\,}|w|)]\,)\rbag_{v}
.
Then,
\lbag\delta\rbag_{v}
is updated to the set

\{j\in\mathscr{H}_{v}\cup\mathscr{L}_{v}:\Delta_{D}(\,w[j+m_{v}{\,..\,}|w|]\,)=\delta\}.

Thus, the set
\lbag\delta\rbag_{u}
is obtained by

\bigcup_{\delta^{\prime}\colon\tau\odot\delta^{\prime}=\delta}\lbag\delta^{\prime}\rbag_{v}.

Now, to compute
\lbag\delta\rbag_{u}
, it suffices to merge the bags
\lbag\delta^{\prime}\rbag_{v}
.
We manage the partition by the following data structure defined as follows, which is similar to the disjoint-union structure in
[
24
, Sec. 21]
.

†
†
itembox-title:
Disjoint Set Union (DSU)
Data to Manage
: Disjoint sets
\{\mathcal{B}_{\lambda}\}_{x\in\Lambda}
indexed by a label set
\Lambda
.

Queries
:

MakeBag
(
\lambda
):

Register a new empty bag
\mathcal{B}_{\lambda}
and add
\lambda
to
\Lambda
.

Insert
(
\lambda,j
):

Add an element
j
to the bag
\mathcal{B}_{\lambda}
.

Merge
(
X
):

Merge all bags
\mathcal{B}_{\lambda}
with
\lambda\in X
into a single bag
\mathcal{B}_{\lambda^{*}}
with
\lambda^{*}\in X
and return a pair
(\lambda^{*},\mathit{moves})
, where
\mathit{moves}=\bigcup_{\lambda\in X\setminus\{\lambda^{*}\}}\mathcal{B}_{\lambda}
. All labels
\lambda
in
X\setminus\{\lambda^{*}\}
is removed from
\Lambda
together with the corresponding
\mathcal{B}_{\lambda}
.

If we adopt the small-to-large strategy in
Merge
(
\cdot
), it is easy to see that

•

MakeBag
(
\cdot
) and
Insert
(
\cdot
) queries run in
O(1)
time, and

•

Merge
(
\cdot
) query runs in amortized
O(\log s)
time, where
s:=|\Lambda|+|\bigcup_{\lambda\in\Lambda}\mathcal{B}_{\lambda}|
.

8.3
Necessary Queries

The remaining task is to give an algorithm to compute

\{\Delta_{C}(\,w[i+m_{v}{\,..\,}j)\,):j\in\lbag\delta\rbag_{v},\ i+m_{v}\leq j\},

for each accepting transition
\delta
.
To do this, we define the following data structure,
RightEnumerator
.

†
†
itembox-title:
RightEnumerator

Depending Global Objects

R
egex
C
, the entire string
w
, and factorization forest of
C
.

Shared Data

•

A set of indices
J
.

•

An index
\widehat{j}
.

method
\textsc{Initialize}_{\textrm{R}}(\widehat{j})

: Set
\widehat{w}\leftarrow[\widehat{j},|w|]
and
J\leftarrow\emptyset
.

method
\textsc{Add}_{\textrm{R}}(j)

:
J\leftarrow J\cup\{j\}
.

method
\textsc{Get}_{\textrm{R}}(i)

: Return the set
\{\Delta_{C}(\,w[i{\,..\,}j)\,)\>:\>j\in J,\ i\leq j\}
.

We defer to Section
9
the construction of the data structure, which processes
\textsc{Initialize}_{\textrm{R}}(\widehat{j})
,
\textsc{Add}_{\textrm{R}}(j)
, and
\textsc{Get}_{\textrm{R}}(i)
in
O(1)
time.
