Algorithm 2

Algorithm Overview for
Right-
B
Transition Enumeration
.

8.4
Algorithm Description

Our implementation of the algorithm for the
Right-
B
Transition Enumeration
is given in Algorithm
2
.
Algorithm iterates over
v\in\pi
in bottom-up order while maintaining

•

an integer
m
, which is the length of current string
\Gamma(v)
,

•

a DSU data structure and set
\Lambda
of labels, which is initially empty,

•

a mapping
\mathcal{M}
from bag labels
\lambda
to transitions
\delta
, where
\mathcal{M}[\lambda]=\delta
represents that the bag
\mathcal{B}_{\lambda}
in DSU data structure manages the bag
\lbag\delta\rbag_{v}
, and

•

RightEnumerator
data structure
\mathcal{S}[\lambda]
for each
\lambda\in\Lambda
.

Our algorithm manages the partition using two data structures, DSU and
RightEnumerator
.
More precisely, we manage

•

the partition
\bigcup_{\lambda\in\Lambda}\mathcal{B}_{\lambda}=\bigcup_{\lambda\in\Lambda}\lbag\mathcal{M}[\lambda]\rbag_{v}
itself using DSU, and

•

for each
\lambda
, the bag
\lbag\mathcal{M}[\lambda]\rbag_{v}
using the
RightEnumerator
data structure
\mathcal{S}[\lambda]
to enables us to use
\textsc{Add}_{\textrm{R}}(j)
and
\textsc{Get}_{\textrm{R}}(j)
queries on
\lbag\mathcal{M}[\lambda]\rbag_{v}
.

To simulate our equation
(\natural)
for updating, we first update transitions related to bags in the first loop (line
2
).
Then, we merge the bags designated by
\text{DSU.merge}(\Lambda_{\delta})
in line
2
.
We also update
\mathcal{S}[\lambda^{*}]
and
\Lambda
to reflect moves by the merging.

In the block from line
2
, we compute the set of adequate transitions corresponding to the case
v\in\pi
using
\textsc{Get}_{\textrm{R}}
.

Finally, in the loop defined in line
2
, we add each
i\in\mathscr{L}_{v}
to an appropriate bag
\lbag\delta\rbag
of a label
\lambda
with
\delta=\Delta_{D}(\,w[i+m_{v}{\,..\,}|w|)\,)
.
If such a bag does not exist, the algorithm adds a new bag consisting only of
i
.

The following lemma explicitly shows our loop invariants, which are somewhat straightforward from above discussion.
We give a formal proof for the sake of completeness.

Lemma 50
.

After merging required by moving to the current
v
, at the beginning of line
2
,
the following holds:

(i)

\{\mathcal{B}_{\lambda}\}_{\lambda\in\Lambda}
is a partition of
\mathscr{H}_{v}
.

(ii)

For each
\lambda\in\Lambda
and
j\in\mathcal{B}_{\lambda}
,
\Delta_{D}(\,w[j+m_{v}{\,..\,}|w|)\,)=\mathcal{M}[\lambda]
.

(iii)

For each
\delta\in\mathcal{T}_{D}
, there is at most one
\lambda\in\Lambda
with
\mathcal{M}[\lambda]=\delta
.

Proof.

We prove by induction.

Base Case:

When
v
is the bottom-most vertex of
\pi
, the conditions are satisfied because
\mathscr{H}_{v}=\emptyset
and
\Lambda=\emptyset
.

Induction Step:

As induction step, consider the step that we move-up to
u
from
v
and check the conditions on
u
.
By induction hypothesis, at the beginning of line
2
, those three conditions hold for
v
.
The loop from line
2
for
v
adds
\{i\in\mathscr{L}_{v}:\Delta_{D}(\,w[i+m_{v}{\,..\,}|w|]\,)=\mathcal{M}[\lambda]\}
to
\mathcal{B}_{\lambda}
for each
\lambda
.
Therefore, after the entire iteration for
v
(or equivalently, the beginning of the iteration for
u
),
the following holds for every
\lambda\in\Lambda
:

\begin{array}[]{lcl}\mathcal{B}_{\lambda}&=&\{j\in\mathscr{H}_{v}:\Delta_{D}(\,w[j+m_{v}{\,..\,}|w|]\,)=\mathcal{M}[\lambda]\}\cup\{i\in\mathscr{L}_{v}:\Delta_{D}(\,w[i+m_{v}{\,..\,}|w|]\,)=\mathcal{M}[\lambda]\}\\
&=&\{j\in\mathscr{H}_{u}:\Delta_{D}(\,w[j+m_{v}{\,..\,}|w|]\,)=\mathcal{M}[\lambda]\},\end{array}

since
u{-}v
is the heavy edge of
u
and thus
\mathscr{H}_{u}=\mathscr{H}_{v}\cup\mathscr{L}_{v}
.
It makes the condition (i) satisfied.

In the iteration for
u
, after processing the loop from line
2
,
the following holds for every
x\in X
and
j\in\mathcal{B}_{\lambda}
:

\begin{array}[]{lcl}\mathcal{M}[\lambda]&=&\Delta_{D}(\,\widehat{w}(m_{u}{\,..\,}m_{v}]\,)\odot\Delta_{D}(\,w[j+m_{v}{\,..\,}|w|]\,)\\
&=&\Delta_{D}(\,w[j+m_{u}{\,..\,}j+m_{v})\,)\odot\Delta_{D}(\,w[j+m_{v}{\,..\,}|w|]\,)\\
&=&\Delta_{D}(\,w[j+m_{u}{\,..\,}|w|]\,),\end{array}

where the second equality holds from
\widehat{w}[1,m_{v}]=w[j,j+m_{v})
for every
j\in\mathscr{H}_{u}
.
It makes the condition (ii) satisfied while keeping the condition (i).

Furthermore, for each
\delta\in\mathcal{T}_{C}
, the loop from line
2
merges all the bags labeled by
\Lambda_{\delta}
.
Since this makes condition (iii) satisfied while keeping the conditions (i) and (ii),
all the conditions hold for
u
at the beginning of the loop defined in line
2
.
∎

The following lemma proves the correctness of the algorithm.

Lemma 51
.

Algorithm
2
correctly solves
Right-
B
Transition Enumeration
.
More precisely, it returns the following set of transitions
\delta
of
\mathcal{T}_{C}
:

\left\{\delta=\Delta_{C}(\,w[i+m_{v}{\,..\,}j)\,):\begin{array}[]{l}v\in\pi,\ i\in\mathscr{L}_{v},\ j\in\mathscr{H}_{v},\ i+m_{v}\leq j,\\
w[0{\,..\,}i)\in\mathbb{L}\left(A\right),\\
w[i{\,..\,}i+m_{v})=w[j{\,..\,}j+m_{v})=\Gamma(v)\in\mathbb{L}\left(B\right),\\
w[j+m_{v}{\,..\,}|w|]\in\mathbb{L}\left(D\right)\end{array}\right\}.

Proof.

From the definition of
Get
R
(
\cdot
), line
2

adds transitions
\delta\in\mathcal{T}_{C}
to
Accum
if and only if there is a
j\in\mathcal{B}_{\lambda}
with
i+m_{v}\leq j
and
\Delta_{C}(\,w[i+m_{v}{\,..\,}j)\,)=\delta
.
This index
j
satisfies
w[j{\,..\,}j+m_{v})=w[i{\,..\,}i+m_{v})=\Gamma(v)
because
j\in\mathscr{H}_{v}
.
It also satisfies
w[j+m_{v}{\,..\,}|w|)\in\mathbb{L}\left(M_{D}\right)
because of the condition (ii) of Lemma
50
,
j\in\mathcal{B}_{\lambda}
, and the fact that
\mathcal{M}[\lambda]
is an accepting transition of
D
(line
2
).
Thus, all transitions the algorithm adds to
Accum
satisfy the condition of this lemma.

Conversely, if a vertex
v
and indices
i
and
j
satisfy the condition of the lemma,
the algorithm adds
\delta=\Delta_{C}(\,w[i+m_{v}{\,..\,}j)\,)
to
Accum
at line
2
at the loop corresponding to
v
,
i
, and
\lambda
,
where
\lambda\in\Lambda
is the unique bag label such that
j\in\mathcal{B}_{\lambda}
, which exists because of condition (i) of Lemma
50
.
∎

Time Complexity Analysis.

Now we analyze the time complexity.
First, we claim the following, which in particular implies that operations iterating over all
\lambda\in\Lambda
cost only a constant factor to the time complexity.

Lemma 52
.

At any moment of the Algorithm
2
, we have
|\Lambda|\leq|\mathcal{T}_{D}|
.

Proof.

|\Lambda|
only increases at line
2
.
From condition (iii) of Lemma
50
and the criterion of line
2
,
after adding a bag label to
\Lambda
, the value (transition)
\mathcal{M}[\lambda]
differs for all different labels
\lambda
.
Thus, we have
|\Lambda|\leq|\mathcal{T}_{D}|
.
∎

On the assumption that the
Right-Enumeration
data structure answers each query in constant time, the following holds.

Lemma 53
.

The time complexity of Algorithm
2
is bounded by
O(N\log N)

where
N=|\mathsf{ST}[\pi]|
. (Recall that
\mathsf{ST}[\pi]:=\mathsf{ST}[u]
for the topmost vertex of
\pi
.)

Proof.

We bound the total number of queries on
RightEnumerator
data structure the algorithm calls, which are the bottleneck of our algorithm.

Loop of line
2
.

Since we call
\textsc{Add}_{\textrm{R}}(j)
for
j\in\mathit{moved}
obtained by
\text{DSU}.\text{merge}
,
the total number of
\textsc{Add}_{\textrm{R}}
called by this loop is bound by the total number of moving throughout merging bags.
Such number is clearly bound by
O(N\log N)

due to the two facts that
1) the total number of elements managed by
DSU
is bounded by
N
;
2) each element is moved at most
\log N
times due to the small-to-large merging.

Loop of line
2
.

We call
\textsc{Get}_{\textrm{R}}
queries at most
|\Lambda|\leq|\mathcal{T}_{D}|
times for each
i\in\mathscr{L}_{v}
.
Thus, the number of
\textsc{Get}_{\textrm{R}}
queries in the algorithm is
|\mathcal{T}_{D}|
times the sum of
|\mathscr{L}_{v}|
for all
v\in\pi
, which is
|\mathcal{T}_{D}|\cdot N\leq O(N)
.

Loop of line
2
.

The total number of
\textsc{Add}_{\textrm{R}}
called by this loop is bounded by
\sum_{v\in\pi}|\mathscr{L}_{v}|=N
.

These arguments show that the time complexity of Algorithm
2
is bounded by
O(N\log N)
.
∎

9
Data Structure for Right-
B
Transition Enumeration

In this section, we give an implementation of the
RightEnumerator
data structure that processes
\textsc{Initialize}_{\textrm{R}}
,
\textsc{Add}_{\textrm{R}}
, and
\textsc{Get}_{\textrm{R}}
in constant time.
For the sake of readability, we here restate the function of
RightEnumerator
.

†
†
itembox-title:
RightEnumerator

Depending Global Objects

R
egex
C
, the entire string
w
, and factorization forest of
C
.

Shared Data

•

A set of indices
J
.

•

An index
\widehat{j}
.

method
\textsc{Initialize}_{\textrm{R}}(\widehat{j})

: Set
\widehat{w}\leftarrow[\widehat{j},|w|]
and
J\leftarrow\emptyset
.

method
\textsc{Add}_{\textrm{R}}(j)

:
J\leftarrow J\cup\{j\}
.

method
\textsc{Get}_{\textrm{R}}(i)

: Return the set
\{\Delta_{C}(\,w[i{\,..\,}j)\,)\>:\>j\in J,\ i\leq j\}
.

For each node
f
of the factorization forest, we denote the corresponding interval by
[\ell_{v},r_{v})
.
Moreover, if
f
is an idempotent node, we denote the corresponding transition by
e_{f}
.

9.1
Our Ideas for
RightEnumerator

Our Basic Idea.

Our data structure utilizes the factorization forest
\mathsf{FF}_{C,w}
(or simply
\mathsf{FF}
)
on the transition monoid
\mathcal{T}_{C}
and the string
w
, and maintains a set
J
of indices of
w
.
Our main strategy towards processing queries in constant time is to maintain the following set of transitions for each node
f\in\mathsf{FF}
:

\mathcal{D}_{f}:=\{\Delta_{C}(\,w[\ell_{f}{\,..\,}j)\,):j\in J,\ j\in[\ell_{f},r_{f})\}.

Consider answering
\textsc{Get}_{\textrm{R}}(i)
query using
\mathcal{D}_{f}
.
Let
X_{i}
be the set of nodes such that the (disjoint) union of intervals
[\ell_{f},r_{f})
over all
f\in X_{i}
is
[i,|w|]
: i.e.,
[i,|w|]=\bigcup_{f\in X_{i}}[\ell_{f},r_{f})
.

Then, the set of transitions that
\textsc{Get}_{\textrm{R}}(i)
should return can be written as follows:

\begin{array}[]{lcl}\{\Delta_{C}(\,w[i{\,..\,}j)\,):j\in J,\ i\leq j\}&=&\bigcup_{f\in X_{i}}\{\Delta_{C}(\,w[i{\,..\,}j)\,):j\in J,j\in[\ell_{f},r_{f})\}\\
&=&\bigcup_{f\in X_{i}}\{\Delta_{C}(\,w[i{\,..\,}\ell_{f})\,)\odot\Delta_{C}(\,w[\ell_{f}{\,..\,}j)\,):j\in J,j\in[\ell_{f},r_{f})\}\\
&=&\bigcup_{f\in X_{i}}\{\Delta_{C}(\,w[i{\,..\,}\ell_{f})\,)\odot\delta:\delta\in\mathcal{D}_{f}\}=\bigcup_{f\in X_{i}}\Delta_{C}(\,w[i{\,..\,}\ell_{f})\,)\odot\mathcal{D}_{f}\end{array}

which can be computed just by iterating each
f\in X_{i}
.

By the identity above, the target set can be obtained simply by iterating over the elements of
X_{i}
.
Hence, we need to have a small family
X_{i}
that exactly covers the suffix interval
[i{\,..\,}|w|]
.
Using the famous
segment tree
instead of the factorization forest yields such family
X_{i}
with
|X_{i}|=O(\log|w|)
, which is from the fact that the segment tree on a
|w|
-length array has a height
O(\log|w|)
.
This yields the desired
RightEnumerator
data structure that processes each query in
O(\log|w|)
time, which is suitable for our purpose if we allow an additional logarithmic factor on the time complexity of the whole algorithm.

For further acceleration, we employ the factorization forest, which has a constant height.
The issue here is that, since some of the idempotent nodes may have an unbounded number of children, the size of
X_{i}
is unbounded.
For example, consider the following factorization forest:
w
1
i-3
i-2
i-1
i
\cdots
\cdots
\cdots
|w|{-}1
|w|
[i{-}2\,{\,..\,}\,|w|{-}1]
[1\,{\,..\,}\,i{-}3]
[1\,{\,..\,}\,|w|]

In this case,
X_{i}
should have size
|w|-i+1=O(|w|)
because we cannot use any of the non-leaf nodes to exactly cover
[i{\,..\,}|w|]
.
Thus, we cannot directly iterate over all
f\in X_{i}
for the query
\textsc{Get}_{\textrm{R}}(i)
.

Using Idempotency.

The idea here is to process simultaneously almost all
f\in X_{i}
that are children of the same idempotent node.
Let
g
be an idempotent node with
i\in[\ell_{g},r_{g})
,
and let
f_{1},\ldots,f_{p},\dots,f_{q}

be the children of
g

in that order, where
X_{i}
contains
f_{p},\dots,f_{q}

6
6

6

Since
X_{i}
covers
[i{\,..\,}|w|]
, if
f_{p}\in X_{i}
, then
f_{p^{\prime}}\in X_{i}
for every
p^{\prime}>p
.
.
Let
e_{g}
be the idempotent transition corresponding to the node
g
; i.e.,
\Delta_{C}(\,w[\ell_{f_{t}},r_{f_{t}})\,)=e_{g}
for all
t\in\{1,\dots,q\}
.
We simultaneously process
f_{p+1},\dots,f_{q}\in X_{i}
using the following observation.

Lemma 54
.

We have

\bigcup_{t=p+1}^{q}\Delta_{C}(\,w[i{\,..\,}\ell_{f_{t}})\,)\odot\mathcal{D}_{f_{t}}=\Delta_{C}(\,w[i{\,..\,}\ell_{f_{p}})\,)\odot\bigcup_{t=p+1}^{q}\left(e_{g}\odot\mathcal{D}_{f_{t}}\right).

Proof.

We have the following equation:

\displaystyle\bigcup_{t=p+1}^{q}\Delta_{C}(\,w[i{\,..\,}\ell_{f_{t}})\,)\odot\mathcal{D}_{f_{t}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\bigcup_{t=p+1}^{q}\Delta_{C}(\,w[i{\,..\,}r_{f_{t-1}})\,)\odot\mathcal{D}_{f_{t}}\qquad(\because r_{f_{t-1}}=\ell_{f_{t}})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\bigcup_{t=p+1}^{q}\bigl(\Delta_{C}(\,w[i{\,..\,}\ell_{f_{p}})\,)\odot\Delta_{C}(\,w[\ell_{f_{p}}{\,..\,}r_{f_{p}})\,)\odot\cdots\odot\Delta_{C}(\,w[\ell_{f_{t-1}}{\,..\,}r_{f_{t-1}})\,)\ \bigr)\odot\mathcal{D}_{f_{t}}

\displaystyle\qquad(\because w[i{\,..\,}\ell_{f_{p}})w[\ell_{f_{p}}{\,..\,}r_{f_{p}})\cdots w[\ell_{f_{t-1}}{\,..\,}r_{f_{t-1}})=w[i{\,..\,}r_{f_{t-1}}))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\bigcup_{t=p+1}^{q}\Delta_{C}(\,w[i{\,..\,}\ell_{f_{p}})\,)\odot\bigl(\ \underbrace{e_{g}\odot\cdots\odot e_{g}}_{\text{$t-p$}}\ \bigr)\odot\mathcal{D}_{f_{t}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left\{\Delta_{C}(\,w[i{\,..\,}\ell_{f_{p}})\,)\odot(e_{g}\odot\Delta)\colon\Delta\in\bigcup_{t=p+1}^{q}\mathcal{D}_{f_{t}}\right\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Delta_{C}(\,w[i{\,..\,}\ell_{f_{p}})\,)\odot\bigcup_{t=p+1}^{q}\left(e_{g}\odot\mathcal{D}_{f_{t}}\right).\qed

The remaining task is to compute the set
\displaystyle\bigcup_{t=p+1}^{q}\left(e_{g}\odot\mathcal{D}_{f_{t}}\right)
efficiently.
To do so, we define the following
\mathrm{maxidx}
:

\begin{array}[]{lcl}\mathrm{maxidx}_{g}[\delta]&:=&\max\{t:1\leq t\leq q,\delta\in e_{g}\odot\mathcal{D}_{f_{t}}\}.\end{array}

where we define the max of the empty set is
-\infty
: i.e.,
\max\emptyset=-\infty
.
Then, we have the following.

Lemma 55
.

We have

\bigcup_{t=p+1}^{q}\left(e_{g}\odot\mathcal{D}_{f_{t}}\right)=\{\delta:\delta\in\mathcal{T}_{C},\ \mathrm{maxidx}_{g}[\delta]>p\}.

Proof.

(
\subseteq
):
Assume
\delta\in\displaystyle\bigcup_{t=p+1}^{q}\left(e_{g}\odot\mathcal{D}_{f_{t}}\right)
and let
t
be an index with
\delta\in e_{g}\odot\mathcal{D}_{f_{t}}
.
Then, we have
\mathrm{maxidx}_{g}[\delta]\geq t>p
.

(
\supseteq
):
Assume
t:=\mathrm{maxidx}_{g}[\delta]>p
.
Then,
\delta\in e_{g}\odot\mathcal{D}_{f_{t}}\subseteq\displaystyle\bigcup_{t=p+1}^{q}\left(e_{g}\odot\mathcal{D}_{f_{t}}\right)
.
∎

Combining Lemmas
54
and
55
immediately yields the following.

Lemma 56
.

We have

\bigcup_{t=p+1}^{q}\Delta_{C}(\,w[i{\,..\,}\ell_{f_{t}})\,)\odot\mathcal{D}_{f_{t}}=\{\Delta_{C}(\,w[i{\,..\,}\ell_{f_{p}})\,)\odot\delta:\delta\in\mathcal{T}_{C},\mathrm{maxidx}_{g}[\delta]>p\}.

Using
\mathrm{maxidx}
, the right hand side of the formula on Lemma
56
can be computed just by iterating over all
\delta\in\mathcal{T}_{C}
. Thus, we can compute the desired left hand side in constant time.

9.2
Implementation of
RightEnumerator

Remark.

To simplify our pseudocodes, we use
class
or
module
features supported by many programming languages.
We employ the object-oriented (Java) style; see Sections 1.1 and 1.2 of
[
98
]
.
Particularly, in this section, we write

•

S.\mathrm{add}(x)
for the operation of adding the element
x
to the set
S
,

•

f.\mathrm{parent}
to represent the parent of the node
f
,

•

g.\mathrm{numOfChildren}
to represent the number of children of the node
g
,

•

g.\mathrm{childOf}(p)
to represent the
p
-th child (from the left) of the node
g
(
1
-origin), and

•

g.\mathrm{childIdx}(f)
to represent, for two nodes
f
and
g
with
g=f.\mathrm{parent}
, an integer
p
such that
f
is the
p
-th child of
g
(from the left).

The Implementation.

Our data structure is given in Algorithm
3
,
which manages the sets
\mathcal{D}_{f}
for each node
f
and the value
\mathrm{maxidx}_{g}[\delta]
for each idempotent node
g
and
\delta\in\mathcal{T}_{C}
.

The constructor
\textsc{Initialize}_{\textrm{R}}
initializes
\mathcal{D}_{f}
and
\mathrm{maxidx}_{g}[\delta]
, which seems to consume
O(|w|)
time.
This issue can be bypassed as follows. Instead of initializing all nodes at once at the beginning, we create and initialize each node only when the data structure first accesses it.
Then,
Initialize
R
(
\cdot
) can be implemented to work in constant time without affecting the time complexity of other procedures.

method

Initialize
R
(
E_{C},\mathcal{F}
)

1

Input:

A
R
egex
C
and its factorization forest
\mathsf{FF}
, both are given by references or pointers

\mathcal{D}_{f}\leftarrow\emptyset
for each node
f
of
\mathsf{FF}
;

2

\mathrm{maxidx}_{g}[\delta]\leftarrow-\infty
for each idempotent node
g
of
\mathsf{FF}
and
\delta\in\mathcal{T}_{C}
;

3

method

Add
R
(
j
)

4

Input:

j\in[|w|]

f\leftarrow\text{the leaf node of $\mathsf{FF}$ corresponding to the index }j
;

5

while

f\text{ is not root}

do

6

g\leftarrow f\text{.parent}
;

7

\delta\leftarrow\Delta_{C}(\,w[\ell_{f}{\,..\,}j)\,)
;

8

\mathcal{D}_{f}.\text{add}(\delta)
;

9

if

g
is an idempotent node

then

10

p\leftarrow g.\text{childIdx}(f)
;

//
f
is
p
-th child of
g

\mathrm{maxidx}_{g}[e_{g}\odot\delta]\leftarrow\max(p,\mathrm{maxidx}_{g}[e_{g}\odot\delta])
;

11

f\leftarrow g
;

//
move up the factorization forest

method

Get
R
(
i
)

12

Input:

i\in[|w|]

f\leftarrow\text{the leaf node corresponding to the index }i
;

13

\textsc{Accum}\leftarrow\mathcal{D}_{f}
;

14

while

f\text{ is not root}

do

15

g\leftarrow f\text{.parent}
;

16

p\leftarrow g.\text{childIdx}(f)
;

//
f
is the
p
-th child of
g

if

p<g.\mathrm{numOfChildren}

then

//
f
is not the rightmost child of
g

17

f^{+}\leftarrow g.\text{childOf}(p+1)
;

//
f^{+}
is the immediate right sibling of
f

foreach

\delta\in\mathcal{D}_{f^{+}}

do

18

\textsc{Accum}.\text{add}(\Delta_{C}(\,w[i{\,..\,}\ell_{f^{+}})\,)\odot\delta)
;

19

if

g
is an idempotent node

then

20

foreach

\delta\in\mathcal{T}_{C}

do

21

if

p+1<\mathrm{maxidx}_{g}[\delta]

then

22

\textsc{Accum}.\text{add}(\Delta_{C}(\,w[i{\,..\,}\ell_{f^{+}})\,)\odot\delta)
;

23

f\leftarrow g
;

//
move up the factorization forest

return

Accum
;

24

Algorithm 3

Implementation of the
Right-Enumeration
Data Structure.

Now we prove the correctness of our implementation.
The following lemma ensures that the procedure
\textsc{Add}_{\textrm{R}}
correctly updates
\mathcal{D}_{f}
.

Lemma 57
.

Let
j\in[|w|]
.
Then, the query
\textsc{Add}_{\textrm{R}}(j)
adds
\Delta_{C}(\,w[\ell_{f}{\,..\,}j)\,)
to
\mathcal{D}_{f}
for all nodes
f
with
j\in[\ell_{f},r_{f})
(and only for those nodes).

Proof.

The loop defined in line
3
iterates over all nodes
f
with
j\in[\ell_{f},r_{f})
.
For each of such
f
, line
3
adds the transition
\Delta_{C}(\,w[\ell_{f}{\,..\,}j)\,)
to
\mathcal{D}_{f}
.
∎

Thus, we have the following, which is immediate from Lemma
57
.

Lemma 58
.

Let
J
be a set of indices.
Assume we have invoked
\textsc{Add}_{\textrm{R}}(j)
once for each
j\in J
, in any order.
Then, for every node
f
,

\mathcal{D}_{f}=\{\Delta_{C}(\,w[\ell_{f}{\,..\,}j)\,):j\in J,j\in[\ell_{f},r_{f})\}
.

We now ensure that the method
\textsc{Add}_{\textrm{R}}
correctly updates
\mathrm{maxidx}_{g}[\delta]
by the following lemma.

Lemma 59
.

Let
j\in[|w|]
.
Then, the query
\textsc{Add}_{\textrm{R}}(j)
updates
\mathrm{maxidx}_{g}[\delta]
by
\max(p,\mathrm{maxidx}_{g}[\delta])
for all idempotent node
g
with
j\in[\ell_{g},r_{g})
, where

•

\delta=e_{g}\odot\Delta_{C}(\,w[\ell_{f}{\,..\,}j)\,)
, where
f
is the unique child of
g
with
j\in[\ell_{f},r_{f})
, and

•

p
is the integer such that
f
is the
p
-th child of
g
,

and does not update other idempotent nodes.

Proof.

The loop of line
3
iterates over all
(g,f)
satisfying the condition of the lemma, and line
3
is processed when and only when
g
is idempotent,
f
is the
p
-th child of
g
, and
e_{g}\odot\delta=e_{g}\odot\Delta_{C}(\,w[\ell_{f}{\,..\,}j)\,)
.
∎

Now, we have the following.

Lemma 60
.

Let
J
be a set of indices.
Assume we have invoked
\textsc{Add}_{\textrm{R}}(j)
once for each
j\in J
, in any order.
Then, for each idempotent node
g
and
\delta\in\mathcal{T}_{C}
, we have

\mathrm{maxidx}_{g}[\delta]=\max\{t:1\leq t\leq q,\delta\in e_{g}\odot\mathcal{D}_{f_{t}}\}.

where
q
is the number of children of
g
and
f_{t}
is the
t
-th child of
g
.

Proof.

From Lemma
59
,

\mathrm{maxidx}_{g}[\delta]
is equal to the maximum among integers
t
such that there is an index
j\in J
with

•

j\in[\ell_{f_{t}},r_{f_{t}})\subseteq[\ell_{g},r_{g})
, where
f_{t}
is the
t
-th child of
g
, and

•

\delta=e_{g}\odot\Delta_{C}(\,w[\ell_{f_{t}}{\,..\,}j)\,)
.

From Lemma
58
, we have

\displaystyle\{e_{g}\odot\Delta_{C}(\,w[\ell_{f_{t}}{\,..\,}j)\,):j\in J\cap[\ell_{f_{t}},r_{f_{t}})\}=e_{g}\odot\mathcal{D}_{f_{t}}.

Thus, we have

\displaystyle\mathrm{maxidx}_{g}[\delta]

\displaystyle=\max\{t:1\leq t\leq q,\ \exists j\in J\cap[\ell_{f_{t}},r_{f_{t}}),\ \delta=e_{g}\odot\Delta_{C}(\,w[\ell_{f_{t}}{\,..\,}j)\,)\}

\displaystyle=\max\{t:1\leq t\leq q,\delta\in e_{g}\odot\mathcal{D}_{f_{t}}\}.\qed

The following lemma proves the correctness of the implementation of the method
\textsc{Get}_{\textrm{R}}
in Algorithm
2
.

Lemma 61
.

Let
i\in[|w|]
. Then,
\textsc{Get}_{\textrm{R}}(i)
returns the set
\{\Delta_{C}(\,w[i{\,..\,}j)\,):j\in J,\ i\leq j\}
.

Proof.

Get
R
(
i
) returns the union of

(i)

\mathcal{D}_{f}
for the leaf node
f
corresponding to the index
i
(line
3
),

(ii)

\Delta_{C}(\,w[i{\,..\,}\ell_{f^{+}})\,)\odot\mathcal{D}_{f^{+}}
for each node
f
with
i\in[\ell_{f},r_{f})

that is not the rightmost child of the parent of
f
, where
f^{+}
is the immediate right sibling of
f
(line
3
), and

(iii)

\{\Delta_{C}(\,w[i{\,..\,}\ell_{f^{+}})\,)\odot\delta:\delta\in\mathcal{T}_{C},\ \mathrm{maxidx}_{g}[\delta]>p+1\}

for each node
f
with
i\in[\ell_{f},r_{f})
that is
the non-rightmost
p
-th child of an idempotent node
g
,
where
f^{+}
is the immediate right sibling of
f
,
(p+1)
-th child of
g
(line
3
).

From now on, we rewrite these sets in a more manageable form.

(i).

We have

\mathcal{D}_{f}=\{\Delta_{C}(\,w[\ell_{f}{\,..\,}j)\,):j\in J\cap[\ell_{f},r_{f})\}=\{\Delta_{C}(\,w[i{\,..\,}j)\,):j\in J,\ \underline{j=i}\}

where the first equality is from Lemma
58
and the second equality follows from
[\ell_{f},r_{f})=\{i\}
.

(ii).

For each node
f
satisfying the condition of (ii), we have

\displaystyle\Delta_{C}(\,w[i{\,..\,}\ell_{f^{+}})\,)\odot\mathcal{D}_{f^{+}}

\displaystyle=\{\Delta_{C}(\,w[i{\,..\,}\ell_{f^{+}})\,)\odot\Delta_{C}(\,w[\ell_{f^{+}}{\,..\,}j)\,):j\in J\cap[\ell_{f^{+}},r_{f^{+}})\}

\displaystyle=\{\Delta_{C}(\,w[i{\,..\,}j)\,):j\in J\cap\underline{[\ell_{f^{+}},r_{f^{+}})}\},

where the first equality is from Lemma
58
.

(iii).

Let
g
be an idempotent node and
f_{1},\ldots,f_{p}=f,f_{p+1}={f^{+}},\ldots,f_{q}
be its children such that
i\in[\ell_{f},r_{f})
.
Since Lemma
60
ensures that
\mathrm{maxidx}_{g}
is correctly computed, we can apply Lemma
56
to obtain

\displaystyle\{\Delta_{C}(\,w[i{\,..\,}\ell_{f^{+}})\,)\odot\delta:\delta\in\mathcal{T}_{C},\ \mathrm{maxidx}_{g}[\delta]>p+1\}

\displaystyle=\bigcup_{t=p+2}^{q}\Delta_{C}(\,w[i{\,..\,}\ell_{f_{t}})\,)\odot\mathcal{D}_{f_{t}}

\displaystyle=\bigcup_{t=p+2}^{q}\Delta_{C}(\,w[i{\,..\,}\ell_{f_{t}})\,)\odot\{\Delta_{C}(\,w[\ell_{f_{t}}{\,..\,}j)\,):j\in J\cap[\ell_{f_{t}},r_{f_{t}})\}

\displaystyle=\bigcup_{t=p+2}^{q}\{\Delta_{C}(\,w[i{\,..\,}j)\,):j\in J\cap[\ell_{f_{t}},r_{f_{t}})\}

\displaystyle=\{\Delta_{C}(\,w[i{\,..\,}j)\,):j\in J\cap[\ell_{f_{p+2}},r_{f_{q}})\}

\displaystyle=\{\Delta_{C}(\,w[i{\,..\,}j)\,):j\in J\cap\underline{[r_{f^{+}},r_{g})}\}.

Now, it suffices to prove that the union of intervals

(I)

consisting only of an index
i
,

(II)

[\ell_{f^{+}},r_{f^{+}})
for each pair of nodes
(f,f^{+})
defined in (ii), and

(III)

[r_{f^{+}},r_{g})
for each tuple of nodes
(g,f,f^{+})
defined in (iii)

coincides with the interval
[i{\,..\,}|w|]
.

(
\subseteq[i{\,..\,}|w|]
):
Clearly, each interval is included in
[i{\,..\,}|w|]
.

(
\supseteq[i{\,..\,}|w|]
):
We prove that for each
j\in[i{\,..\,}|w|]
,
j
is in one of such intervals.
Let
g
be the lowest node of the factorization forest such that
i,j\in[\ell_{g},r_{g})
.

If
g
is a leaf node, we have
i=j
and the interval (I) contains
j
.
Otherwise, let
f
and
f^{\prime}
be the children of
g
such that
i\in[\ell_{f},r_{f})
and
j\in[\ell_{f^{\prime}},r_{f^{\prime}})
.
Since
g
is the lowest node with
i,j\in[\ell_{g},r_{g})
, we have
f\neq f^{\prime}
.
Let
f^{+}
be the node immediately at the right of
f
.

If
f^{\prime}=f^{+}
, the interval
[\ell_{f^{+}},r_{f^{+}})
in (II) for the pair
(f,f^{+})
contains
j
.
Otherwise,
g
has at least three children, and thus, is an idempotent node.
Thus, the interval
[r_{f^{+}},r_{g})
in (III) for the tuple
(g,f,f^{+})
contains
j
.
Therefore, the union of the intervals defined in (I), (II), and (III) coincides with
[i{\,..\,}|w|]
and the lemma is proved.
∎

Clearly, each query in Algorithm
3
runs in time proportional to the height of the factorization forest and
|\mathcal{T}_{C}|
, both of which are constants.
Therefore, each query runs in constant time, and thus, Algorithm
3
is an implementation of
Right-Enumeration
data structure with the desired property.

10
Algorithm Overview for Left-
B
Transition Enumeration

10.1
Overall Strategy

Here, we solve
Left-
B
Transition Enumeration
on a heavy-path
\pi
of the suffix tree
\mathsf{ST}_{w}
of
w
in
O(|\mathsf{ST}[\pi]|\log|w|)
-time.
Our algorithm iterates over
v\in\pi
in bottom-up order, and for each
v
with
\Gamma(v)\in\mathbb{L}\left(B\right)
, computes the following set of transitions for each
i\in\mathscr{L}_{v}
such that
w[i+m_{v}{\,..\,}|w|]\in\mathbb{L}\left(D\right)
:

\mathcal{D}_{\text{left}}:=\{\Delta_{C}(\,w[j+m_{v}{\,..\,}i)\,):j\in\mathscr{H}_{v},\ w[1{\,..\,}j)\in\mathbb{L}\left(A\right),j+m_{v}\leq i\}

where
m_{v}=|\Gamma(v)|
is the length of the associated string
\Gamma(v)
of the vertex
v
.

To compute
\mathcal{D}_{\text{left}}
in
O(\log|w|)
-time, we use a data structure,
LeftEnumerator
, defined on the ex
C
and the suffix
\widehat{w}
corresponding to the heavy-path
\pi
.
It provides queries
Initialize
L
(
\cdot
),
Add
L
(
\cdot
),
Shrink
L
(
\cdot
), and
Get
L
(
\cdot
) defined as follows.

†
†
itembox-title:
LeftEnumerator

Depending Global Data

R
egex
C
, the entire string
w
, and factorization forest of
C
.

Shared Data

•

a set of indices
J

•

a suffix
\widehat{w}

•

a non-negative integer
m

Providing the following queries:

Initialize
L
(
\widehat{j}
)

: Set
\widehat{w}\leftarrow w[\widehat{j}{\,..\,}|w|]
and
m\leftarrow n-\widehat{j}
.

Add
L
(
j
)

:
J\leftarrow J\cup\{j\}
. It is guaranteed that
w[j{\,..\,}j+m)
is a prefix of
\widehat{w}
.

Shrink
L
(
d
)

: Set
m\leftarrow m-d
. It is guaranteed that
d\geq 0
.

Get
L
(
i
)

: Return the set
\{\Delta_{C}(\,w[j+m{\,..\,}i)\,):j\in J,\ j+m\leq i\}
.

Procedure

LeftBTransitionEnumeration(
\pi
)

1

\mathit{enumerator}\leftarrow\textsc{LeftEnumerator}
;

2

\mathit{enumerator}
.
Initialize
L
(
\widehat{j}
), where
\widehat{j}
is the index corresponding to the leaf of
\pi
;

3

\textsc{Accum}\leftarrow\emptyset
;

4

foreach

v\in\pi
in bottom-up order

do

5

m_{v}\leftarrow|\Gamma(v)|
;

6

\mathit{enumerator}
.
Shrink
L
(
m-m_{v}
);

7

if

\Gamma(v)\in\mathbb{L}\left(B\right)

then

8

//
this check is in
O(1)
time using the factorization forest.

foreach

i\in\mathscr{L}_{v}

do

9

if

w[i+m_{v}{\,..\,}|w|]\in\mathbb{L}\left(D\right)

then

10

\mathcal{D}\leftarrow\mathit{enumerator}
.
Get
L
(
i
);

11

\textsc{Accum}\leftarrow\textsc{Accum}\cup\mathcal{D}
;

12

foreach

i\in\mathscr{L}_{v}
with
w[1{\,..\,}i)\in\mathbb{L}\left(A\right)

do

13

\mathit{enumerator}
.
Add
L
(
i
);

14

return

Accum
;

15

Algorithm 4

Algorithm Overview for
Left-
B
Transition Enumeration

We defer the implementation details of data structure
LeftEnumerator
, which processes each query in amortized
O(\log|w|)
time, to the subsequent sections.
We now present our algorithm for
Left-
B
Transition Enumeration
, as shown in Algorithm
4
.
As noted earlier
7.3
,
we begin by
J=\emptyset
and iterate over
v\in\pi
in bottom-up order. For each
v\in\pi
, we apply the following operations.

•

First, we set
m\leftarrow m_{v}
using a
Shrink
L
(
\cdot
) query (line
4
).

•

Then, assuming
w_{v}\in\mathbb{L}\left(B\right)
, for each
i\in\mathscr{L}_{v}
with
w[i+m{\,..\,}|w|]\in\mathbb{L}\left(D\right)
, we compute the set
\{\Delta_{C}(\,w[j+m{\,..\,}i)\,):j\in J,\ j+m\leq i\}
by calling
\textsc{Get}_{\mathrm{L}}(i)
(line
4
).

•

Finally, we properly update
J
by adding each
i\in\mathscr{L}_{v}
with
w[1{\,..\,}i)\in\mathbb{L}\left(A\right)
by calling
\textsc{Add}_{\mathrm{L}}(i)
(line
4
).

The following lemma ensures that the algorithm correctly updates the set
J
.

Lemma 62

(Loop Invariant)
.

Every visiting the line
4
of Algorithm
4
, the following holds:

\mathit{enumerator}.J=\{j\in\mathscr{H}_{v}:w[1{\,..\,}j)\in\mathbb{L}\left(A\right)\}.

Proof.

We prove by induction.
If
v
is the leaf of
\pi
, the statement is immediate from
\mathit{enumerator}.J=\mathscr{H}_{v}=\emptyset
.
Otherwise, let
u
be the child of
v
on
\pi
.
Since
\mathscr{H}_{v}=\mathscr{H}_{u}\cup\mathscr{L}_{u}
, we have

\{j\in\mathscr{H}_{v}:w[1{\,..\,}j)\in\mathbb{L}\left(A\right)\}=\{j\in\mathscr{H}_{u}:w[1{\,..\,}j)\in\mathbb{L}\left(A\right)\}\cup\{j\in\mathscr{L}_{v}:w[1{\,..\,}j)\in\mathbb{L}\left(A\right)\}.

By induction hypothesis, the first term of the right-hand side was equal to
\mathit{enumerator}.J
at the beginning of the loop for
u
.
The second term of the right-hand side is exactly the set that was added to
\mathit{enumerator}.J
while the loop for
u
in line
4
. Thus, the lemma is proved.
∎

The following lemma ensures the correctness of Algorithm
4
.

Lemma 63
.

Algorithm
4
correctly solves
Left-
B
Transition Enumeration
.
More precisely, it returns the following set of transitions
\delta
of
\mathcal{T}_{C}
:

\left\{\delta=\Delta_{C}(\,w[j+m_{v}{\,..\,}i)\,):\begin{array}[]{l}v\in\pi,\ i\in\mathscr{L}_{v},\ j\in\mathscr{H}_{v},\ j+m_{v}\leq i,\\
w[0{\,..\,}j)\in\mathbb{L}\left(A\right),\\
w[j{\,..\,}j+m_{v})=w[i{\,..\,}i+m_{v})=\Gamma(v)\in\mathbb{L}\left(B\right),\\
w[i+m_{v}{\,..\,}|w|]\in\mathbb{L}\left(D\right)\end{array}\right\}.

Proof.

From the definition of
Get
L
(
\cdot
), line
4
adds transitions
\delta\in\mathcal{T}_{C}
to
Accum
if and only if there is a
j\in J
with
j+m_{v}\leq i
and
\Delta_{C}(\,w[j+m_{v}{\,..\,}i)\,)=\delta
.
By Lemma
62
,
j
satisfies
w[1{\,..\,}j)\in\mathbb{L}\left(A\right)
and
j\in\mathscr{H}_{v}
.
Moreover, we have
w[j{\,..\,}j+m_{v})=w[i{\,..\,}i+m_{v})=\Gamma(v)
because
j\in\mathscr{H}_{v}
.
Line
4
ensures
w[i+m_{v}{\,..\,}|w|]\in\mathbb{L}\left(D\right)
.
Thus, all transitions the algorithm adds to
Accum
satisfy the condition of this lemma.

Conversely, if a vertex
v
and indices
i
and
j
satisfy the condition of the lemma, the algorithm adds
\delta=\Delta_{C}(\,w[j+m_{v}{\,..\,}i)\,)
to
Accum
at line
4
at the loop corresponding to
v
and
i
.
∎

Time Complexity of Algorithm
4
.

Now we analyze the time complexity.
It is clear that Algorithm
4
calls
O(|\mathsf{ST}[\pi]|)
number of queries.
Thus, using an implementation of the
Left-Enumeration
data structure that processes each of four types of queries in (amortized)
O(\log|w|)
time, we can bound the time complexity of Algorithm
4
by
O(|\mathsf{ST}[\pi]|\log|w|)
.

10.2
Data Structure Overview for
LeftEnumerator

To solve
Left-
B
Transition Enumeration
, it suffices to implement
LeftEnumerator
.
Unfortunately, it does not seem easy to realize this data structure just by using factorization forests as we did in Section
9
, due to the presence of the
Shrink
L
(
\cdot
) queries.

To this end, we consider an auxiliary data structure,
AuxiliaryEnumerator
, that efficiently processes queries that are somewhat weaker than our purpose.
Then, we construct
LeftEnumerator
using
AuxiliaryEnumerator
.
The precise definition of
AuxiliaryEnumerator
is as follows.

†
†
itembox-title:
AuxiliaryEnumerator

Depending Global Data

R
egex
C
, the entire string
w
, and factorization forest of
C
.

Shared Data

•

a set of
intervals

\mathcal{J}=\{[j_{1},k_{1}),[j_{2},k_{2}),\ldots\}

•

a suffix
\widehat{w}

•

a non-negative integer
m

Providing the following queries or methods:

Initialize
A
(
\widehat{j}
)

: Set
\widehat{w}\leftarrow[\widehat{j},n)
and
m\leftarrow n-\widehat{j}
.

Add
A
(
j,k
)

:
\mathcal{J}\leftarrow\mathcal{J}\cup\{[j,k)\}
. It is guaranteed that
j+m\leq k
and
w[j{\,..\,}k)
is a prefix of
\widehat{w}
.

Shrink
A
(
d
)

: Set
m\leftarrow m-d
. It is guaranteed that
d\geq 0
.

Get
A
(
i
)

: Return the set

\{\mathcal{T}_{C}(\,w[j+m{\,..\,}i)\,):[j,k)\in\mathcal{J},\ k\leq i\}
.

Here, we explain how
AuxiliaryEnumerator
differs from
LeftEnumerator
.

First Difference: Interval.

The first difference is that, whereas
LeftEnumerator
maintains a set of indices
J
, the auxiliary data structure
AuxiliaryEnumerator
maintains a set of intervals
\mathcal{J}
.
Due to this difference, the
\textsc{Add}_{\mathrm{A}}(j,k)
query takes an additional argument
k\geq j+m
compared to the
\textsc{Add}_{\mathrm{L}}(j)
query, where
k
is the index of the right endpoint of the interval.
Note that each interval
[j,k)\in\mathcal{J}
always satisfies
j+m\leq k
, since this condition holds when that interval is added to
\mathcal{J}
and
m
never increases thereafter.

Second Difference: Outputs of
Get
queries.

The second difference is what
Get
(
\cdot
) queries return.
Recall that
Get
L
(
i
) returns:

\{\Delta_{C}(\,w[j+m{\,..\,}i)\,):j\in J,\ j+m\leq i\}.

On the other hand,
Get
A
(
i
) returns:

\{\Delta_{C}(\,w[j+m{\,..\,}i)\,):[j,k)\in\mathcal{J},\ k\leq i\}.

Using the notation

J_{\mathrm{L}}:=\{j\in J:j+m\leq i\},\quad J_{\mathrm{A}}:=\{j:[j,k)\in\mathcal{J},\ k\leq i\},

we can write

\textsc{Get}_{\mathrm{L}}(i)=\{\Delta_{C}(\,w[j+m{\,..\,}i)\,):j\in J_{L}\},\quad\textsc{Get}_{\mathrm{A}}(i)=\{\Delta_{C}(\,w[j+m{\,..\,}i)\,):j\in J_{A}\}.

The definition of
J_{\mathrm{L}}
depends on
m
, so whether
j\in J_{\mathrm{L}}
may change as a result of
\textsc{Shrink}_{\mathrm{L}}
queries.
On the other hand, the definition of
J_{\mathrm{A}}
does not depend on
m
, and thus whether
[j,k)\in J_{\mathrm{A}}
remains invariant by
\textsc{Shrink}_{\mathrm{L}}
queries.
This is the main reason we work with
\textsc{Get}_{\mathrm{A}}(\cdot)
instead of
\textsc{Get}_{\mathrm{L}}(\cdot)
;
\textsc{Get}_{\mathrm{A}}(\cdot)
is easier to compute.

Simulating
\textsc{Get}_{\mathrm{L}}(i)
using
\textsc{Get}_{\mathrm{A}}(i)
.

The remaining part of this section focuses on how to simulate
LeftEnumerator
using
AuxiliaryEnumerator
.
To do so, we need to simulate
\textsc{Get}_{\mathrm{L}}(\cdot)
using
\textsc{Get}_{\mathrm{A}}(\cdot)
.
In our construction, we manage the following condition:

J=\{j:[j,k)\in\mathcal{J}\}.

Particularly, since
j+m\leq k
holds for all
[j,k)\in\mathcal{J}
, we have
J_{\mathrm{A}}\subseteq J_{\mathrm{L}}
.
Therefore, the remaining task is to compute

\textsc{Get}_{\text{near}}(i):=\{\Delta_{C}(\,w[j+m{\,..\,}i)\,):j\in J_{\mathrm{near}}\}

for some
J_{\mathrm{near}}
with
J_{\mathrm{L}}\setminus J_{\mathrm{A}}\subseteq J_{\mathrm{near}}\subseteq J_{\mathrm{L}}
.
Then, we can compute
\textsc{Get}_{\mathrm{L}}(i)
by

\textsc{Get}_{\mathrm{L}}(i)=\textsc{Get}_{\mathrm{A}}(i)\cup\textsc{Get}_{\text{near}}(i).

We note that the definition of
J_{\mathrm{near}}
has some flexibility; particularly, it may have non-empty intersection with
J_{\mathrm{A}}
.
Here, we adopt the definition

J_{\mathrm{near}}:=\{j\in J\colon j+m\leq i<j+2m\}.

Clearly, we have
J_{\mathrm{near}}\subseteq J_{\mathrm{L}}
.

Let

k^{*}_{j}:=\min_{k\colon[j,k)\in\mathcal{J}}k.

Then, if we have
k^{*}_{j}\leq j+2m
for all
j\in J
, we have

J_{\mathrm{L}}\setminus J_{\mathrm{A}}=\{j\colon j\in J\colon j+m\leq i<k^{*}_{j}\}\subseteq\{j\colon j\in J\colon j+m\leq i<j+2m\}=J_{\mathrm{near}}.

Thus, the remaining tasks are:

(i)

implementing
AuxiliaryEnumerator
,

(ii)

implementing
\textsc{Get}_{\text{near}}(\cdot)
, and

(iii)

ensuring that
k^{*}_{j}\leq j+2m
holds for every moment of the algorithm.

Here, we focus on (iii) and defer (i) and (ii) to Section
11
and Section
12
, respectively.

Ensuring (iii).

The issue in ensuring (iii) is that
\textsc{Shrink}_{\mathrm{A}}(\cdot)
may cause (iii) to fail by decreasing
m
.
We overcome this issue by the following idea: if
\textsc{Shrink}_{\mathrm{A}}(\cdot)
breaks (iii) for some
j\in J
, then we update
k^{*}_{j}
to
j+m
by adding to
\mathcal{J}
the interval
[j,j+m)
using new
m
.

Note that this re-registration scheme clearly preserves the condition

J=\{j\colon[j,k)\in\mathcal{J}\}

because we add
[j,k)
to
\mathcal{J}
only when
j\in J
.
Moreover, once a
\textsc{Shrink}_{\mathrm{A}}(\cdot)
query updates
k^{*}_{j}:=j+m
by calling
\textsc{Add}_{\mathrm{A}}(j,j+m)
, next update will happen only after
m
is halved; it requires the condition
j+2m<k^{*}_{j}
.
Thus, we call at most
O(\log|w|)

Add
A
(
\cdot
) queries in total for each call of
\textsc{Add}_{\mathrm{L}}(\cdot)
query, and particularly, the re-registration scheme costs
O(\log|w|)
to the total time complexity.

Algorithm 5

Implementation of
LeftEnumerator
Using the Auxiliary Data Structure
AuxiliaryEnumerator

10.3
Implementing
LeftEnumerator
using
AuxiliaryEnumerator

Implementation.

Here, we formally implement the idea in Section
10.2
.
Our implementation is given in Algorithm
5
.
It internally uses
AuxiliaryEnumerator
and a procedure
GetNearTransitions
(
i,m
), which computes the set
\textsc{Get}_{\text{near}}(i)
for current
m
.
To manage the “re-registered”
\textsc{Add}_{\mathrm{A}}(\cdot)
queries, we use a queue
Q
.
As in Section
9
, we use class or module features in our pseudocode.
Specifically, we write

•

Q.\mathrm{pushBack}(x)
for the operation of adding
x
to the last of the queue
Q
,

•

Q.\mathrm{popFront}()
for the operation of removing the first element of the queue
Q
, and

•

Q.\mathrm{front}()
for the first element of the queue
Q
.

Correctness proof.

The following lemma is straightforward and specifies our invariants.

Lemma 64
.

Let
J
be a set of indices.
Assume we have invoked

•

Initialize
L
(
\cdot
) once at the beginning,

•

\textsc{Add}_{\mathrm{L}}(j)
once for each
j\in J
in any order, and

•

\textsc{Shrink}_{\mathrm{L}}(\cdot)
and
\textsc{Get}_{\mathrm{L}}(\cdot)
for any number of times.

Let
\mathcal{J}
be the set of intervals
[j,k)
that
\textsc{Add}_{\mathrm{A}}(j,k)
is called.
Then, at any moment, we have the following.

(i)

J=\{j:[j,k)\in\mathcal{J}\}
.

(ii)

j+m\leq k^{*}_{j}\leq j+2m
holds for each
j\in J
, where
k^{*}_{j}=\min_{k\colon[j,k)\in\mathcal{J}}k
.

(iii)

If
m=0
, we have
Q=\emptyset
. Otherwise,
J=\{j:(j,m^{\prime})\in Q\}
.

(iv)

Every pair
(j,m^{\prime})\in Q
satisfies
m\leq m^{\prime}<2m
.

(v)

The second elements of
Q
is non-increasing: i.e.,
m_{1}\geq m_{2}\geq\cdots\geq m_{|Q|}
for
Q=[(j_{1},m_{1}),(j_{2},m_{2}),\ldots,(j_{|Q|},m_{|Q|})]

Proof.

We prove by induction.
Since
Initialize
L
(
\cdot
) sets
J=\mathcal{J}=Q=\emptyset
, at the beginning of the algorithm, the conditions hold.
For the remaining three methods, we assume that the conditions are satisfied before they are invoked, and we show that they remain satisfied after the execution.

\textsc{Get}_{\mathrm{L}}(\cdot)
.

All conditions are clearly preserved because
Get
L
(
\cdot
) updates no data.

\textsc{Add}_{\mathrm{L}}(j)
.

The conditions (i) and (ii) are preserved because
\textsc{Add}_{\mathrm{L}}(j)
just adds
j
to
J
and
[j,j+m)
to
\mathcal{J}
without updating
m
.
The conditions (iii) and (iv) are preserved because
Q
is not updated when
m=0
and
(j,m)
is added to
Q
when
m>0
.
The condition (v) is preserved because of the facts that
m_{|Q|}\geq m
held before processing the query, which is from the condition (iv) of the induction hypothesis, and
(j,m)
is added to the last of
Q
.

\textsc{Shrink}_{\mathrm{L}}(d)
.

The condition (i) is preserved because
\textsc{Shrink}_{\mathrm{L}}(d)
adds an interval
[j,j+m)
to
\mathcal{J}
only when
(j,m^{\prime})
was already in
Q
for some
m^{\prime}
, which ensures
j\in J
because of the condition (iii) of the induction hypothesis.
For the remaining conditions, we branch into two cases: whether
m
becomes
0
at line
5
or not.

m
becomes
0
.

From the condition (iii) of the induction hypothesis, we have
J=\{j\colon(j,m^{\prime})\in Q\}
before processing the query.
Moreover, from the condition (iv) of the induction hypothesis, the condition
0=2m<m^{\prime}
at line
5
holds for all
(j,m^{\prime})\in Q
.
Thus, the method adds
[j,j)
to
\mathcal{J}
and sets
k^{*}_{j}=j
for all
j\in J
, which makes the condition (ii) satisfied.
Moreover, the method removes all elements of
Q
and adds no element to
Q
, which makes the conditions (iii), (iv), and (v) satisfied.

m
remains positive.

From the condition (v) of the induction hypothesis, the condition
2m<m^{\prime}
at line
5
holds for an initial segment of
Q
(in order from the front), and is not satisfied for the remaining elements. Thus, the method removes all elements
(j,m^{\prime})
with
2m<m^{\prime}
from
Q
and instead adds
(j,m)
at the last of
Q
.
That is, using the largest index
x
of
Q
with
2m<m_{x}
,
Q
becomes

Q=[(j_{x+1},m_{x+1}),\ldots(j_{|Q|},m_{|Q|}),(j_{1},m),(j_{2},m),\ldots,(j_{x},m)].

This sets
k^{*}_{j}:=m
for each
j\in\{j_{1},\dots,j_{x}\}
, which makes the condition (ii) satisfied.
The condition (iii) is preserved because the set
\{j\colon(j,m^{\prime})\in Q\}
does not change.
The condition (iv) is clear from the definition of
x
.
The condition (v) is from
m_{x+1}\geq\dots\geq m_{|Q|}\geq m
, which is from the conditions (iv) and (v) of the induction hypothesis.
∎

Now, we prove the correctness of Algorithm
5
.

Lemma 65
.

Get
L
(
i
) returns the following set of transitions:

\{\Delta_{C}(\,w[j+m{\,..\,}i)\,):j\in J,\ j+m\leq i\}.

Proof.

From the condition (i) of Lemma
64
, we have

\displaystyle\textsc{Get}_{\mathrm{A}}(i)

\displaystyle=\left\{\Delta_{C}(\,w[j+m{\,..\,}i)\,):[j,k)\in\mathcal{J},\ k\leq i\right\}=\left\{\Delta_{C}(\,w[j+m{\,..\,}i)\,):j\in J,\ k^{*}_{j}\leq i\right\}.

Moreover, from the condition (ii) of Lemma
64
, we have

\displaystyle\left\{\Delta_{C}(\,w[j+m{\,..\,}i)\,):j\in J,\ j+2m\leq i\right\}

\displaystyle\subseteq\left\{\Delta_{C}(\,w[j+m{\,..\,}i)\,):j\in J,\ k^{*}_{j}\leq i\right\}

\displaystyle\subseteq\left\{\Delta_{C}(\,w[j+m{\,..\,}i)\,):j\in J,\ j+m\leq i\right\}.

Thus, we have

\displaystyle\textsc{Get}_{\mathrm{L}}(i)

\displaystyle=\textsc{Get}_{\mathrm{A}}(i)\cup\textsc{GetNearTransitions}(i,m)

\displaystyle=\left\{\Delta_{C}(\,w[j+m{\,..\,}i)\,):j\in J,\ k^{*}_{j}\leq i\right\}\cup\left\{\Delta_{C}(\,w[j+m{\,..\,}i)\,):j\in J,\ j+m\leq i<j+2m\right\}

\displaystyle=\left\{\Delta_{C}(\,w[j+m{\,..\,}i)\,):j\in J,\ j+m\leq i\right\}.\qed

On Time Complexity.

Now we analyze the time complexity.
From the discussion in Sections
11
and
12
, each query of
AuxiliaryEnumerator
and the procedure
GetNearTransitions
(
\cdot
) runs in constant time.
Thus,
Initialize
L
(
\cdot
) and
Get
L
(
\cdot
) runs in constant time.
To analyze the remaining queries, we define the potential
\Phi(Q)
by
\sum_{(j,m^{\prime})\in Q}(1+\log_{2}m^{\prime})
.
Then, each call of
Add
L
(
\cdot
) increases
\Phi(Q)
by at most
1+\log_{2}|w|\leq O(\log|w|)
, while each step of the loop in
Shrink
L
(
\cdot
) defined by line
5
decreases
\Phi(Q)
by at least
1
.
Therefore,
Add
L
(
\cdot
) and
Shrink
L
(
\cdot
) runs in amortized
O(\log|w|)
-time and
O(1)
-time, respectively.

11
Implementing
AuxiliaryEnumerator

In this section, we implement
AuxiliaryEnumerator
, the data structure introduced in Section
10.2
.
It supports queries
\textsc{Shrink}_{\mathrm{A}}
,
\textsc{Add}_{\mathrm{A}}
, and
\textsc{Get}_{\mathrm{A}}
in
O(1)
time.
Some of the ideas in this section are common to
RightEnumerator
in Section
9
, so we recommend reading that section first.

†
†
itembox-title:
AuxiliaryEnumerator

Depending Global Data

R
egex
C
, the entire string
w
, and factorization forest of
C
.

Shared Data

•

a set of
intervals

\mathcal{J}=\{[j_{1},k_{1}),[j_{2},k_{2}),\ldots\}

•

a suffix
\widehat{w}

•

a non-negative integer
m

Providing the following queries or methods:

Initialize
A
(
\widehat{j}
)

: Set
\widehat{w}\leftarrow[\widehat{j},n)
and
m\leftarrow n-\widehat{j}
.

Add
A
(
j,k
)

:
\mathcal{J}\leftarrow\mathcal{J}\cup\{[j,k)\}
. It is guaranteed that
j+m\leq k
and
w[j{\,..\,}k)
is a prefix of
\widehat{w}
.

Shrink
A
(
d
)

: Set
m\leftarrow m-d
. It is guaranteed that
d\geq 0
.

Get
A
(
i
)

: Return the set

\{\mathcal{T}_{C}(\,w[j+m{\,..\,}i)\,):[j,k)\in\mathcal{J},\ k\leq i\}
.

11.1
Overview

Differences from
RightEnumerator
.

Although
AuxiliaryEnumerator
processes queries similar to
RightEnumerator
, the following points are different:

•

The
Get
A
(
i
) query in
AuxiliaryEnumerator
searches for indices
j
to the left of a given index
i
: i.e.,
[j,i)
, whereas
Get
R
(
i
) query in
RightEnumerator
searches for indices
j
to the right of
i
: i.e.,
[i,j)
.

•

AuxiliaryEnumerator
maintains a set
\mathcal{J}
of intervals
[j,k)
, not a set
J
of indices
j
. Moreover, it is guaranteed that, for each
[j,k)\in\mathcal{J}
,

–

j+m\leq k
holds when it is added to
\mathcal{J}
, and

–

w[j{\,..\,}k)
is a prefix of pre-determined string
\widehat{w}:=w[\widehat{j}{\,..\,}|w|]
.

•

AuxiliaryEnumerator
maintains an integer
m
, which decreases over time via
Shrink
A
(
\cdot
) method and influences the output of
Get
A
(
\cdot
) method.

Defining
\mathcal{D}_{f}
and
\mathrm{minidx}_{f}
.

As in the case of
RightEnumerator
, we utilize the factorization forest of
C
, where each node
f
corresponds to the interval
[\ell_{f},r_{f})
.
We define the
\mathcal{D}_{f}
and
\mathrm{minidx}_{f}
, which correspond to
\mathcal{D}_{f}
and
\mathrm{maxidx}_{f}
in
RightEnumerator
, respectively.
Precisely, for each node
f
, we define

\mathcal{D}_{f}:=\{\Delta_{C}(\,w[j+m{\,..\,}r_{f})\,):[j,k)\in\mathcal{J},\ k\in[\ell_{f},r_{f})\},

and for each idempotent node
g
and
\delta\in\mathcal{T}_{C}
, we define

\mathrm{minidx}_{g}[\delta]:=\min\{t:1\leq t\leq q,\ \delta\in\mathcal{D}_{f_{t}}\odot e_{g}\},

where
q
is the number of children of
g
and
f_{t}
is the
t
-th child of
g
.
Note that the definitions of these values depend on
m
; in particular, they change as a result of
\textsc{Shrink}_{\mathrm{A}}
queries.

For convenience, we also introduce the following notation:

\mathcal{J}_{f}:=\{\,[j,k)\in\mathcal{J}:k\in[\ell_{f},r_{f})\}.

In words,
\mathcal{J}_{f}
is the set of intervals of
\mathcal{J}
whose right end is covered by
f
.
Using this notation, we can rewrite
\mathcal{D}_{f}
as follows:

\mathcal{D}_{f}=\{\Delta_{C}(\,w[j+m{\,..\,}r_{f})\,):[j,k)\in\mathcal{J}_{f}\}.

Lazy Update Strategy.

If we manage
\mathcal{D}_{f}
and
\mathrm{minidx}_{g}
, then we can implement the
\textsc{Get}_{\mathrm{A}}
query in a way similar to the
RightEnumerator
(see Section
11.2
for a formal proof).
However, it seems difficult to manage these values fully, because they depend on
m
, and every
\textsc{Shrink}_{\mathrm{A}}
query requires updating all of them.

To resolve this issue, we adopt the so-called
lazy update
strategy; we postpone updating the values of
\mathcal{D}_{f}
and
\mathrm{minidx}_{g}[\cdot]
until they are actually accessed.
Precisely, we introduce two new methods
\textsc{Update}_{\mathrm{A}}(f)
and
\textsc{UpdateIdem}_{\mathrm{A}}(g)
, which updates the values of
\mathcal{D}_{f}
and
\mathrm{minidx}_{g}[\cdot]
, respectively, to their correct values.
Whenever the algorithm refers to these values (in both
\textsc{Add}_{\mathrm{A}}
and
\textsc{Get}_{\mathrm{A}}
queries), the corresponding
\textsc{Update}_{\mathrm{A}}(f)
or
\textsc{UpdateIdem}_{\mathrm{A}}(g)
is called once immediately beforehand.

For each node
f
, let
m_{f}
be the value of
m
at the time when
\textsc{Update}_{\mathrm{A}}(f)
was last called (we explicitly manage this value
m_{f}
together with the value described below).
Since
m
only decreases, we always have
m\leq m_{f}
.
Instead of maintaining
\mathcal{D}_{f}
, we manage the following:

\mathcal{D}^{\mathrm{L}}_{f}:=\{\Delta_{C}(\,w[j+\underline{m_{f}}{\,..\,}r_{f})\,):[j,k)\in\mathcal{J}_{f}\}.

The only difference from
\mathcal{D}_{f}
is the underlined part
m_{f}
, which was
m
in
\mathcal{D}_{f}
.

Similarly, for each idempotent node
g
, let
m^{\mathrm{Idem}}_{g}
be the value of
m
at the time when
\textsc{UpdateIdem}_{\mathrm{A}}(g)
was last called (similarly, we explicitly manage this value).
Since
m
only decreases, we always have
m\leq m^{\mathrm{Idem}}_{g}
.
Then, we manage the following:

\mathrm{minidx}^{\mathrm{L}}_{g}[\delta]:=\min\left\{t:1\leq t\leq q,\ \delta\in\underline{\{\Delta_{C}(\,w[j+m^{\mathrm{Idem}}_{g}{\,..\,}r_{f_{t}})\,)\colon[j,k)\in\mathcal{J}_{f_{t}}\}}\odot e_{g}\right\},

where
q
is the number of children of
g
and
f_{t}
is the
t
-th child of
g
.
Note that the underlined part does not necessarily coincide with
\mathcal{D}^{\mathrm{L}}_{f_{t}}
because it evaluates
w[j+m^{\mathrm{Idem}}_{g}{\,..\,}r_{f_{t}})
, not
w[j+m_{f_{t}}{\,..\,}r_{f_{t}})
.

Since the definition of
\mathcal{D}^{\mathrm{L}}_{f}
and
\mathrm{minidx}^{\mathrm{L}}_{g}[\cdot]
do not depend on
m
, these values do not change via
\textsc{Shrink}_{\mathrm{A}}
queries.
Moreover, if
m_{f}=m
,
\mathcal{D}^{\mathrm{L}}_{f}
coincides
\mathcal{D}_{f}
, and if
m^{\mathrm{Idem}}_{g}=m
,
\mathrm{minidx}^{\mathrm{L}}_{g}[\cdot]
coincides
\mathrm{minidx}_{g}[\cdot]
.

Implementing
\textsc{Update}_{\mathrm{A}}
and
\textsc{UpdateIdem}_{\mathrm{A}}
.

Now, we explain how to implement
\textsc{Update}_{\mathrm{A}}
and
\textsc{UpdateIdem}_{\mathrm{A}}
.
We here use the invariant that, at any moment, for all nodes
f
and
[j,k)\in\mathcal{J}_{f}
,

j+m_{f}\leq k,\quad w[\widehat{j}{\,..\,}\widehat{j}+m_{f})=w[j{\,..\,}j+m_{f}),

and for all idempotent node
g
and
[j,k)\in\mathcal{J}_{g}
,

j+m^{\mathrm{Idem}}_{g}\leq k,\quad w[\widehat{j}{\,..\,}\widehat{j}+m^{\mathrm{Idem}}_{g})=w[j{\,..\,}j+m^{\mathrm{Idem}}_{g}).

These invariants are from the precondition of
\textsc{Add}_{\mathrm{A}}(j,k)
, that is,
j+m\leq k
and
w[j{\,..\,}k)
is a prefix of
\widehat{w}=w[\widehat{j}{\,..\,}|w|]
.
Nevertheless, we defer the formal proofs of those invariants after we describe the precise implementation of
AuxiliaryEnumerator
.

The following lemma gives the update formula of
\mathcal{D}^{\mathrm{L}}_{f}
.

Lemma 66
.

Let
f
be a node and assume

j+m_{f}\leq k,\quad w[\widehat{j}{\,..\,}\widehat{j}+m_{f})=w[j{\,..\,}j+m_{f})

holds for all
[j,k)\in\mathcal{J}_{f}
.
Then, we have

\mathcal{D}_{f}=\Delta_{C}(\,w[\widehat{j}+m{\,..\,}\widehat{j}+m_{f})\,)\odot\mathcal{D}^{\mathrm{L}}_{f}.

Proof.

We have

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}_{f}

\displaystyle=\{\Delta_{C}(\,w[j+m{\,..\,}r_{f})\,):[j,k)\in\mathcal{J}_{f}\}

\displaystyle=\{\Delta_{C}(\,w[j+m{\,..\,}j+m_{f})\,)\odot\Delta_{C}(\,w[j+m_{f}{\,..\,}r_{f})\,):[j,k)\in\mathcal{J}_{f}\}

\displaystyle=\Delta_{C}(w[\widehat{j}+m{\,..\,}\widehat{j}+m_{f}))\odot\{\Delta_{C}(\,w[j+m_{f}{\,..\,}r_{f})\,):[j,k)\in\mathcal{J}_{f}\}

\displaystyle=\Delta_{C}(w[\widehat{j}+m{\,..\,}\widehat{j}+m_{f}))\odot\mathcal{D}^{\mathrm{L}}_{f},

where the second equality follows from
j+m\leq j+m_{f}\leq k\leq r_{f}
, and the third equality follows from
w[\widehat{j}{\,..\,}\widehat{j}+m_{f})=w[j{\,..\,}j+m_{f})
.
∎

The following lemma gives the update formula of
\mathrm{minidx}^{\mathrm{L}}_{g}
.

Lemma 67
.

Let
g
be an idempotent node and assume

j+m^{\mathrm{Idem}}_{g}\leq k,\quad w[\widehat{j}{\,..\,}\widehat{j}+m^{\mathrm{Idem}}_{g})=w[j{\,..\,}j+m^{\mathrm{Idem}}_{g})

holds for all
[j,k)\in\mathcal{J}_{g}
.
Then, for each
\delta\in\mathcal{T}_{C}
, we have

\mathrm{minidx}_{g}[\delta]=\min_{\delta^{\prime}\in\mathcal{T}_{C}}\left\{\mathrm{minidx}^{\mathrm{L}}_{g}[\delta^{\prime}]:\delta=\Delta_{C}(\,w[\widehat{j}+m{\,..\,}\widehat{j}+m^{\mathrm{Idem}}_{g})\,)\odot\delta^{\prime}\right\}.

Proof.

We denote the number of children of
g
by
q
and the
t
-th child of
g
by
f_{t}
.
Then, by the argument similar to the proof of Lemma
66
, for each
t
, we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}_{f_{t}}=\Delta_{C}(\,w[\widehat{j}+m{\,..\,}\widehat{j}+m^{\mathrm{Idem}}_{g})\,)\odot\{\Delta_{C}(\,w[j+m^{\mathrm{Idem}}_{g}{\,..\,}r_{f_{t}})\,):[j,k)\in\mathcal{J}_{f_{t}}\}.

Thus, we have

\displaystyle\mathrm{minidx}_{g}[\delta]

\displaystyle=\min\{t:1\leq t\leq q,\ \delta\in\mathcal{D}_{f_{t}}\odot e_{g}\}

\displaystyle=\min\Bigl\{t:1\leq t\leq q,

\displaystyle\quad\quad\delta\in\{\Delta_{C}(\,w[\widehat{j}+m{\,..\,}\widehat{j}+m^{\mathrm{Idem}}_{g})\,)\odot\Delta_{C}(\,w[j+m^{\mathrm{Idem}}_{g}{\,..\,}r_{f_{t}})\,)\odot e_{g}\colon[j,k)\in\mathcal{J}_{f_{t}}\}\Bigr\}

\displaystyle=\min\Bigl\{t:1\leq t\leq q,\ \exists\delta^{\prime}\in\{\Delta_{C}(\,w[j+m^{\mathrm{Idem}}_{g}{\,..\,}r_{f_{t}})\,)\odot e_{g}\colon[j,k)\in\mathcal{J}_{f_{t}}\}\},

\displaystyle\quad\quad\delta=\Delta_{C}(\,w[\widehat{j}+m{\,..\,}\widehat{j}+m^{\mathrm{Idem}}_{g})\,)\odot\delta^{\prime}\}\Bigr\}

\displaystyle=\min_{\delta^{\prime}\in\mathcal{T}_{C}}\left\{\mathrm{minidx}^{\mathrm{L}}_{g}[\delta^{\prime}]:\delta=\Delta_{C}(\,w[\widehat{j}+m{\,..\,}\widehat{j}+m^{\mathrm{Idem}}_{g})\,)\odot\delta^{\prime}\right\}.\qed

Clearly, these updates can be computed in constant time.

11.2
Implementing
AuxiliaryEnumerator

Now, we give an implementation of
AuxiliaryEnumerator
.
Due to its length, we divide our implementation into three pseudocodes, Algorithms
6
,
7
, and
8
.

Implementing
\textsc{Initialize}_{\mathrm{A}}
,
\textsc{Shrink}_{\mathrm{A}}
,
\textsc{Update}_{\mathrm{A}}
, and
\textsc{UpdateIdem}_{\mathrm{A}}
.

Algorithm
6
includes the methods
\textsc{Initialize}_{\mathrm{A}}
,
\textsc{Shrink}_{\mathrm{A}}
,
\textsc{Update}_{\mathrm{A}}
, and
\textsc{UpdateIdem}_{\mathrm{A}}
.

Initialize
A
(
\widehat{j}
) initializes
\widehat{j}
,
m
,
\mathcal{D}^{\mathrm{L}}
, and
\mathrm{minidx}^{\mathrm{L}}
, which seems to consume
O(|w|)
time. As done in
RightEnumerator
, we consider this runs in constant time by creating and initializing each node only when the data structure first accesses it.

Shrink
A
(
d
) just reduces
m
by
d
and clearly runs in constant time.

Update
A
(
f
) and
UpdateIdem
A
(
g
) updates
\mathcal{D}^{\mathrm{L}}_{f}
and
\mathrm{minidx}^{\mathrm{L}}_{f}
, respectively, using Lemma
66
and Lemma
67
, respectively, which also run in constant time.

method

A
(
\widehat{j}
)

1

Input:

\widehat{j}\in[|w|]

\widehat{j}
;

2

m\leftarrow|w|-\widehat{j}+1
;

3

For each node
f
,
m_{f}\leftarrow m
and
\mathcal{D}^{\mathrm{L}}_{f}\leftarrow\emptyset
;

4

For each idempotent node
g
,
m^{\mathrm{Idem}}_{g}\leftarrow m
and for each transition
\delta\in\mathcal{T}_{C}
,
\mathrm{minidx}^{\mathrm{L}}_{g}[\delta]\leftarrow\infty
;

5

method

A
(
d
)

6

Input:

0\leq d\leq m

m\leftarrow m-d
;

7

(

)

A node
f

\mathcal{D}^{\mathrm{L}}_{f}\leftarrow\Delta_{C}(\,w[\widehat{j}+m{\,..\,}\widehat{j}+m_{f})\,)\odot\mathcal{D}^{\mathrm{L}}_{f}
;

9

m_{f}\leftarrow m
;

10

method

(

)

11

Input:

An idempotent node
g

foreach

\delta\in\mathcal{T}_{C}

do

12

\mathrm{minidx}^{\mathrm{L}}_{g}[\delta]\leftarrow\displaystyle\min_{\delta^{\prime}\in\mathcal{T}_{C}}\left\{\mathrm{minidx}^{\mathrm{L}}_{g}[\delta^{\prime}]:\delta=\Delta_{C}(\,w[\widehat{j}+m{\,..\,}\widehat{j}+m^{\mathrm{Idem}}_{g})\,)\odot\delta^{\prime}\right\}
;

13

m^{\mathrm{Idem}}_{g}\leftarrow m
;

AuxiliaryEnumerator
(first part).

method

A
(
j,k
)

1

Input:

An interval
[j,k)
such that
w[j{\,..\,}k)
is a prefix of
\widehat{w}=w[\widehat{j}{\,..\,}|w|]

;

f
is not root

do

(

\mathcal{D}^{\mathrm{L}}_{f}.\mathrm{add}(\delta)

if

g

(

p\leftarrow g.\mathrm{childIdx}(f)

//
f
is the
