Algorithm 7

Implementation of
AuxiliaryEnumerator
(second part).

Implementing
\textsc{Add}_{\mathrm{A}}
.

Algorithm
7
implements the method
\textsc{Add}_{\mathrm{A}}
.
As in previous algorithms, we adopt classes and modules; particularly, we write

•

S.\mathrm{add}(x)
for the operation of adding the element
x
to the set
S
,

•

f.\mathrm{parent}
to represent the parent of the node
f
,

•

g.\mathrm{numOfChildren}
to represent the number of children of the node
g
,

•

g.\mathrm{childOf}(p)
to represent the
p
-th child (from the left) of the node
g
(
1
-origin), and

•

g.\mathrm{childIdx}(f)
to represent, for two nodes
f
and
g
with
g=f.\mathrm{parent}
, an integer
p
such that
f
is the
p
-th child of
g
(from the left).

Now we prove the correctness of Algorithm
7
.
First, we prove the invariants used in Lemmas
66
and
67
.
It is clear that
\textsc{Update}_{\mathrm{A}}
and
\textsc{UpdateIdem}_{\mathrm{A}}
does not break those invariants because
m_{f}
and
m^{\mathrm{Idem}}_{g}
only decrease.
Moreover, we remark that the method
\textsc{Get}_{\mathrm{A}}
, which we implement in Algorithm
8
, does not change
\mathcal{D}^{\mathrm{L}}
or
\mathrm{minidx}^{\mathrm{L}}
other than by calling
\textsc{Update}_{\mathrm{A}}
or
\textsc{UpdateIdem}_{\mathrm{A}}
, and thus, does not break the invariant.
Therefore, it suffices to prove the following two lemmas.

Lemma 68
.

Assume that

j+m_{f}\leq k,\quad w[\widehat{j}{\,..\,}\widehat{j}+m_{f})=w[j{\,..\,}j+m_{f})

holds for all nodes
f
and
[j,k)\in\mathcal{J}_{f}
.
Then, after calling
\textsc{Add}_{\mathrm{A}}(\cdot)
, this invariant still holds.

Proof.

Since
m_{f}
does not increase, it suffices to prove that the invariant holds for the interval
[j,k)
that is newly added to
\mathcal{J}
.
Since the loop of line
7
iterates over all nodes
f
with
k\in[\ell_{f},r_{f})
,
\textsc{Update}_{\mathrm{A}}(f)
is called for each
f
with
[j,k)\in\mathcal{J}_{f}
, and particularly,
m_{f}
is updated to
m
.
Thus, from the precondition of
\textsc{Add}_{\mathrm{A}}(j,k)
, the invariant holds.
∎

Lemma 69
.

Assume that

j+m^{\mathrm{Idem}}_{g}\leq k,\quad w[\widehat{j}{\,..\,}\widehat{j}+m^{\mathrm{Idem}}_{g})=w[j{\,..\,}j+m^{\mathrm{Idem}}_{g})

holds for all idempotent node
g
and
[j,k)\in\mathcal{J}_{g}
.
Then, after calling
\textsc{Add}_{\mathrm{A}}(\cdot)
, this invariant still holds.

Proof.

Since
m^{\mathrm{Idem}}_{f}
does not increase, it suffices to prove that the invariant holds for the interval
[j,k)
that is newly added to
\mathcal{J}
.
Since the loop of line
7
iterates over all idempotent nodes
g
with
k\in[\ell_{g},r_{g})
,
\textsc{UpdateIdem}_{\mathrm{A}}(g)
is called for each
g
with
[j,k)\in\mathcal{J}_{g}
, and particularly,
m^{\mathrm{Idem}}_{g}
is updated to
m
.
Thus, from the precondition of
\textsc{Add}_{\mathrm{A}}(j,k)
, the invariant holds.
∎

Combining Lemmas
66
and
68
yields that, after calling
\textsc{Update}_{\mathrm{A}}(f)
,
\mathcal{D}^{\mathrm{L}}_{f}
coincides
\mathcal{D}_{f}
.
Moreover, combining Lemmas
67
and
69
yields that, after calling
\textsc{UpdateIdem}_{\mathrm{A}}(g)
,
\mathrm{minidx}^{\mathrm{L}}_{g}
coincides
\mathrm{minidx}_{g}
.
These conditions continue to be satisfied until a
\textsc{Shrink}_{\mathrm{A}}
query decreases
m
.
Therefore, hereafter, we identify

\mathcal{D}^{\mathrm{L}}_{f}
with
\mathcal{D}_{f}
and

\mathrm{minidx}^{\mathrm{L}}_{g}
with
\mathrm{minidx}_{g}
on the nodes on which
\textsc{Update}_{\mathrm{A}}
and
\textsc{UpdateIdem}_{\mathrm{A}}
, respectively, are called after the last
\textsc{Shrink}_{\mathrm{A}}
query.

The statements and proofs of Lemmas
70
–
73
are analogous to and almost same as those of Lemmas
57
–
60
.

Lemma 70
.

Let
j\in[|w|]
.
Then, the query
\textsc{Add}_{\mathrm{A}}(j,k)
adds
\Delta_{C}(\,w[j+m{\,..\,}r_{f})\,)
to
\mathcal{D}_{f}
for all nodes
f
with
k\in[\ell_{f},r_{f})
(and only for those nodes).

Proof.

The loop defined in line
7
iterates over all nodes
f
with
k\in[\ell_{f},r_{f})
.
For each of such
f
, line
7
adds the transition
\Delta_{C}(\,w[j+m{\,..\,}r_{f})\,)
to
\mathcal{D}_{f}
.
∎

Thus, we have the following, which is immediate from Lemma
70
.

Lemma 71
.

Let
\mathcal{J}
be a set of indices.
Assume we have invoked
\textsc{Add}_{\mathrm{A}}(j,k)
once for each
[j,k)\in\mathcal{J}
, in any order.
Then, for every node
f
such that
\textsc{Update}_{\mathrm{A}}(f)
is called after the last
\textsc{Shrink}_{\mathrm{A}}
query,

\mathcal{D}_{f}=\{\Delta_{C}(\,w[j+m{\,..\,}r_{f})\,):[j,k)\in\mathcal{J}_{f}\}
.

We now ensure that the method
\textsc{Add}_{\mathrm{A}}
correctly updates
\mathrm{minidx}_{g}[\delta]
by the following lemma.

Lemma 72
.

The query
\textsc{Add}_{\mathrm{A}}(j,k)
updates
\mathrm{minidx}_{g}[\delta]
by
\min(p,\mathrm{minidx}_{g}[\delta])
for all idempotent node
g
with
k\in[\ell_{g},r_{g})
, where

•

\delta=\Delta_{C}(\,w[j+m{\,..\,}r_{f})\,)\odot e_{g}
, where
f
is the unique child of
g
with
k\in[\ell_{f},r_{f})
, and

•

p
is the integer such that
f
is the
p
-th child of
g
,

and does not update other idempotent nodes.

Proof.

The loop of line
7
iterates over all
(g,f)
satisfying the condition of the lemma, and line
7
is processed when and only when
g
is idempotent,
f
is the
p
-th child of
g
, and
\delta\odot e_{g}=\Delta_{C}(\,w[j+m{\,..\,}r_{f})\,)\odot e_{g}
.
∎

Now, we have the following.

Lemma 73
.

Let
\mathcal{J}
be a set of intervals.
Assume we have invoked
\textsc{Add}_{\mathrm{A}}(j,k)
once for each
[j,k)\in\mathcal{J}
, in any order.
Then, for each idempotent node
g
and
\delta\in\mathcal{T}_{C}
, we have

\mathrm{maxidx}_{g}[\delta]=\max\{t:1\leq t\leq q,\ \delta\in\mathcal{D}_{f_{t}}\odot e_{g}\}.

where
q
is the number of children of
g
and
f_{t}
is the
t
-th child of
g
.

Proof.

From Lemma
72
,

\mathrm{maxidx}_{g}[\delta]
is equal to the maximum among integers
t
such that there is an interval
[j,k)\in\mathcal{J}
with

•

k\in[\ell_{f_{t}},r_{f_{t}})\subseteq[\ell_{g},r_{g})
, where
f_{t}
is the
t
-th child of
g
, and

•

\delta=\Delta_{C}(\,w[j+m,r_{f_{t}})\,)\odot e_{g}
.

By definition of
\mathcal{D}_{f_{t}}
, we have

\displaystyle\{\Delta_{C}(\,w[j+m{\,..\,}r_{f_{t}})\,)\odot e_{g}:[j,k)\in\mathcal{J}_{f_{t}}\}=\mathcal{D}_{f_{t}}\odot e_{g}.

Thus, we have

\displaystyle\mathrm{maxidx}_{g}[\delta]

\displaystyle=\max\{t:1\leq t\leq q,\ \exists[j,k)\in\mathcal{J}_{f_{t}},\ \delta=\Delta_{C}(\,w[j+m,r_{f_{t}})\,)\odot e_{g}\}

\displaystyle=\max\{t:1\leq t\leq q,\delta\in\mathcal{D}_{f_{t}}\odot e_{g}\}.\qed

Implementing
\textsc{Get}_{\mathrm{A}}
.

method

Get
A
(
i
)

1

Input:

i\in[|w|]

Let
f
be the leaf node corresponding to the index
i
;

2

\textsc{Update}_{\mathrm{A}}(f)
;

3

\textsc{Accum}\leftarrow\mathcal{D}^{\mathrm{L}}_{f}
;

4

while

f
is not root

do

5

g\leftarrow f.\mathrm{parent}
;

6

p\leftarrow g.\mathrm{childIdx}(f)
;

//
f
is the
p
-th child of
g

if

p>1

then

//
IF:
f
is not the leftmost child of
g

7

f^{-}\leftarrow g.\text{childOf}(p-1)
;

//
f^{-}
is the immediate left sibling of
f

p\leftarrow g.\text{childIdx}(f^{-})
;

8

\textsc{Update}_{\mathrm{A}}(f^{-})
;

9

foreach

\delta\in\mathcal{D}^{\mathrm{L}}_{f^{-}}

do

10

\textsc{Accum}.\text{add}\left(\delta\odot\Delta_{C}(\,w[r_{f^{-}}{\,..\,}i)\,)\right)
;

11

if

g
is an idempotent node

then

12

\textsc{UpdateIdem}_{\mathrm{A}}(g)
;

13

foreach

\delta\in\mathcal{T}_{C}

do

14

if

p-1>\mathrm{minidx}^{\mathrm{L}}_{g}[\delta]

then

15

\textsc{Accum}.\text{add}\left(\delta\odot\Delta_{C}(\,w[r_{f^{-}}{\,..\,}i)\,)\right)
;

16

f\leftarrow g
;

17

return

Accum
;

18

Algorithm 8

Implementation of
AuxiliaryEnumerator
(third part).

Now, we give an implementation of the
\textsc{Get}_{\mathrm{A}}
method in Algorithm
8
.
Since the algorithm calls
\textsc{Update}_{\mathrm{A}}(f^{-})
immediately before using the value of
\mathcal{D}^{\mathrm{L}}_{f^{-}}
and
\textsc{UpdateIdem}_{\mathrm{A}}(g)
immediately before using the value of
\mathrm{minidx}^{\mathrm{L}}_{g}
, as done in the analysis of
\textsc{Add}_{\mathrm{A}}
, we identify
\mathcal{D}^{\mathrm{L}}_{f^{-}}
into
\mathcal{D}_{f}
and
\mathrm{minidx}^{\mathrm{L}}_{g}
into
\mathrm{minidx}_{g}
.
The following lemma proves the correctness of the implementation, which is analogous to Lemma
61
.

Lemma 74
.

Let
i\in[|w|]
. Then,
Get
A
(
i
) returns the set

\{\Delta_{C}(\,w[j+m{\,..\,}i)\,):[j,k)\in\mathcal{J},\ k\leq i\}
.

Proof.

Get
A
(
i
) returns the union of

(i)

\mathcal{D}_{f}
for the leaf node
f
corresponding to the index
i
(line
8
),

(ii)

\mathcal{D}_{f^{-}}\odot\Delta_{C}(\,w[r_{f^{-}}{\,..\,}i)\,)
for each node
f
with
i\in[\ell_{f},r_{f})
that is not the leftmost child of the parent of
f
, where
f^{-}
is the immediate left sibling of
f
(line
8
), and

(iii)

\{\delta\odot\Delta_{C}(\,w[r_{f^{-}}{\,..\,}i)\,)\colon\delta\in\mathcal{T}_{C},\ p-1>\mathrm{minidx}_{g}[\delta]\}
for each node
f
with
i\in[\ell_{f},r_{f})
that is the non-leftmost
p
-th child of an idempotent node
g
, where
f^{-}
is the immediate left sibling of
f
,
(p-1)
-th child of
g
(line
8
).

From now on, we rewrite these sets in a more manageable form.

(i).

We have

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}_{f}

\displaystyle=\{\Delta_{C}(\,w[j+m{\,..\,}r_{f})\,):[j,k)\in\mathcal{J}_{f}\}=\{\Delta_{C}(\,w[j+m{\,..\,}r_{f})\,):[j,k)\in\mathcal{J},\ k\in[\ell_{f},r_{f})\}

\displaystyle=\{\Delta_{C}(\,w[j+m{\,..\,}r_{f})\,):[j,k)\in\mathcal{J},\ \underline{k=i}\}

where the first equality is from the definition of
\mathcal{D}_{f}
and the third equality is from the fact that
f
is the leaf.

(ii).

For each node
f
satisfying the condition of (ii), we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}_{f^{-}}\odot\Delta_{C}(\,w[r_{f^{-}}{\,..\,}i)\,)

\displaystyle=\{\Delta_{C}(\,w[j+m{\,..\,}r_{f^{-}})\,)\odot\Delta_{C}(\,w[r_{f^{-}}{\,..\,}i)\,):[j,k)\in\mathcal{J}_{f^{-}}\}

\displaystyle=\{\Delta_{C}(\,w[j+m{\,..\,}i)\,):[j,k)\in\mathcal{J},\ \underline{k\in[\ell_{f^{-}},r_{f^{-}})}\},

where the first equality is from the definition of
\mathcal{D}_{f^{-}}
.

(iii).

Let
g
be an idempotent node and
f_{1},\ldots,f_{p-1}=f^{-},f_{p}=f,\ldots,f_{q}
be its children such that
i\in[\ell_{f},r_{f})
.
Then, we obtain

\displaystyle\{\delta\odot\Delta_{C}(\,w[r_{f^{-}}{\,..\,}i)\,):\delta\in\mathcal{T}_{C},\ \mathrm{minidx}_{g}[\delta]<p-1\}

\displaystyle=\{\delta\odot\Delta_{C}(\,w[r_{f^{-}}{\,..\,}i)\,):\delta\in\mathcal{T}_{C},\ \min\{t:1\leq t\leq q,\ \delta\in\mathcal{D}_{f_{t}}\odot e_{g}\}<p-1\}

\displaystyle=\bigcup_{t=1}^{p-2}\mathcal{D}_{f_{t}}\odot e_{g}\odot\Delta_{C}(\,w[r_{f^{-}}{\,..\,}i)\,)

\displaystyle=\bigcup_{t=1}^{p-2}\mathcal{D}_{f_{t}}\odot\underbrace{e_{g}\odot\dots\odot e_{g}}_{p-1-t}\odot\Delta_{C}(\,w[r_{f^{-}}{\,..\,}i)\,)

\displaystyle=\bigcup_{t=1}^{p-2}\left\{\Delta_{C}(\,w[j+m{\,..\,}r_{f_{t}})\,)\odot\Delta_{C}(\,w[\ell_{f_{t+1}}{\,..\,}r_{f_{t+1}})\,)\odot\dots\odot\Delta_{C}(\,w[\ell_{f_{p-1}}{\,..\,}r_{f_{p-1}})\,)\odot\Delta_{C}(\,w[\ell_{f_{p}}{\,..\,}i)\,):[j,k)\in\mathcal{J}_{f_{t}}\right\}

\displaystyle=\bigcup_{t=1}^{p-2}\left\{\Delta_{C}(\,w[j+m{\,..\,}i):[j,k)\in\mathcal{J}_{f_{t}}\,)\right\}

\displaystyle=\{\Delta_{C}(\,w[j+m{\,..\,}i)\,):[j,k)\in\mathcal{J},\ \underline{k\in[\ell_{g},\ell_{f^{-}})}\}.

Now, it suffices to prove that the union of intervals

(I)

consisting only of an index
i
,

(II)

[\ell_{f^{-}},r_{f^{-}})
for each pair of nodes
(f,f^{-})
defined in (ii), and

(III)

[\ell_{g},\ell_{f^{-}})
for each tuple of nodes
(g,f,f^{-})
defined in (iii)

coincides with the interval
[1,i]
.
We omit the proof, since it is identical to the corresponding part of the proof of Lemma
61
, except that the left and right are interchanged.
∎

On Time Complexity.

Clearly, each query in Algorithms
6
,
7
, and
8
runs in time proportional to the depth of the factorization forest and
|\mathcal{T}_{C}|
, both of which are constants.
Therefore, as desired, each query runs in constant time.

12
Enumerating Near Transitions

In this section, we construct
GetNearTransitions
(
i,m
), which we used in Section
10
but whose construction was deferred.

Recalling the setting.

We first recall our setting.
Let
\mathsf{ST}
be the suffix tree of the string
w
,
v
be a vertex of
\mathsf{ST}
,
\mathscr{H}_{v}
and
\mathscr{L}_{v}
be the set of indices in heavy and light subtree of
v
, respectively.
We assume that the substring
w_{B}:=\Gamma(v)
corresponding to the root-
v
path is in
\mathbb{L}\left(B\right)
.
We denote
m:=|w_{B}|
.
Let
i\in\mathscr{L}_{v}
be an index with
w[i+m{\,..\,}|w|]\in\mathbb{L}\left(D\right)
.

The goal here is to give an
O(\log|w|)
-time algorithm to compute the set

\mathcal{D}:=\{\Delta_{C}(\,w[j+m{\,..\,}i)\,):j\in J,\ j+m\leq i<j+2m\},

where

J=\{j\in\mathscr{H}_{v}:w[1{\,..\,}j)\in\mathbb{L}\left(A\right)\}.

Let

\mathscr{H}^{i,m}_{v}:=\{j\in\mathscr{H}_{v}:j+m\leq i<j+2m\}.

Then, we can rewrite
\mathcal{D}
as:

\mathcal{D}=\{\Delta_{C}(\,w[j+m{\,..\,}i)\,):j\in\mathscr{H}^{i,m}_{v},w[1{\,..\,}j)\in\mathbb{L}\left(A\right)\}.

Operations on
\mathscr{H}_{v}
.

We manage
\mathscr{H}_{v}
by a balanced binary search tree (e.g., an AVL tree), which can perform the following
O(\log|\mathscr{H}_{v}|)
-time
[
24
, Chap. 13]
:

•

Adding a value
x
to
\mathscr{H}_{v}
.

•

Checking if there is a value
x
in
\mathscr{H}_{v}
.

•

Given a value
x
, computing a successor/predecessor of
x
, i.e.,
\min\{y\in\mathscr{H}_{v}:x<y\}
or
\max\{y\in\mathscr{H}_{v}:y<x\}
.

We can modify Algorithm
5
so that it additionally maintains this search tree as follows. In the
Initialize
L
(
\cdot
) method, we initialize the search tree. Each time the
\textsc{Add}_{\mathrm{L}}(j)
method is called, we add
j
into the search tree. This modification contributes to the time complexity of
Left-
B
Transition Enumeration
by only
O(|\mathsf{ST}[\pi]|\log|w|)
, and thus does not affect the overall time complexity.

As in previous sections, we adopt classes or modules; particularly, we write
\mathscr{H}_{v}.\mathrm{succ}(x)
and
\mathscr{H}_{v}.\mathrm{pred}(x)
to represent the successor and predecessor of
x
in
\mathscr{H}_{v}
, respectively.

Computing
\mathcal{D}
.

Now we consider computing
\mathcal{D}
.
Assuming
\mathscr{H}^{i,m}_{v}
is non-empty, we define the following two values:

j_{0}:=\min\mathscr{H}^{i,m}_{v},\quad j_{\text{last}}:=\max\mathscr{H}^{i,m}_{v}.

We note that
j_{0}
and
j_{\text{last}}
can be computed in
O(\log|w|)
-time using
\mathscr{H}_{v}.\text{succ}(i-2m)
and
\mathscr{H}_{v}.\text{pred}(i-m+1)
, as well as determining non-emptiness of
\mathscr{H}^{i,m}_{v}
.

If
|\mathscr{H}^{i,m}_{v}|\leq 2
, we can compute
\mathcal{D}
just by brute-forcing on
\mathscr{H}^{i,m}_{v}
. Note that whether
|\mathscr{H}^{i,m}_{v}|\leq 2
can be determined by the constant number of calls of successor operation.
Now, we assume
|\mathscr{H}^{i,m}_{v}|\geq 3
.
Let
\bar{w}:=w[j_{0}{\,..\,}j_{\text{last}}+m)
.
The following lemma exploits the structure of
\mathscr{H}^{i,m}_{v}
and
\bar{w}
.

Lemma 75
.

\bar{w}
is a periodic string: i.e.,
\bar{w}=\theta^{r}
for some prefix
\theta
of
\bar{w}
and rational
r>1
.
Moreover,
\mathscr{H}^{i,m}_{v}
is an arithmetic progression with common difference
d
, where
d
is a period of
\bar{w}
.

Proof.

Our proof is two-fold.
First, we determine the common difference
d
.
Next, we show the set
\{j_{0},j_{0}+d,j_{0}+2d,\ldots,j_{\text{last}}\}
coincides
\mathscr{H}^{i,m}_{v}
.

Determining
d
.

Take arbitrary
j\in\mathscr{H}^{i,m}_{v}\setminus\{j_{0},j_{\text{last}}\}
.
The positional relation among indices
j_{0}
,
j
, and
j_{\mathrm{last}}
are illustrated as follows.

i-2m+1
i-m
i
w_{B}
w_{B}
w_{B}
j_{0}
j
j_{\text{last}}

Since
j_{0},j\in\mathscr{H}^{i,m}_{v}\subseteq\mathscr{H}_{v}
, we have
w[j_{0}{\,..\,}j_{0}+m)=w[j{\,..\,}j+m)=w_{B}
.
Thus,
w_{B}[t]=w_{B}[t+(j-j_{0})]
holds for all
t\in\{1,\dots,m-(j-j_{0})\}
, and particularly,
w_{B}
has a period
(j-j_{0})
.
Applying the same argument for
j
and
j_{\text{last}}
yields that
w_{B}
has a period
(j_{\text{last}}-j)
.

By the constraint
i-2m+1\leq j_{0}<j_{\text{last}}\leq i-m
, the following holds:

|w_{B}|=m>(i-m)-(i-2m+1)\geq j_{\text{last}}-j_{0}=(j_{\text{last}}-j)+(j-j_{0}).

Thus, we can apply Fine and Wilf’s Theorem (Lemma
10
) to obtain that
w_{B}
has a period

d_{j}:=\gcd(j-j_{0},j_{\text{last}}-j)<\frac{m}{2},

which is a divisor of
j_{\text{last}}-j_{0}
.
Let
e_{j}:=(j_{\text{last}}-j_{0})/d_{j}
, which is an integer.

Let
\theta_{j}:=w_{B}[1{\,..\,}d_{j}]
.
Then,
w_{B}=(\theta_{j})^{r}
for some rational
r>1
.
We also have
w[j_{0}{\,..\,}j_{\text{last}})=\theta_{j}^{e_{j}}
because it is a prefix of
w_{B}
.
Thus, we have

\bar{w}=w[j_{0}{\,..\,}j_{\text{last}})\,w[j_{\text{last}}{\,..\,}j_{\text{last}}+m)=\theta_{j}^{e_{j}}\,\underbrace{w_{B}}_{\text{has period $d_{j}$}}

also has a period
d_{j}
.
Since this holds for all
j\in\mathscr{H}_{i,m}\setminus\{j_{0},j_{\text{last}}\}
, again from Fine and Wilf’s Theorem,
\bar{w}
has a period

d:=\gcd\{d_{j}:j\in\mathscr{H}^{i,m}_{v}\setminus\{j_{0},j_{\text{last}}\}\},

where the reason why we can apply the Theorem is that, for any two
j,j^{\prime}\in\mathscr{H}^{i,m}_{v}\setminus\{j_{0},j_{\text{last}}\}
,
d_{j}+d_{j^{\prime}}<m\leq|\bar{w}|
holds.

Proving
\mathscr{H}^{i,m}_{v}
forming an arithmetic progression.

Let
e=(j_{\text{last}}-j_{0})/d
.
To complete the proof, we prove

\mathscr{H}^{i,m}_{v}=\{j_{0}+kd:k\in\{0,1,\ldots,e\}\}.

(\subseteq)
:

Let
j\in\mathscr{H}^{i,m}_{v}
.
If
j=j_{0}
, we have
j=j_{0}+0\cdot d
. Moreover, if
j=j_{\text{last}}
, we have
j=j_{0}+ed
.
Otherwise, take
d_{j}
defined above, which divides
d
and is a divisor of
j-j_{0}
.
It leads that there is an integer
k_{j}
with
j=j_{0}+k_{j}d
, which satisfies
0<k_{j}<e
because of
j_{0}<j<j_{\text{last}}
.

(\supseteq)
:

Conversely, let
k\in\{0,1,\ldots,e\}
.
If
k=0
, we have
j_{0}+kd=j_{0}\in\mathscr{H}^{i,m}_{v}
.
Moreover, if
k=e
, we have
j_{0}+kd=j_{\text{last}}\in\mathscr{H}^{i,m}_{v}
.
Assume otherwise.
Since
\bar{w}=w[j_{0}{\,..\,}j_{\text{last}}+m)
has a period
d
,
we have
w[j_{0}+kd{\,..\,}j_{0}+kd+m)=w[j_{0}{\,..\,}j_{0}+m)=w_{B}
.
Thus,
j_{0}+kd
is either in
\mathscr{H}_{v}
or
\mathscr{L}_{v}
.
Moreover, again from periodicity, we have
w[j_{0}+kd+m]=w[j_{0}+m]
; that is, the letter immediately after the substring
w[j_{0}+kd{\,..\,}j_{0}+kd+m)
is same as that of the substring
w[j_{0}{\,..\,}j_{0}+m)
.
Thus, combining with the fact that
j_{0}\in\mathscr{H}_{v}
, we have
j_{0}+kd\in\mathscr{H}_{v}
, and thus,
j_{0}+kd\in\mathscr{H}^{i,m}_{v}
.
∎

From Lemma
75
, we can write
\mathscr{H}^{i,m}_{v}=\{j_{0}+kd:k\in\{0,1,\ldots,e\}\}
using a tuple
(j_{0},d,e)
.
This tuple
(j_{0},d,e)
can be computed in
O(\log|w|)
-time as follows.
First, we compute the smallest element
j_{0}
, the second smallest element
j_{1}
, and the largest element
j_{\text{last}}
of
\mathscr{H}^{i,m}_{v}
in
O(\log|w|)
-time using the successor/predecessor queries on
\mathscr{H}_{v}
.
Then, we have
d=j_{1}-j_{0}
and
e=(j_{\text{last}}-j_{0})/(j_{1}-j_{0})
.

On
(j_{0},d,e)
, we define two substrings
\theta_{1}:=w[j_{0}{\,..\,}j_{0}+d)
, and
\theta_{2}:=w[j_{0}+m{\,..\,}j_{0}+m+d)
.
Since
\bar{w}
has a period
d
that divides
j_{\mathrm{last}}-j_{0}
, we have

\bar{w}=\theta_{1}^{e}\ w_{B}=w_{B}\ \theta_{2}^{e}.

Let
w_{1}:=w[1{\,..\,}j_{0})
and
w_{2}:=w[j_{\text{last}}+m{\,..\,}i)
.
We define

\mathcal{S}_{A}:=\{k\geq 0:w_{1}\,\theta_{1}^{k}\in\mathbb{L}\left(A\right)\},

and for each
\delta\in\mathcal{T}_{C}
,

\mathcal{S}_{C,\delta}:=\{k\geq 0:\Delta_{C}(\theta_{2}^{k}\>w_{2})=\delta\}.

From Lemma
46
,
\mathcal{S}_{A}
and
\mathcal{S}_{C,\delta}
are ultimately periodic.
Moreover, for every transition
\delta
of
C
,
the following set

\mathcal{S}_{\delta}:=\mathcal{S}_{A}\underbrace{+}_{\text{Minkowski Sum}}\mathcal{S}_{C,\delta}

is also ultimately periodic because of Lemma
14
.
Furthermore, if we write
\mathcal{S}_{\delta}=(\mu,M,\lambda,T)
,
\mu
and
\lambda
are bounded by a constant that depends only on
A
and
C
.
Now, we have the following.

Lemma 76
.

A transition
\delta\in\mathcal{T}_{C}
is in
\mathcal{D}
if and only if
e\in\mathcal{S}_{\delta}
.

Proof.

(
\Rightarrow
):

Assume
\delta\in\mathcal{D}
.
Then, there is an index
j\in\mathscr{H}^{i,m}_{v}
such that
w[1{\,..\,}j)\in\mathbb{L}\left(A\right)
and
\Delta_{C}(\,w[j+m{\,..\,}i)\,)=\delta
.
From Lemma
75
,
j=j_{0}+kd
holds for some
k\in\{0,\dots,e\}
, and thus,

w[1{\,..\,}j)=w_{1}\,w[j_{0}{\,..\,}j)=w_{1}\,\theta_{1}^{k},

which implies
k\in\mathcal{S}_{A}
.
Moreover, we have

w[j+m{\,..\,}i)=w[j+m{\,..\,}j_{\text{last}}+m)\,w_{2}=\theta_{2}^{e-k}\,w_{2},

and thus,
e-k\in\mathcal{S}_{C,\delta}
.
Therefore, from the definition of the Minkowski sum, we have
e=k+(e-k)\in\mathcal{S}_{A}+\mathcal{S}_{C,\delta}=\mathcal{S}_{\delta}
.

(
\Leftarrow
):

Let
e\in\mathcal{S}_{\delta}
.
Then, there is an integer
k\in\{0,\dots,e\}
with
k\in\mathcal{S}_{A}
and
e-k\in\mathcal{S}_{C,\delta}
.
Let
j=j_{0}+kd
.
Then, we have

w[1{\,..\,}j)=w_{1}\,w[j_{0}{\,..\,}j)=w_{1}\,w[j_{0}{\,..\,}j_{0}+kd)=w_{1}\,\theta^{k}\in\mathbb{L}\left(A\right).

Moreover, we have

\Delta_{C}(\,w[j+m{\,..\,}i)\,)=\Delta_{C}(w[j+m{\,..\,}j_{\text{last}}+m)\ w_{2})=\Delta_{C}(\theta_{2}^{e-k}\,w_{2})=\delta,

where the last equality is from
e-k\in\mathcal{S}_{C,\delta}
.
Therefore, we have
\delta\in\mathcal{D}
and the lemma is proved.
∎

On Time Complexity.

Since
\mathcal{S}_{\delta}
depends only on
A
and
C
, we can compute it in constant time, as well as determining if
e\in\mathcal{S}_{\delta}
. Computing
e
takes
O(\log|w|)
time.
Thus, from Lemma
76
, the desired set
\mathcal{D}
can be computed in
O(\log|w|)
time.

Appendix A
Proof of Lemma for Closure Properties of Ultimately Periodic Sets

We prove Lemma
14
in Section
2
.

See
14

Proof.

Let
P=(\mu,M,\lambda,T)
and
Q=(\mu^{\prime},M^{\prime},\lambda^{\prime},T^{\prime})
.
Moreover, let
\bar{\lambda}
be the least common multiple of
\lambda
and
\lambda^{\prime}
.

Union:
It suffices to prove that for
i\geq\max(\mu,\mu^{\prime})
,
i\in P\cup Q
if and only if
i+\bar{\lambda}\in P\cup Q
.
Assume
i\in P\cup Q
.
Then, we have either
i\in P
and
i\in Q
.
In the case where
i\in P
, we have
i\geq\max(\mu,\mu^{\prime})\geq\mu
, and thus,
(i\bmod\lambda)\in T
.
Thus, we have
((i+\bar{\lambda})\bmod\lambda)\in T
, which implies
i+\bar{\lambda}\in P\subseteq P\cup Q
because
i+\bar{\lambda}\geq\mu
.
The same argument also works for the case of
i\in Q
.
Thus, we have
i+\bar{\lambda}\in P\cup Q
.
The inverse direction is proved in the same way.

Minkowski Sum:
It suffices to prove that for
i\geq\mu+\mu^{\prime}+\bar{\lambda}
,
i\in P\cup Q
holds if and only if
i+\bar{\lambda}\in P+Q
.
Assume
i\in P+Q
.
Then, there is a pair of integers
j\in P
and
k\in Q
with
j+k=i
.
Since
j+k=i\geq\mu+\mu^{\prime}
, we have either
(j\bmod\lambda)\in T
or
(k\bmod\lambda^{\prime})\in T^{\prime}
.
If
(j\bmod\lambda)\in T
, we have
((j+\bar{\lambda})\bmod\lambda)\in T
and thus,
j+\bar{\lambda}\in P
.
Thus,
i+\bar{\lambda}=(j+\bar{\lambda})+k\in P+Q
.
The same argument also holds for the case
(k\bmod\lambda^{\prime})\in T^{\prime}
.
Therefore, we have
i+\bar{\lambda}\in P+Q
.

For the inverse direction, assume
i+\bar{\lambda}\in P+Q
.
Then, there is a pair of integers
j^{\prime}\in P
and
k^{\prime}\in Q
with
j^{\prime}+k^{\prime}=i+\bar{\lambda}
.
Since
j^{\prime}+k^{\prime}-2\bar{\lambda}=i-\bar{\lambda}\geq\mu+\mu^{\prime}
, we have either
j^{\prime}-\bar{\lambda}\geq\mu
or
k^{\prime}-\bar{\lambda}\geq\mu^{\prime}
.
If the former holds, since
((j^{\prime}-\bar{\lambda})\bmod\lambda)\in T
, we have
i=(j^{\prime}-\bar{\lambda})+k^{\prime}\in P+Q
.
The same argument also holds for the latter case.
Therefore, we have
i\in P+Q
.

e
-Multiplication:
The set
e\cdot P
is equal to
(e\mu,e\cdot M,e\lambda,e\cdot T)
.
∎

Appendix B
Normalizing 1-use REWB: Proof of Theorem
6

In this section, we prove our Theorem
6
for 1-use
R
EWB.

See
6

Structure of This Appendix.

We prove Theorem
6
by combining Lemmas
77
and
78
.
After introducing the required definitions through Sections
B.1
and
B.2
,
we prove Lemma
77
in Section
B.3
.

Lemma 77

(Normalizing First Step)
.

Let
E
be a
1
-use
R
EWB.
Then, we can decompose
E
into the following form:

(\alpha_{1}\,\mathord{\backslash x}\,D_{1})+(\alpha_{2}\,\mathord{\backslash x}\,D_{2})+\cdots+(\alpha_{K^{\prime}}\,\mathord{\backslash x}\,D_{K^{\prime}})+\Phi

where each
\alpha_{i}
is a reference-free (i.e., not including
\mathord{\backslash y}
for any
y
) and
D_{i}
and
\Phi
are
R
egex rather than
R
EWB.
In particular, the following holds:

K^{\prime}\leq|E|,\ |\alpha_{i}|=O(|E|),\ |D_{j}|=O(|E|),\ |\Phi|=O(|E|).

After proving this lemma,
in Section
B.4
,
we transform
\alpha_{i}
into a corresponding expression generated by the following restricted grammar:

\mathcal{H}::=\epsilon\mid\sigma\mid\mathcal{H}\,\mathcal{H}\mid\mathcal{H}\,+\,\mathcal{H}\mid\mathcal{H}^{*}\mid[\mathbf{REGEX}]_{x}.

Then we prove the second phase lemma in Section
B.5
.

Lemma 78

(Normalizing Second Step)
.

Let
\alpha
be an expression of the restricted grammar
\mathcal{H}
.
Then, we can decompose
\alpha
into the following form:

(A_{1}\ [B_{1}]_{x}\ C_{1})+(A_{2}\ [B_{2}]_{x}\ C_{2})+\cdots+(A_{K^{\prime\prime}}\ [B_{K^{\prime\prime}}]_{x}\ C_{K^{\prime\prime}})+\Psi,

where
A_{i},B_{i},C_{i}
, and
\Psi
are
R
egexes.

In particular, the following holds for each expression size:

K^{\prime\prime}\leq|E|,\ |A_{i}|=O(|E|^{2}),\ |B_{i}|\leq|E|,\ |C_{i}|=O(|E|^{2}),\ |\Psi|\leq|E|.

Combining Lemmas
77
and
78
immediately leads to our Theorem
6
.

B.1
Basic Definitions

We first recall and introduce required definitions.

REWB.

Let
\Sigma
be a finite alphabet and
\mathcal{V}
be a finite set of variables.
The set of regular expressions with backreferences (
R
EWB) extends standard regular expressions (
R
egex) and is defined by the following grammar:

\begin{array}[]{lcl}E&::=&\epsilon\mid\sigma\mid\emptyset\\
&\mid&E\cdot E\mid E+E\mid(E)^{*}\\
&\mid&[E]_{x}\mid\mathord{\backslash x}\end{array}

where
\sigma\in\Sigma
and
x\in\mathcal{V}
.

Notation Remark:
In this appendix, for ease of readability

•

We sometimes write
[\,E\,]_{x}
instead of
(\,E\,)_{x}
to distinguish them from parentheses.

•

We sometimes simply write
E_{1}E_{2}
instead of
E_{1}\cdot E_{2}
by omitting the symbol
\cdot
.

Term: Binding and Reference.

We call each term of the form
[\cdots]_{x}
a
binding
and one of the form
\mathord{\backslash x}
a
reference
.

Size of Expression:
|E|
.

Here we define the size of expressions
E
as the total number of nodes of the abstract syntax tree of
E
.
Formally,
|E|
is defined by the size function
\mathit{size}(E)
as follows:

\begin{array}[]{lcl}\mathit{size}(t)&:=&1,\quad\text{ (for $t\in\{\epsilon,\sigma,\emptyset,\mathord{\backslash x}\}$)},\\
\mathit{size}(E_{1}\diamond E_{2})&:=&\mathit{size}(E_{1})+\mathit{size}(E_{2})+1,\quad\text{ (for $\diamond\in\{\ \cdot\ ,\ +\ \}$)}\\
\mathit{size}(\,(E)^{*}\,)&:=&\mathit{size}(E)+1,\\
\mathit{size}(\,[E]_{x}\,)&:=&\mathit{size}(E)+1.\end{array}

REGEX.

We define the fragment
R
egex by the following grammar:

E_{R}::=\epsilon\mid\sigma\mid\emptyset\mid E_{R}E_{R}\mid E_{R}+E_{R}\mid(E_{R})^{*}.

Semantics and Language.

Next, we define the semantics of
R
EWB via the semantic function
\llbracket E\rrbracket_{\lambda}
.
For a
R
EWB
E
and a valuation
\lambda
from
\mathcal{V}
to
\Sigma^{*}
,

\llbracket E\rrbracket_{\lambda}
returns a set of pairs
\langle w,\lambda^{\prime}\rangle
,
each consisting of a matched string and an updated valuation:

\begin{array}[]{lcl}\llbracket\epsilon\rrbracket_{\lambda}&:=&\{\,\langle\epsilon,\lambda\rangle\,\},\\
\llbracket\sigma\rrbracket_{\lambda}&:=&\{\,\langle\sigma,\lambda\rangle\,\},\\[3.0pt]
\llbracket\emptyset\rrbracket_{\lambda}&:=&\emptyset,\\[3.0pt]
\llbracket E_{1}E_{2}\rrbracket_{\lambda}&:=&\{\langle w_{1}w_{2},\nu\rangle:\langle w_{1},\mu\rangle\in\llbracket E_{1}\rrbracket_{\lambda},\langle w_{2},\nu\rangle\in\llbracket E_{2}\rrbracket_{\mu}\},\\[3.0pt]
\llbracket E_{1}+E_{2}\rrbracket_{\lambda}&:=&\llbracket E_{1}\rrbracket_{\lambda}\cup\llbracket E_{2}\rrbracket_{\lambda},\\[3.0pt]
\llbracket E^{*}\rrbracket_{\lambda}&:=&\bigcup_{k\geq 0}\llbracket E^{k}\rrbracket_{\lambda},\qquad\text{where}\ E^{0}:=\epsilon,\;E^{k+1}:=E^{k}E,\\[3.0pt]
\llbracket[E]_{x}\rrbracket_{\lambda}&:=&\{\langle w,\mu[x:=w]\rangle:\langle w,\mu\rangle\in\llbracket E\rrbracket_{\lambda}\},\\[3.0pt]
\llbracket\mathord{\backslash x}\rrbracket_{\lambda}&:=&\{\langle\lambda(x),\lambda\rangle\}.\end{array}

This function is well-defined by induction on the height of Kleene stars.

Let
\iota
be the initialized valuation that maps every variable to the empty string
\epsilon
: i.e.,
\iota(v)=\epsilon
for all
v\in\mathcal{V}
.
We simply write
\llbracket E\rrbracket
to denote
\llbracket E\rrbracket_{\iota}
.
We define the language of a
R
EWB
E
,
\mathbb{L}\left(E\right)
, as follows:

\mathbb{L}\left(E\right):=\{w:\langle w,\lambda\rangle\in\llbracket E\rrbracket\}.

Pre-processing:
\emptyset
-free
R
EWB.

As a preprocessing step,
we eliminate
\emptyset
from a given
R
EWB by repeatedly applying the following language-preserving rewrites:

\begin{array}[]{l}E+\emptyset\Mapsto E,\ \emptyset+E\Mapsto E,\qquad E\,\emptyset\Mapsto\emptyset,\ \emptyset\,E\Mapsto\emptyset,\qquad\emptyset^{*}\Mapsto\epsilon,\ [\emptyset]_{x}\Mapsto\emptyset.\end{array}

Observe that each rule strictly decreases the size (the number of symbols) of the expression.
Therefore, this rewriting process always terminates.

We say that a
R
EWB
E
is
\emptyset
-free
if
E
contains no occurrence of the constant
\emptyset
.
More formally, such expressions are generated by the following grammar:

E_{-\emptyset}::=\epsilon\mid\sigma\mid E_{-\emptyset}\ E_{-\emptyset}\mid E_{-\emptyset}+E_{-\emptyset}\mid\bigl(E_{-\emptyset}\bigr)^{*}\mid\bigl[E_{-\emptyset}\bigr]_{x}\mid\mathord{\backslash x}

Proposition 79
.

For every
R
EWB
E
, there exists a
R
EWB
G

such that
\mathbb{L}\left(E\right)=\mathbb{L}\left(G\right)

and either
G
is
\emptyset
-free or
G=\emptyset
.

Remark:
Hereafter, we assume that the input
R
EWB is
\emptyset
-free and not equal to
\emptyset
.

1-use REWB.

Let
E
be a (
\emptyset
-free)
R
EWB.
We call
E

1-use
if, in every possible computation of
E
,
at most one backreference operation
\mathord{\backslash x}
(for some variable
x
) is performed.
For example,

•

[(a+b)^{*}]_{x}\#\mathord{\backslash x}
is a 1-use
R
EWB.

•

[\Sigma^{*}]_{x}\mathord{\backslash x}\mathord{\backslash x}
is not a 1-use
R
EWB.

•

[a^{*}]_{x}[b^{*}]_{y}\#(\mathord{\backslash x}+\mathord{\backslash y})
is a 1-use
R
EWB.

•

[\Sigma^{*}]_{x}[\Sigma^{*}_{y}]\mathord{\backslash x}\mathord{\backslash y}
is not a 1-use
R
EWB.

•

[\Sigma^{*}]_{x}(\mathord{\backslash x}+a)(b+\mathord{\backslash x})
is not a 1-use
R
EWB.

•

[\Sigma^{*}]_{x}(a\mathord{\backslash x}b)^{*}
is not a 1-use
R
EWB.

To formally define the notion of 1-use,
we introduce the notion of
template strings
.

Template String.

For a given
R
EWB expression
E
,
we can see
E
as a language generator over the alphabet

\Gamma:=\Sigma\cup\{\,[,\,]_{x},\mathord{\backslash x}:x\in\mathcal{V}\}
by treating special symbols
[
,
]_{x}
, and
\mathord{\backslash x}
as usual letters.
We write
\mathbb{T}(E)\subseteq\Gamma^{*}
to denote the set of template strings generated by
E
.
Every element of
\mathbb{T}(E)
is called a
template string
.

\mathbb{T}(E)
is formally defined as follows:
We define the set of template strings
T(E)\subseteq\Gamma^{*}
by structural recursion:

\begin{array}[]{lcl}\mathbb{T}(\epsilon)&:=&\{\epsilon\},\\
\mathbb{T}(\sigma)&:=&\{\sigma\},\\
\mathbb{T}(E_{1}E_{2})&:=&T(E_{1})T(E_{2}),\\
\mathbb{T}(E_{1}+E_{2})&:=&T(E_{1})\cup T(E_{2}),\\
\mathbb{T}(E^{*})&:=&(\mathbb{T}(E))^{*},\\
\mathbb{T}([E]_{x})&:=&\{[\,w\,]_{x}:w\in\mathbb{T}(E)\},\\
\mathbb{T}(\mathord{\backslash x})&:=&\{\mathord{\backslash x}\}.\end{array}

For our example expression
E_{4}
,
\mathbb{T}(E_{4})
contains the following template strings for
\Sigma=\{a,b\}
:

\mathbb{T}(E_{4})\ni[bab]_{x}\,a\mathord{\backslash x}b\,a\mathord{\backslash x}b,~~[abba]_{x}\,a\mathord{\backslash x}b\,a\mathord{\backslash x}b\,a\mathord{\backslash x}b,~~\ldots

By the definition, the following properties trivially hold.

Proposition 80
.

Let
E
be an
\emptyset
-free expression.
If
E
contains
\mathord{\backslash x}
, then there exists
s\in\mathbb{T}(E)
such that
s
contains
\mathord{\backslash x}
.
Similarly, if
E
has
[\cdots]_{x}
, then there exists
s\in\mathbb{T}(E)
such that
s
contains
[\cdots]_{x}
.

Remark.
Each template string can be evaluated by using
\llbracket\bullet\rrbracket
. For example,

\llbracket a[bc]_{x}d\rrbracket=\{\langle abcd,x\mapsto bc\rangle\}.

From the definition and the remark, the following property of
\mathbb{T}(\bullet)
holds.

Proposition 81
.

Let
E
be a
R
EWB. Then, the following holds:

\llbracket E\rrbracket=\llbracket\mathbb{T}(E)\rrbracket

where
\llbracket\mathbb{T}(E)\rrbracket=\bigcup_{s\in\mathbb{T}(E)}\llbracket s\rrbracket
.

For a template string
s
, we write
\#_{\mathord{\backslash\mathcal{V}}}(s)
to denote the total number of occurrences of reference symbols
\mathord{\backslash z}
for
z\in\mathcal{V}
.
Let
\#_{\mathord{\backslash\mathcal{V}}}(E):=\max\{\#_{\mathord{\backslash\mathcal{V}}}(s):s\in\mathbb{T}(E)\}

where
\max\emptyset=0
.
Now we define 1-use
R
EWB as follows:

R
EWB
E
is 1-use
if and only if

\#_{\mathord{\backslash\mathcal{V}}}(E)\leq 1
.

B.2
Normalization Overview

We aim to normalize a given 1-use
R
EWB
E
into the following form:

(A_{1}\,[B_{1}]_{x}\,C_{1}\,\mathord{\backslash x}\,D_{1})+(A_{2}\,[B_{2}]_{x}\,C_{2}\,\mathord{\backslash x}\,D_{2})+\cdots+(A_{K}\,[B_{K}]_{x}\,C_{K}\mathord{\backslash x}\,D_{K})+E^{\prime}

where
A_{i}
,
B_{i}
,
C_{i}
,
D_{i}
, and
E^{\prime}
are
R
egexes.
Here
E^{\prime}
represents for the 0-use (pure
R
egex) part.

The crucial restriction is that, in each summand,
the only binding is
[B_{i}]_{x}
and the only reference is
\mathord{\backslash x}
, and both occur at the top level.
In particular, the subexpressions
A_{i},B_{i},C_{i},
and
D_{i}
are pure
R
egexes (i.e., they contain neither bindings nor references).

To obtain our goal form, we need to solve the following tasks:

1.

Lift nested reference subterms
\mathord{\backslash x}
to the top-level in Section
B.3
.

2.

Then, lift nested binding subterms
[\cdots]_{x}
to the top-level in Section
B.5
.

B.3
Lifting References
\mathord{\backslash x}
and Lemma
77

On lifting
\mathord{\backslash x}
to the top-level,
we first consider two patterns
(\cdots\mathord{\backslash x}\cdots)^{*}
and
[\cdots\mathord{\backslash x}\cdots]_{y}
.

B.3.1
Handling Simple Patterns

Pattern “references in Kleene stars”:
(\cdots\mathord{\backslash x}\cdots)^{*}
.

Since our input
E
is
1
-use, any references
\mathord{\backslash x}
never appear inside Kleene stars.
For instance, an expression
[\Sigma^{*}]_{x}(a\mathord{\backslash x}b)^{*}
uses the variable
x
finitely but unboundedly many times.

Pattern “references in bindings”:
[\cdots\mathord{\backslash x}\cdots]_{y}
.

We rewrite and simplify a given 1-use
R
EWB
E
so that any references
\mathord{\backslash x}
never appear inside bindings
[\cdots]_{y}
.
As an example, consider an expression
[\Sigma^{*}]_{x}[a\,\mathord{\backslash x}\,b]_{y}
.
Since we use the variable
x
in the subterm
a\,\mathord{\backslash x}\,b
,
any subterms executed after this part are not allowed to use a backreference
\mathord{\backslash y}
for any variable
y
.
In other words, we can remove all bindings executed after
a\,\mathord{\backslash x}\,b
.
In fact, for this expression,
we can safely rewrite it as follows:

[\Sigma^{*}]_{x}[a\,\mathord{\backslash x}\,b]_{y}\leadsto[\Sigma^{*}]_{x}a\,\mathord{\backslash x}\,b

by removing
[\_]_{y}
.

This rewriting is accomplished by the following translation function:

\begin{array}[]{lcl}\mathit{unnest}(\,[E]_{x}\,)&:=&\begin{cases}\mathit{unnest}(E)&\text{If some references appear in $E$},\\
[E]_{x}&\text{Otherwise},\\
\end{cases}\\[5.0pt]
\mathit{unnest}(EF)&:=&\mathit{unnest}(E)\mathit{unnest}(F),\\
\mathit{unnest}(E+F)&:=&\mathit{unnest}(E)+\mathit{unnest}(F),\\
\mathit{unnest}(E^{*})&:=&E^{*},\\
\mathit{unnest}(t)&:=&t\quad(t\in\{\epsilon,\sigma\}\cup\{\mathord{\backslash z}:z\in\mathcal{V}\})\end{array}

The following properties clearly hold by the definition of
\mathit{unnest}
.

Proposition 82
.

\begin{array}[]{ll}(1)&\mathbb{L}\left(E\right)=\mathbb{L}\left(\mathit{unnest}(E)\right),\\
(2)&|\mathit{unnest}(E)|\leq|E|.\end{array}

After applying the function
\mathit{unnest}
, it suffices to consider 1-use
R
EWBs generated by the following restricted grammar
\mathcal{F}
of
R
EWB:

\mathcal{F}::=\epsilon\mid\sigma\mid\mathcal{F}\,\mathcal{F}\mid\mathcal{F}+\mathcal{F}\mid(F)^{*}\mid[F]_{x}\mid\mathord{\backslash x}

where
F
is restricted to expressions that are
reference
-free
R
EWBs.

B.3.2
Tagged References and Lifting References Covered by Unions

After applying
\mathit{unnest}
, our lifting phase for references is not yet complete because, in general, references may not appear at the top-level but rather be nested within unions.
For instance, consider the following expression:

E:=a+\mathord{\backslash x}+(b\,\mathord{\backslash y}c)+((d^{*}\mathord{\backslash x})+(\mathord{\backslash x}e^{*}))f^{*}.

In this example, the third and fourth reference occurrences are not at the top-level.

To lift such references,
as a first step, we annotate a designated reference occurrence with a tag
\rho
as
\mathord{\backslash x}^{\rho}
.
This tag serves purely as a syntactic marker to distinguish this occurrence from other references.
Applying this to our example
E
,
we use
\rho
to generate the following set of tagged expressions:

\begin{array}[]{ll}E^{\rho}_{1}:&a+\mathord{\backslash x}^{\rho}+(b\,\mathord{\backslash y}c)+((d^{*}\mathord{\backslash x})+(\mathord{\backslash x}e^{*}))f^{*},\\
E^{\rho}_{2}:&a+\mathord{\backslash x}+(b\,\mathord{\backslash y}^{\rho}c)+((d^{*}\mathord{\backslash x})+(\mathord{\backslash x}e^{*}))f^{*},\\
E^{\rho}_{3}:&a+\mathord{\backslash x}+(b\,\mathord{\backslash y}c)+((d^{*}\mathord{\backslash x}^{\rho})+(\mathord{\backslash x}e^{*}))f^{*},\\
E^{\rho}_{4}:&a+\mathord{\backslash x}+(b\,\mathord{\backslash y}c)+((d^{*}\mathord{\backslash x})+(\mathord{\backslash x}^{\rho}e^{*}))f^{*}.\end{array}

We formally define the
\rho
-tagging,
denoted by
\Xi_{\rho}(E)
, as a function that maps an expression
E
to a set of expressions where exactly one reference occurrence is tagged with
\rho
.
Let
E
be an expression and let
\mathit{Occ}_{\mathrm{ref}}(E)
be the list
of all
occurrences
of reference symbols in
E
, ordered from left to right in the concrete syntax.
Write
\mathit{Occ}_{\mathrm{ref}}(E)=(o_{1},o_{2},\dots,o_{N})
.
For each
i\in\{1,2,\ldots,N\}
, define
E_{i}^{\rho}
to be the expression obtained from
E
by replacing

exactly
the occurrence
o_{i}
of a reference symbol (say
\backslash x
) with its tagged form

\backslash x^{\rho}
, leaving all other symbols unchanged.
Then, we define
\Xi_{\rho}(E)
as follows:

\Xi_{\rho}(E):=\{E_{1}^{\rho},E_{2}^{\rho},\dots,E_{N}^{\rho}\}

Applying this function to our example expression
E
, we obtain the set
\Xi_{\rho}(E)=\{E^{\rho}_{1},E^{\rho}_{2},E^{\rho}_{3},E^{\rho}_{4}\}
.

We naturally extend
\llbracket\cdot\rrbracket
and
\mathbb{T}(\cdot)
to accommodate the tag
\rho
as follows:

\begin{array}[]{lcl}\llbracket\,\mathord{\backslash x}^{\rho}\,\rrbracket&:=&\llbracket\,\mathord{\backslash x}\,\rrbracket,\\
\mathbb{T}(\,\mathord{\backslash x}^{\rho}\,)&:=&\{\,\mathord{\backslash x}^{\rho}\,\}.\end{array}

To decompose the set of tagged template strings, we define two notations:

\begin{array}[]{lcl}\mathcal{Z}_{\text{ref}}(E)&:=&\{w\in\mathbb{T}(E):\text{$w$ does not contain any reference symbols }\},\\
\mathbb{T}(E)\restriction_{\text{ref}}\rho&:=&\{w\in\mathbb{T}(E):\text{$w$ contains $\rho$}\}=\mathbb{T}(E)\setminus\mathcal{Z}_{\text{ref}}(E)\end{array}

Since
\mathcal{Z}_{\text{ref}}(E)
and
\mathbb{T}(E)\restriction_{\text{ref}}\rho
are disjoint sets of
\mathbb{T}(E)
, the following basic property clearly holds.

Proposition 83
.

Let
E
be an expression and
assume
\Xi_{\rho}(E)=E^{\rho}_{1},E^{\rho}_{2},\ldots,E^{\rho}_{N}
where
N:=\mathit{Occ}_{\mathrm{ref}}(E)
is the total number of occurrences of references in
E
.
Then, the following holds:

\llbracket\,\mathbb{T}(E)\,\rrbracket=\llbracket\,\mathcal{Z}_{\text{ref}}(E)\,\rrbracket\cup\bigcup^{N}_{i=1}\bigl(\llbracket\,\mathbb{T}(E_{i})\restriction_{\text{ref}}\rho\,\rrbracket\,\bigr)

Computing
\mathcal{Z}
.

Let
E
an expression of the grammar
\mathcal{F}
.
We define the function
\mathit{noref}(E)
that constructs an expression computing
\mathcal{Z}_{\text{ref}}(E)
.

\begin{array}[]{lcl}\mathit{noref}(\epsilon)&:=&\epsilon,\\
\mathit{noref}(\sigma)&:=&\sigma,\\
\mathit{noref}(E_{1}E_{2})&:=&\mathit{noref}(E_{1})\mathit{noref}(E_{2}),\\
\mathit{noref}(E_{1}+E_{2})&:=&\mathit{noref}(E_{1})+\mathit{noref}(E_{2}),\\
\mathit{noref}(E^{*})&:=&E^{*},\\
\mathit{noref}([E]_{x})&:=&\mathit{noref}(E),\\
\mathit{noref}(\mathord{\backslash x})&:=&\emptyset,\end{array}

By the definition, the following basic properties clearly hold.

Lemma 84
.

\begin{array}[]{ll}(1)&\mathcal{Z}_{\text{ref}}(E)=\mathbb{T}(\mathit{noref}(E)),\\
(2)&|\mathit{noref}(E)|\leq|E|.\end{array}

Computing
\mathbb{T}(E)\restriction_{\text{ref}}\rho
.

Let
E
be a
R
EWB with a tagged reference.
We define the function
\mathit{appear}_{\rho}(E)
that computes an expression computing
\mathbb{T}(E)\restriction_{\text{ref}}\rho
:

\begin{array}[]{lcl}\mathit{appear}_{\rho}(\mathord{\backslash x}^{\rho})&:=&\mathord{\backslash x}^{\rho},\\
\mathit{appear}_{\rho}(E_{1}E_{2})&:=&\begin{cases}\mathit{appear}_{\rho}(E_{1})\ E_{2}&\text{If $E_{1}$ contains $\rho$},\\
E_{1}\ \mathit{appear}_{\rho}(E_{2})&\text{Else if $E_{2}$ contains $\rho$},\\
\emptyset&\text{Otherwise},\end{cases}\\
\mathit{appear}_{\rho}(E_{1}+E_{2})&:=&\begin{cases}\mathit{appear}_{\rho}(E_{1})&\text{If $E_{1}$ contains $\rho$},\\
\mathit{appear}_{\rho}(E_{2})&\text{Else if $E_{2}$ contains $\rho$},\\
\emptyset&\text{Otherwise},\end{cases}\\
\mathit{appear}_{\rho}(t)&:=&\emptyset,\qquad\bigl(t=\epsilon,\sigma,\mathord{\backslash x},E^{*},[E]_{x}\bigr).\end{array}

Remark:
In our setting,
since
E^{*}
and
[E]_{x}
never contain
\rho
, we set
\mathit{appear}_{\rho}(t)=\emptyset
for such arguments.
By the definition, the following basic property clearly holds.

Lemma 85
.

\mathbb{T}(E)\restriction_{\text{ref}}\rho=\mathbb{T}(\mathit{appear}_{\rho}(E)).

Also, the following property for the form and size holds.

Proposition 86
.

\mathit{appear}_{\rho}(E)=\alpha\,\mathord{\backslash x}^{\rho}\,\beta

where
\alpha
and
\beta
are reference-free
R
EWBs.
Furthermore,
|\mathit{appear}_{\rho}(E)|\leq|E|
holds.
In particular, the following holds:

|\alpha|\leq|E|,\qquad|\beta|\leq|E|.

Summary of This Section.

From a given 1-use
R
EWB
E
, we can construct the following:

(\alpha_{1}\,\mathord{\backslash x_{1}}\,\beta_{1})+(\alpha_{2}\,\mathord{\backslash x_{2}}\,\beta_{2})+\cdots+(\alpha_{K^{\prime}}\,\mathord{\backslash x_{K^{\prime}}}\,\beta_{K^{\prime}})+\mathit{noref}(E)

where
\alpha_{i}
and
\beta_{i}
are reference-free
R
EWBs.
Also, the following holds:

K^{\prime}\leq|E|,\ |\alpha_{i}|=O(|E|),\ |\beta_{j}|=O(|E|),\ |\mathit{noref}(E)|=O(|E|).

This clearly corresponds our Lemma
77
.
See
77

B.4
Removing Unnecessary Variables and only Keeping
x

For each subexpression
\alpha_{i}\ \mathord{\backslash x_{i}}\ \beta_{i}
, we perform the following two translations:

1.

We replace all bindings of the form
[F]_{y}
(for all variables
y
) with
F
in
\beta_{i}
.

•

This replacing does not alter the expression’s behaviour because, on 1-use
R
EWBs, any use references of the form
\mathord{\backslash x}
do not appear in
\beta_{i}
.

•

To formally state this rewrite, we use the following function and rewrite
\beta_{i}\leadsto\mathit{elim}(\beta_{i})
.

\begin{array}[]{lcl}\mathit{elim}(t)&{:=}&t\quad(t\in\{\epsilon,\sigma\}),\\
\mathit{elim}(\>E_{1}E_{2}\>)&{:=}&\mathit{elim}(\,E_{1}\,)\mathit{elim}(\,E_{2}\,),\\
\mathit{elim}(\>E_{1}+E_{2}\>)&{:=}&\mathit{elim}(\,E_{1}\,)+\mathit{elim}(\,E_{2}\,),\\
\mathit{elim}((E)^{*})&{:=}&\mathit{elim}(E)^{*},\\
\mathit{elim}(\>[E]_{y}\>)&{:=}&\mathit{elim}(\,E\,)\end{array}

2.

We replace all subterms of the form
[F]_{y}
(
x_{i}\neq y
) with
F
in
\alpha_{i}
.

•

This replacing does not alter the expression’s behaviour because we only use the variable
x_{i}
and thus modifications on
y
are irrelevant.

•

To formally state this rewrite, we use the following function and rewrite
\alpha_{i}\leadsto\mathit{keep}_{x_{i}}(\alpha_{i})
.

\begin{array}[]{lcl}\mathit{keep}_{x}(t)&{:=}&t\quad(t\in\{\epsilon,\sigma\}),\\[3.0pt]
\mathit{keep}_{x}(\>E_{1}E_{2}\>)&{:=}&\mathit{keep}_{x}(\,E_{1}\,)\mathit{keep}_{x}(\,E_{2}\,),\\[3.0pt]
\mathit{keep}_{x}(\>E_{1}+E_{2}\>)&{:=}&\mathit{keep}_{x}(\,E_{1}\,)+\mathit{keep}_{x}(\,E_{2}\,),\\[3.0pt]
\mathit{keep}_{x}((E)^{*})&{:=}&\mathit{keep}_{x}(E)^{*},\\
\mathit{keep}[(\>[E]_{y}\>)&{:=}&\begin{cases}[\,\mathit{keep}_{x}(E)\,]_{x}&\text{If $y=x$},\\
\mathit{keep}_{x}(E)&\text{Otherwise}.\end{cases}\end{array}

Grammar for this Phase.

We first decompose
E
by Proposition
83
as follows:

E\Mapsto\mathit{noref}(E),\mathit{appear}_{\rho}(E_{1}),\mathit{appear}_{\rho}(E_{2}),\ldots,\mathit{appear}_{\rho}(E_{N}),

and for each
\mathit{appear}_{\rho}(E_{i})=\alpha_{i}\ \mathord{\backslash x_{i}}\ \beta
, we rewrite it as follows:

\alpha_{i}\ \mathord{\backslash x_{i}}\ \beta\Mapsto\mathit{keep}_{x_{i}}(\alpha_{i})\ \mathord{\backslash x_{i}}\ \mathit{elim}(\beta).

Each expression of the form
\mathit{keep}_{x}(\alpha)
is generated by the following restricted grammar
\mathcal{G}
:

\mathcal{G}::=\epsilon\mid\sigma\mid\mathcal{G}\mathcal{G}\mid\mathcal{G}+\mathcal{G}\mid\mathcal{G}^{*}\mid[\,\mathcal{G}\,]_{x}.

Remark:
If
[\cdots]_{y}
appears in
\mathit{keep}_{x}(\alpha)
, then
y=x
must hold.

Hereafter, we translate an expression
\alpha
of
\mathcal{G}
into the following language-equivalent form:

\alpha\Mapsto A\>[B]_{x}\>C

where
A
,
B
, and
C
are pure REGEX.

B.5
Lift Bindings to the Top level and Lemma
78

In moving all occurrences of binding to the top-level,
we handle two patterns:
[\cdots[F]_{x}\cdots]_{x}
and
(\cdots[F]_{x}\cdots)^{*}
.

B.5.1
Pattern
[\cdots[F]_{x}\cdots]_{x}

For instance, consider the expression
[a\,[\Sigma^{*}]_{x}\,b]_{x}
.
Since only the last modification to the variable
x
is effective, we can rewrite the expression to
[a\,\Sigma^{*}\,b]_{x}
without changing its behaviour.

\begin{array}[]{lcl}\mathit{unnest}_{2}(\,[E]_{x}\,)&:=&[\,\mathit{elim}(E)\,]_{x},\\
\mathit{unnest}_{2}(EF)&:=&\mathit{unnest}_{2}(E)\mathit{unnest}_{2}(F),\\
\mathit{unnest}_{2}(E+F)&:=&\mathit{unnest}_{2}(E)+\mathit{unnest}_{2}(F),\\
\mathit{unnest}_{2}(E^{*})&:=&(\mathit{unnest}_{2}(E))^{*},\\
\mathit{unnest}_{2}(t)&:=&t\quad(t\in\{\epsilon,\sigma\})\end{array}

By the definition, the following basic properties trivially hold.

Proposition 87
.

The following properties hold for
\mathit{unnest}_{2}
.

\begin{array}[]{ll}(1)&\mathbb{L}\left(E\right)=\mathbb{L}\left(\mathit{unnest}_{2}(E)\right),\\
(2)&|\mathit{unnest}_{2}(E)|\leq|E|.\end{array}

After this translation, we can further restrict our grammar to the following form:

\mathcal{H}::=\epsilon\mid\sigma\mid\mathcal{H}\mathcal{H}\mid\mathcal{H}+\mathcal{H}\mid\mathcal{H}^{*}\mid[\mathbf{REGEX}]_{x}.

B.5.2
Pattern
(\cdots[F]_{x}\cdots)^{*}

Handling this pattern is the most elaborate part in our normalization procedure.

Tagged Binding.

We annotate a designated binding occurrence with a tag
\rho
by writing it as
[E]_{x}^{\rho}
.
The tag serves only as a syntactic marker to distinguish this occurrence from other bindings.

For instance, let us consider the following expression:

E:=(a+[b^{*}]_{x}+(c\,[d^{*}]_{x}e)^{*}\,)^{*}.

To handle each binding subterm separately, we use
\rho
as follows and generate two tagged expressions from the above one:

\begin{array}[]{ll}E^{\rho}_{1}:&(a+[b^{*}]^{\rho}_{x}+(c\,[d^{*}]_{x}e)^{*}\,)^{*}\,,\\
E^{\rho}_{2}:&(a+[b^{*}]_{x}+(c\,[d^{*}]^{\rho}_{x}\,e)^{*}\,)^{*}\,.\end{array}

Similar to tagging references, we write
\mathit{Occ}_{\text{bind}}(E)
to denote the total number of bind occurrences in
E
and
\Xi^{\prime}(E)
to denote the set of expressions where exactly one binding occurrence is tagged with
\rho
.

We naturally extend
\llbracket\cdot\rrbracket
,
\mathbb{T}(\cdot)
, and
\mathit{elim}
to accommodate the tag
\rho
as follows:

\begin{array}[]{lcl}\llbracket\,[E]^{\rho}_{x}\,\rrbracket&:=&\llbracket\,[E]_{x}\,\rrbracket,\\
\mathbb{T}(\,[E]^{\rho}_{x}\,)&:=&[\,\mathbb{T}(E)\,]^{\rho}_{x}=\{\ [\,w\,]^{\rho}_{x}:w\in\mathbb{T}(E)\},\\
\mathit{elim}(\,[E]^{\rho}_{x}\,)&:=&\mathit{elim}(\,E\,).\end{array}

For a tagged expression
E
, we write
\mathbb{T}(E)\restriction\rho
to denote the following set:

\mathbb{T}(E)\restriction_{\text{bind}}\rho:=\{w\in\mathbb{T}(E):\text{$w$ contains a substring of the form $[\cdots]^{\rho}_{x}$ at least once }\ \}.

Decomposition by the last tag

For a template string
s
,
we write
\mathit{last}_{\rho}(s)
if both the following conditions hold:

•

s
contain some binding occurrences.

•

Furthermore, its last binding occurrence is of the form
[\cdots]^{\rho}_{x}
.

Now we write
\mathcal{Z}^{\rho}_{\text{bind}}(\alpha)
to denote the set of template strings generated by
\alpha
that contains no binding symbols:

\mathcal{Z}^{\rho}_{\text{bind}}(\alpha):=\mathbb{T}(E)\setminus(\mathbb{T}(E)\restriction_{\text{bind}}\rho)=\{w\in\mathbb{T}(\alpha):\text{$w$ does not contain any binding occurrnces}\}.

Now we define the following notation to denote the set of template strings generated by
E
whose last modifier is tagged by
\rho
:

\mathbb{T}(E)\Uparrow\rho:=\{s\in\mathbb{T}(E)\restriction_{\text{bind}}\rho:\mathit{last}_{\rho}(s)\}.

As with Proposition
83
, the following decomposition clearly holds.

Proposition 88
.

Let
E
be an expression of the grammar
\mathcal{H}
.
Assume
\Xi^{\prime}(E)=E^{\rho}_{1},E^{\rho}_{2},\ldots,E^{\rho}_{N}
where
N:=\mathit{Occ}_{\text{bind}}(E)
is the total number of occurrences of binding in
E
.
Then, the following holds:

\llbracket\,\mathbb{T}(E)\,\rrbracket=\llbracket\,\mathcal{Z}^{\rho}_{\text{bind}}(E)\,\rrbracket\cup\bigcup^{N}_{i=1}\bigl(\llbracket\,\mathbb{T}(E_{i})\Uparrow\rho\,\rrbracket\bigr)

Hereafter, for each
E_{i}
, we compute an expression of the form
A_{i}\ [B_{i}]^{\rho}_{x}\ C_{i}
such that

•

\llbracket\mathbb{T}(E_{i})\Uparrow\rho\rrbracket=\llbracket A_{i}\ [B_{i}]^{\rho}_{x}\ C_{i}\rrbracket
; and

•

A_{i},B_{i},C_{i}
are pure
R
egexes.

Computing
\mathcal{Z}
.

Let
E
be an expression of
\mathcal{H}
.
We define the function
\mathit{nobind}(E)
that computes an expression computing
\mathcal{Z}(E)
.

\begin{array}[]{lcl}\mathit{nobind}(\epsilon)&{:=}&\epsilon,\\
\mathit{nobind}(\sigma)&{:=}&\sigma,\\
\mathit{nobind}(\>E_{1}E_{2}\>)&{:=}&\mathit{nobind}(\,E_{1}\,)\mathit{nobind}(\,E_{2}\,),\\
\mathit{nobind}(\>E_{1}+E_{2}\>)&{:=}&\mathit{nobind}(\,E_{1}\,)+\mathit{nobind}(\,E_{2}\,),\\
\mathit{nobind}((E)^{*})&{:=}&\mathit{nobind}(E)^{*},\\
\mathit{nobind}(\>[E]_{x}\>)&{:=}&\emptyset,\\
\mathit{nobind}(\>[E]^{\rho}_{x}\>)&{:=}&\emptyset,\end{array}

By the definition, the following properties clearly hold for
\mathit{nobind}
.

Proposition 89
.

\begin{array}[]{ll}(1)&\llbracket\mathcal{Z}^{\rho}_{\text{bind}}(E)\rrbracket=\llbracket\mathit{nobind}(E)\rrbracket,\\
(2)&|\mathit{nobind}(E)|\leq|E|.\end{array}

Strategy for Representing the Part
\llbracket\mathbb{T}(E_{i})\Uparrow\rho\rrbracket

To explain our idea, let us consider the following expression:

(ab+c[\Sigma^{*}]_{x}d+e[\Sigma^{*}]_{x}f)^{*}

where
\Sigma=\{a,b,c,d\}
.
By tagging, we obtain the following two expressions:

E^{\rho}_{1}:\ (ab+c[\Sigma^{*}]^{\rho}_{x}d+e[\Sigma^{*}]_{x}f)^{*},\qquad E^{\rho}_{2}:\ (ab+c[\Sigma^{*}]_{x}d+e[\Sigma^{*}]^{\rho}_{x}f)^{*}.

We here consider
E^{\rho}_{1}
and write
E
to denote the subterm
(ab+c[\Sigma^{*}]^{\rho}_{x}d+e[\Sigma^{*}]_{x}f)
.

Our key idea is to unfold Kleene stars
E^{*}
so that the following holds:

\begin{array}[]{lcl}\mathbb{T}(\,E^{*}\,)\Uparrow\rho&=&\text{repeating $E$}\\
&\cdot&\text{running $E$ guaranteed to visit the $[\cdots]^{\rho}_{x}$ part exactly once}\\
&\cdot&\text{repeating $E$ while never visiting any $[\cdots]_{x}$ subterms}~(\text{corresponds to}~\mathit{nobind}(E)^{*}).\end{array}

For our example, we unfold
E^{*}
as follows

E^{*}\ \cdot\ \uline{c[\Sigma^{*}]^{\rho}_{x}d}\ \cdot\ \uuline{(ab)^{*}}\ .

Using the single-underlined and double-underlined parts,
we enforce that
\rho
appears as the last occurrence of binding across the entire computation of
E^{*}
.

Furthermore, in this form, we can remove
[\cdots]_{x}
from the
E^{*}
because the single-underlined part overwrites the variable
x
.
Namely, it suffices to consider the following expression:

E^{\prime}:=\mathit{elim}(E)^{*}\ \cdot\ {c[\Sigma^{*}]^{\rho}_{x}d}\ \cdot\ {(ab)^{*}}~~=~~\bigl(ab+c\,\Sigma^{*}\,d+e\,\Sigma^{*}\,f\bigr)^{*}\>c\,[\Sigma^{*}]^{\rho}_{x}\,d\ (ab)^{*}~.

The transformed expression fits into our goal form
A[B]_{x}C
.

To compare
\mathbb{T}(E^{*})
and
\mathbb{T}(E^{\prime})
,
we consider the following template string generated by
E^{*}
:

w~~:=~~ab\>c\,[ac]^{\rho}_{x}\,d\>e\,[aaaa]_{x}\,f\>c\,[a]^{\rho}_{x}\,d\>ababab\in\mathbb{T}(E^{*}).

Since this string contains
\rho
as the last binding occurrence, it is represented in our transformed version
E^{\prime}
by discarding binding symbols before the last
\rho
as follows:

w^{\prime}~~:=~~ab\>c\,ac\,d\>e\,aaaa\,f\>c\,[a]^{\rho}_{x}\,d\>ababab\in\mathbb{T}(E^{\prime}).

Furthermore,
\llbracket w\rrbracket=\llbracket w^{\prime}\rrbracket
clearly holds.

Formalizing Our Translation

To compute
\llbracket\mathbb{T}(E)\rrbracket\Uparrow\rho
on the basis of the above our idea, we introduce the following function
\mathit{last}_{\rho}
so that
\llbracket\mathbb{T}(E)\rrbracket\Uparrow\rho=\llbracket\mathit{last}_{\rho}(E)\rrbracket
:

\begin{array}[]{lcl}\mathit{last}_{\rho}(\epsilon)&:=&\emptyset,\\
\mathit{last}_{\rho}(\sigma)&:=&\emptyset,\\
\mathit{last}_{\rho}(E_{1}E_{2})&:=&\begin{cases}\mathit{last}_{\rho}(E_{1})\ \mathit{nobind}(E_{2})&\text{If $E_{1}$ contains $\rho$},\\
\mathit{elim}(E_{1})\ \mathit{last}_{\rho}(E_{2})&\text{Otherwise (since $\rho$ is unique and in $E$, it must be in $E_{2}$)},\end{cases}\\[15.0pt]
\mathit{last}_{\rho}(E_{1}+E_{2})&:=&\begin{cases}\mathit{last}_{\rho}(E_{1})&\text{If $E_{1}$ contains $\rho$},\\
\mathit{last}_{\rho}(E_{2})&\text{Otherwise: $E_{2}$ contains $\rho$},\end{cases}\\[15.0pt]
\mathit{last}_{\rho}(\,(E)^{*}\,)&:=&\mathit{elim}(E)^{*}\ \mathit{last}_{\rho}(E)\ \mathit{nobind}(E)^{*},\\
\mathit{last}_{\rho}([E]_{x})&:=&\emptyset,\\
\mathit{last}_{\rho}([E]^{\rho}_{x})&:=&[E]^{\rho}_{x}.\end{array}

Remark:

The argument expressions
E
of
\mathit{last}_{\rho}
satisfy the following condition:

1.

Since
E\in\mathcal{H}
,
the expression is
reference-free
, i.e., it contains no reference/usage symbols of the form
\mathord{\backslash z}
;

2.

and moreover, every binding occurrence binds the same variable
x
, i.e., every binder is of the form
[\cdot]_{x}
or
[\cdot]^{\rho}_{x}

3.

Also, since
E\in\mathcal{H}
, every binding occurrence is of the form
[R]_{x}
(and, for tagged expressions,
[R]^{\rho}_{x}
) with
R
a pure
R
egex. In particular, binders are never nested, i.e., no binder occurs within the scope of another binder.

4.

Since
E
is an element of the set of tagged expressions
\Xi^{\prime}
, the tag
\rho
is syntactically unique, i.e., there is exactly one tagged binding occurrence of the form
[\cdot]_{x}^{\rho}
in the expression.

Then, the following property holds for
\mathit{last}_{\rho}
.

Lemma 90
.

Let
E
be an expression that satisfies the conditions in the above remark. Then, the following holds:

\llbracket\mathbb{T}(E)\Uparrow\rho\rrbracket=\llbracket\mathit{last}_{\rho}(E)\rrbracket.

In particular, the generated expression
\mathit{last}_{\rho}(E)
satisfies the following condition.

Proposition 91
.

Let
E
be an expression that satisfies the conditions in the above remark.
Then,
\mathit{last}_{\rho}(E)
evaluates to an expression of the form
A\,[B]^{\rho}_{x}\,C
where
A
,
B
, and
C
are pure
R
egex.
Furthermore,

|A|=O(|E|^{2}),\quad|B|\leq|E|,\quad|C|=O(|E|^{2}).

Proof.

By definition,
\mathit{elim}
and
\mathit{nobind}
erase all binding constructs and thus always produce
R
egexes.
Moreover,
\mathit{last}_{\rho}
never introduces new binders: untagged binders are discarded
(
\mathit{last}_{\rho}([F]_{x})=\emptyset
),
while the unique tagged binder is preserved
(
\mathit{last}_{\rho}([F]^{\rho}_{x})=[F]^{\rho}_{x}
).
Since binders are not nested and
\rho
is syntactically unique, the expression
\mathit{last}_{\rho}(E)
contains exactly one binder,
namely the tagged one, and hence can be written as
A[B]^{\rho}_{x}C
with
A,B,C
pure
R
egexes.

To show the size bound, we use the following trivial properties, which are immediately shown by the definition of
\mathit{elim}
and
\mathit{nobind}
:

\text{for every expression $E$},\quad|\mathit{elim}(E)|\leq|E|,\quad|\mathit{nobind}(E)|\leq|E|.

To perform our induction, we consider a spine of subexpressions starting from
E
as follows.
Consider the syntax tree of the input expression
E
and follow the unique path from the root to the unique tagged binder.
This yields a chain of subexpressions

E=E_{0}\succ E_{1}\succ\cdots\succ E_{h}=[B]^{\rho}_{x},

where
X\succ Y
means that
Y
is a proper subexpression of
X
.
Thus,
E_{i+1}
is the unique immediate subexpression of
E_{i}
that still contains the tag
\rho
. Here, the existence and uniqueness follow from the syntactic uniqueness of
\rho
.

Induction on the length of the spine.

We need to consider four cases:

E_{i}=E_{i+1}+F
,
E_{i}=E_{i+1}F
,
E_{i}=FE_{i+1}
, and
E_{i}=(E_{i+1})^{*}
.
For these cases,
each step from
E_{i+1}
to
E_{i}
modifies the prefix/suffix
by concatenating at most one extra piece whose size is
O(|E_{i}|)
:
either
\mathit{elim}(F)\bullet
, or
\bullet\mathit{nobind}(F)
, or
\mathit{elim}(E_{i+1})^{*}\ \bullet\ \mathit{nobind}(E_{i+1})^{*}
.
Consequently,

|A_{i}|\leq|A_{i+1}|+O(|E_{i}|),\qquad|C_{i}|\leq|C_{i+1}|+O(|E_{i}|).

Unrolling from
i=h-1,h-2,\ldots,0
,
we have

|A_{0}|=O\!\left(\sum_{i=0}^{h-1}|E_{i}|\right),\qquad|C_{0}|=O\!\left(\sum_{i=0}^{h-1}|E_{i}|\right).

Finally, along a strict subexpression chain, we have

|E_{0}|>|E_{1}|>\cdots>|E_{h}|\geq 1
,
hence

\sum_{i=0}^{h-1}|E_{i}|\;\leq\;|E|+(|E|-1)+\cdots+1\;=\;O(|E|^{2}).

Therefore
|A|=|A_{0}|=O(|E|^{2})
and
|C|=|C_{0}|=O(|E|^{2})
, and we already noted
|B|\leq|E|
.
∎

Now, summarizing all the objectives up to this point, we immediately prove Lemma
78
.

See
78

Appendix C
Analyzing Hidden Constants

In this section, we analyze the hidden constants of our theorems.
We mainly consider the constant of Theorem
7
.
We first obtain a bound of
2^{O((|A|+|B|+|C|+|D|)^{4})}
by using the fact that any
R
egex
E
admits a transition monoid of size
2^{O(|E|^{2})}
.
We then refine this bound to
2^{O((|A|+|B|+|C|+|D|)^{2})}
through a more fine-grained analysis combined with additional data structures.

C.1
First Bound for Theorem
7
:
2^{O((|A|+|B|+|C|+|D|)^{4})}

We first observe that the time complexity of all parts of our algorithm depends polynomially on the size of the transition monoids we use.

Interval evaluation by factorization forest.

In our algorithm, we repeatedly perform the operations of evaluating
\Delta_{E}(w[i{\,..\,}j))
for
E\in\{A,B,C,D\}
.
By Lemma
18
, it takes
O(|\mathcal{T}_{E}|)
time, which only increases the time complexity polynomially in
|\mathcal{T}_{E}|
.
Thus, from now on, we focus on the remaining part and ignore the complexity of evaluating
\Delta_{E}(w[i,j))
.

Section
6
.

The time complexity of this part depends polynomially on the size of the representation of the ultimately periodic set
\mathcal{S}=\mathcal{S}_{X}+(2\cdot\mathcal{S}_{Y})+\mathcal{S}_{Z}
.
By a similar argument to that in the proof of Lemma
46
, we can state that the parameters
\mu
and
\lambda
of
\mathcal{S}_{X}
,
\mathcal{S}_{Y}
, and
\mathcal{S}_{Z}
are bounded by
|\mathcal{T}_{X}|
,
|\mathcal{T}_{Y}|
, and
|\mathcal{T}_{Z}|
, respectively.
Thus, the parameters of
\mathcal{S}
are bounded by a polynomial in
|\mathcal{T}_{X}|+|\mathcal{T}_{Y}|+|\mathcal{T}_{Z}|
.

Section
7
.

It is easy to see that this part affects the hidden constant only by
|\mathcal{K}_{L}|+|\mathcal{K}_{R}|
in Algorithm
1
, which are bounded by
O(|\mathcal{T}_{C}|)
.

Section
8
.

This part contributes a factor polynomial in
|\Lambda|
,
|\textsc{Accum}|
, and
|\mathcal{T}_{D}|

to the hidden constant in Algorithm
2
.
Since
|\Lambda|\leq|\mathcal{T}_{D}|
, this part affects the hidden constant by a polynomial in
|\mathcal{T}_{C}|+|\mathcal{T}_{D}|
.

Section
9
.

It is not difficult to see that each method in this part runs in time proportional to the depth of the factorization forest
\mathsf{FF}
and the size of
\mathcal{T}_{C}
.
Since the depth of
\mathsf{FF}
is
O(|\mathcal{T}_{C}|)
, each method runs in
O(|\mathcal{T}_{C}|^{2})
time.

Section
10
.

It is easy to see that this part affects the hidden constant only by
|\mathcal{D}|
in Algorithm
4
and
|\mathcal{D}_{1}|+|\mathcal{D}_{2}|
in Algorithm
5
, which are bounded by
O(|\mathcal{T}_{C}|)
.

Section
11
.

By the same argument as in Section
9
, each method runs in
O(|\mathcal{T}_{C}|^{2})
time.

Section
12
.

The time complexity of this part depends polynomially on
|\mathcal{T}_{C}|
and the size of the representation of the ultimately periodic set
\mathcal{S}_{\delta}=\mathcal{S}_{A}+\mathcal{S}_{C,\delta}
.
By a similar argument to that in the proof of Lemma
46
, we can state that the parameters
\mu
and
\lambda
of
\mathcal{S}_{A}
and
\mathcal{S}_{C,\delta}
are bounded by
|\mathcal{T}_{A}|
and
|\mathcal{T}_{C}|
, respectively.
Thus, the parameters of
\mathcal{S}
are bounded by a polynomial in
|\mathcal{T}_{A}|+|\mathcal{T}_{C}|
.

Overhead of normalization.

We have established that the hidden constant depends polynomially on
|\mathcal{T}_{A}|+|\mathcal{T}_{B}|+|\mathcal{T}_{C}|+|\mathcal{T}_{D}|
, where
R
egexes
A,B,C,D
are those defined in Sections
4
and
5
.
We complete the analysis by seeing that each of those
R
egexes, which we refer to as
A^{\prime},B^{\prime},C^{\prime},D^{\prime}
to distinguish them from the original
R
egexes, has length linear in the size of the original
R
egexes
A,B,C,D
.

The operations in Section
4
multiply the length of each
R
egex by
O(\log|\Sigma|)
, which we consider to be an absolute constant
7
7

7

In fact, we can avoid the dependence on
\log|\Sigma|
here by not binarizing the
R
egexes. This requires additional care in later sections, as the suffix tree is no longer binary; nevertheless, it is still possible to obtain the same bound for general
|\Sigma|
. For the sake of simplicity of presentation, we refrained from implementing this approach.
.
Moreover, in Section
5
, we defined

\mathbb{L}\left(B^{\prime}\right):=(\mathbb{L}\left(B\right)\setminus\{\epsilon\})\cdot\left(\mathbb{L}\left(C^{q^{0}_{C}\to\{q_{C}\}}\right)\cap\mathbb{L}\left(D^{q^{0}_{D}\to\{q_{D}\}}\right)\right)

for some
q_{C}\in Q_{C}
and
q_{D}\in Q_{D}
, and

\mathbb{L}\left(A^{\prime}\right):=\mathbb{L}\left(A\right),\qquad\mathbb{L}\left(C^{\prime}\right):=\mathbb{L}\left(C^{\{q_{C}\}\to F_{C}}\right),\qquad\mathbb{L}\left(D^{\prime}\right):=\mathbb{L}\left(D^{\{q_{D}\}\to F_{D}}\right),

for some
q_{C}\in Q_{C}
and
q_{D}\in Q_{D}
.

Clearly, the NFAs for
A^{\prime}
,
C^{\prime}
, and
D^{\prime}
have the same number of vertices as the NFAs for
A
,
C
, and
D
, respectively.
Thus, we can construct transition monoids
\mathcal{T}_{A^{\prime}}
,
\mathcal{T}_{C^{\prime}}
, and
\mathcal{T}_{D}^{\prime}
of size
2^{O(|A|^{2})}
,
2^{O(|C|^{2})}
, and
2^{O(|D|^{2})}
, respectively.
For
B^{\prime}
, straightforward construction yields a transition monoid
\mathcal{T}_{B^{\prime}}
of size
2^{O((|B|+|C||D|)^{2})}
because, using the standard construction, the NFA for
B^{\prime}
has size
O(|B|+|C||D|)
.
Therefore,
|\mathcal{T}_{A^{\prime}}|+|\mathcal{T}_{B^{\prime}}|+|\mathcal{T}_{C^{\prime}}|+|\mathcal{T}_{D^{\prime}}|\leq 2^{O(|A|^{2})}+2^{O((|B|+|C||D|)^{2})}+2^{O(|C|^{2})}+2^{O(|D|^{2})}\leq 2^{O((|A|+|B|+|C|+|D|)^{4})}
.
Thus, the overall hidden constant is
2^{O((|A|+|B|+|C|+|D|)^{4})}
.

C.2
Refined Bound for Theorem
7
:
2^{O((|A|+|B|+|C|+|D|)^{2})}

The main obstacle to achieving a running time of
2^{O((|A|+|B|+|C|+|D|)^{2})}
is that the size of the NFA for
B^{\prime}
is quadratic.
If this size could be reduced to linear in the size of original
R
egexes,
the desired bound would follow immediately; however, this appears to be difficult.

Instead, we observe that the algorithm accesses the transition monoid
\mathcal{T}_{B^{\prime}}
only in very restricted situations.
More precisely, the only operations we perform with respect to
B^{\prime}
are:

•

the construction of an ultimately periodic set
\mathcal{S}
in Section
6
(note that
Y=B^{\prime}
), and

•

deciding whether
w[i{\,..\,}j)\in\mathbb{L}\left(B^{\prime}\right)
(Algorithms
2
and
4
).

Bounding the Size of
\mathcal{S}
(Section
6
).

In Section
6
, we need to decide whether

e\in\mathcal{S}=\mathcal{S}_{X}+(2\cdot\mathcal{S}_{Y})+\mathcal{S}_{Z}
.
Previously, we bounded the size of the representation of
\mathcal{S}_{Y}
by

|\mathcal{T}_{B^{\prime}}|\leq 2^{O((|B|+|C||D|)^{2})}
, which resulted in a large hidden constant.
However, we can show that the size of

\mathcal{S}_{Y}=\{j\colon\theta^{j}\in\mathbb{L}\left(Y\right)\}

is in fact only
2^{O(|B|+|C||D|)}
.

To see this, let

N_{B^{\prime}}=(Q_{B},\Sigma,\Delta_{B},\mathcal{E}_{B},q^{0}_{B},F_{B})

be an
\epsilon
-NFA recognizing
\mathbb{L}\left(B^{\prime}\right)
.
For each
j\geq 0
, let
R^{j}\subseteq Q_{B}
denote the set of states reachable from
q^{0}_{B}
by reading
\theta^{j}
.
Then, by a similar argument to that in the proof of Lemma
46
,
R^{j+\lambda}=R^{j}
holds for all
j\geq\mu
,
where
\mu,\lambda\leq|Q_{B}|
. Consequently, the ultimately periodic set

\mathcal{S}_{Y}=\{j\colon R^{j}\cap F_{B}\neq\emptyset\}

admits a representation of size
2^{|Q_{B}|}\leq 2^{O(|B|+|C||D|)}
, and thus,
\mathcal{S}
admits a representation of size
2^{O((|A|+|B|+|C|+|D|)^{2})}
.

Reducing the Hidden Constant of Interval Evaluation.

By Lemma
18
, interval evaluation over
\mathcal{T}_{B^{\prime}}
runs in

O(|\mathcal{T}_{B^{\prime}}|)\leq 2^{O((|B|+|C||D|)^{2})}
time, which again yields a large hidden constant.
However, this part is not a bottleneck with respect to
|w|
, since the algorithm performs interval evaluation over
\mathcal{T}_{B^{\prime}}
only
O(|w|\log|w|)
times in total;
O(|w|)
times in the branching ABCBD problem and
O(|w|\log|w|)
times in the XYYZ problem when computing the set
\mathcal{S}_{Y}
.

We therefore replace this procedure with a data structure that answers each query in

O(\log|w|)
time while having a significantly smaller hidden constant.
We employ a segment tree, which can be viewed as a factorization forest without idempotent nodes, but with height
O(\log|w|)
independent of the monoid size.

Let
N_{B^{\prime}}=(Q_{B},\Sigma,\Delta_{B},\mathcal{E}_{B},q^{0}_{B},F_{B})

be an
\epsilon
-NFA recognizing
\mathbb{L}\left(B^{\prime}\right)
.
Each node of the segment tree corresponding to an interval
[\ell,r)
stores a binary matrix

M\in 2^{Q_{B}\times Q_{B}}
, where
M_{q_{1},q_{2}}=1
if and only if there exists a path from
q_{1}
to
q_{2}
labeled by
w[\ell{\,..\,}r)
.
Matrix multiplication is defined via
(\lor,\land)
-convolution.

For any interval query
[i,j)
, by the standard argument, we can compute a binary matrix

M^{i,j}\in 2^{Q_{B}\times Q_{B}}
satisfying

M^{i,j}_{q_{1},q_{2}}=1
if and only if there exists a path from
q_{1}
to
q_{2}
labeled by
w[i{\,..\,}j)
,
in time
O(|Q_{B}|^{3}\log|w|)
.
Finally, we can decide whether
w[i{\,..\,}j)\in\mathbb{L}\left(B^{\prime}\right)
by checking whether
there exists a state
q_{2}\in F_{B}
such that
M^{i,j}_{q^{0}_{B},q_{2}}=1
.
Thus, we can implement this part in a way that it contributes only
O((|B|+|C||D|)^{3}|w|\log|w|)
to the total time complexity.

C.3
Bound for Theorem
8

Observe that, in the construction in Appendix
B
, the lengths
|A|
,
|B|
,
|C|
,
|D|
are at most
O(|E|^{2})
,
O(|E|)
,
O(|E|^{2})
, and
O(|E|)
, respectively, for the input
R
EWB
E
.
Combining with the discussion in the previous part, the hidden constant of Theorem
8
is
O(2^{|E|^{4}})
.

C.4
Bound for Theorem
9

By a similar argument to the one above,
we obtain a hidden constant of
2^{O((|X|+|Y|+|Z|)^{2})}+2^{O(|X|+|Y|+|Z|)}\cdot\alpha
for our
O(|w|\log|w|)
-time algorithm in Theorem
9
.
The term
2^{O((|X|+|Y|+|Z|)^{2})}
arises from the computation of the set
\mathcal{S}_{X}
,
\mathcal{S}_{Y}
, and
\mathcal{S}_{Z}
,
where each computation requires
O(|w|\log|w|)
interval evaluations on the factorization forest.

2^{O(|X|+|Y|+|Z|)}\cdot\alpha
part is from the computation of the representation of the set
\mathcal{S}
.

We see that if we allow an additional
O(\log|w|)
factor on the dependence on
|w|
, we can reduce the hidden constant to a polynomial.
As discussed above, for interval evaluation, we adopt a segment tree instead of a factorization forest; this works in
O(poly(|X|+|Y|+|Z|)\log|w|)
time.

The remaining task is to determine whether
e\in\mathcal{S}=\mathcal{S}_{X}+(\alpha\cdot\mathcal{S}_{Y})+\mathcal{S}_{Z}
.
Instead of computing the representation of
\mathcal{S}
, we decide whether
e\in\mathcal{S}
directly.
For an
\epsilon
-NFA
N=(Q,\Sigma,\Delta,\mathcal{E},q^{0},F)
and a string
\theta
, let
N(\theta):=(Q,\{\theta\},\Delta(\theta),\emptyset,q^{0},F)
be an NFA over the unary alphabet
\{\theta\}
, where
\Delta(\theta)
is defined by

\{(p,\theta,q)\colon\text{$q$ can be reached from $p$ by reading $\theta$ in $N$}\}.

Let
N_{X}=(Q_{X},\Sigma,\Delta_{X},\mathcal{E}_{X},q^{0}_{X},F_{X})
be the
\epsilon
-NFA for
X
. Define
N_{Y}
and
N_{Z}
similarly.
We define

\mathcal{N}^{\prime}_{X}:=\{N_{X}^{q\to F_{X}}(\theta)\colon\text{$q$ is reachable from $q^{0}_{X}$ by reading $w_{1}$}\}.

Let
N^{\prime}_{Y}
be the NFA obtained from
N_{Y}^{q^{0}_{Y}\to F_{Y}}(\theta)
by subdividing each edge into
\alpha
segments, and
let
N^{\prime}_{Z}:=N_{Z}^{q^{0}_{Z}\to F}(\theta)
, where
F
is the set of states
q
such that
F_{Z}
is reachable from
q
by reading
w_{2}
.

Then, for each
N^{\prime}_{X}\in\mathcal{N}^{\prime}_{X}
, we construct an
\epsilon
-NFA
N_{\mathrm{all}}
by connecting all accepting states of
N^{\prime}_{X}
to the initial state of
N^{\prime}_{Y}
, and all accepting states of
N^{\prime}_{Y}
to the initial state of
N^{\prime}_{Z}
.
The initial state and the accepting states of
N_{\mathrm{all}}
are the initial state of
N^{\prime}_{X}
and the accepting states of
N^{\prime}_{Z}
, respectively.
Now, we can claim that
e
is in
\mathcal{S}
if and only if
N_{\mathrm{all}}
accepts the length-
e
string, or equivalently, there is a walk of length
e+2
from the initial state to an accepting state of
N_{\mathrm{all}}
.
This can be determined in
O(|N_{\mathrm{all}}|^{3}\log e)
time by standard matrix powering.
Thus, the whole algorithm runs in
O(poly(|X|+|Y|+|Z|)|w|\log^{2}|w|)
time.
